Testing Pushdown Systems Thanks: In collaboration with Computing Institute - University of Campinas.
Adilson Luiz Bonifacio, Arnaldo Vieira Moura

Abstract
Testing on reactive systems is a well-known laborious activity on software development due to their asynchronous interaction with the environment. In this setting model based testing has been employed when checking conformance and generating test suites of such systems using labeled transition system as a formalism as well as the classical ioco conformance relation. In this work we turn to a more complex scenario where the target systems have an auxiliary memory, a stack. We then studied a more powerful model, the Visibly Pushdown Labeled Transition System (VPTS), its variant Input/Output VPTS (IOVPTS), its associated model Visibly Pushdown Automaton (VPA), and aspects of conformance testing and test suite generation. This scenario is much more challenge since the base model has a pushdown stack to capture more complex behaviors which commonly found on reactive systems. We then defined a more general conformance relation for pushdown reactive systems such that it prevents any observable implementation behavior that was not already present in the given specification. Further we gave an efficient algorithm to check conformance in this testing scenario and also showed that it runs in worst case asymptotic polynomial time in the size of both the given specification and the implementation that are put under test.



1
Introduction

Reactive systems have become increasingly common among computer systems, whether in a usual technological solution or in a critical application of the industry.
We see everywhere real-world systems being ruled by reactive behaviors where the systems interact with an external environment by receiving input stimuli and producing outputs in response.
Usually the development of such systems requires a precise and an automatic support during the process, specially in the testing activity because high costs in terms of resources and maintenance time can be generated when the test step is inappropriately performed.

Model-based testing is an important approach that has been employed to test critical and reactive systems because it offers guarantees to its correctness and also because properties, such as completeness of test suites, can be formally proven
[
8
,
11
]
.
These aspects have been studied using appropriate formalisms that capture the behavior of reactive systems, where the exchange of input and output stimuli can occur asynchronously.
Prominent among such formalism are Input/Output Labeled Transition Systems (IOLTSs)
[
15
,
16
]
.

Usually, the testing process aims to verify whether an implementation conforms to a given specification, both described using a common chosen formalism
[
14
]
.
This conformance checking process depends on the specification formalism, on the kind of fault model used, and on a conformance relation that is to be verified
[
2
,
15
,
16
]
.
For IOLTS models, the classical Input/Output Conformance (
ioco
) is a well-studied relation
[
17
,
12
,
4
]
.
A more recent work
[
3
]
proposed a more general approach, based on regular languages, to check
ioco
conformance for IOLTS models.

In this work we study aspects of conformance testing and test suite generation for a class of more powerful models, the so called Input/Output Visibly Pushdown Labeled Transition Systems (IOVPTSs), and their associated formalism, Visibly Pushdown Automata (VPAs).
Using an auxiliary pushdown stack, this formalism can capture the behavior of more complex reactive systems.
Accordingly, we use a more general conformance relation, but one still in the same spirit of the classical
ioco
relation, in the sense that it prevents any observable implementation behavior that was not already present in the given specification.
We also develop and prove the correctness of an efficient algorithm to verify conformance in this testing scenario.
Further, we show that our algorithm runs in worst case asymptotic polynomial time in the size of both the given specification and the implementation that are put under test.

We organize this paper as follows.
Section
2
establishes some notations, definitions and preliminary results over VPAs.
Section
3
defines (IO)VPTSs and relates these models to the associated VPAs.
Section
4
defines conformance relation based on IOVPTS models, and shows how to obtain complete test suites, of polynomial complexity, for this class of models.
Section
5
gives an ioco-like conformance relation for IOVPTS models and the notion of test suite completeness, and then provides a polynomial time algorithm to check ioco-like conformance for IOVPTS implementations.
Section
6
offers some concluding remarks.

2
Notation and preliminary results

In this section we establish some notation and define Visibly Pushdown Automata (VPA).
Some preliminary results that will be useful later are also indicated.

2.1
Basic Notation

Let
X
and
Y
be sets. We indicate by
\mathcal{P}(X)=\{Z\,|\,Z\subseteq X\}
the power set of
X
, and
X-Y=\{z\,|\,\text{$z\in X$ and $z\not\in Y$}\}
indicates set difference.
We will let
X_{Y}=X\cup Y
. When
Y=\{y\}
is a singleton we may also write
X_{y}
for
X_{\{y\}}
.
If
X
is a finite set, the size of
X
will be indicated by
|X|
.

An alphabet is any non-empty set of symbols.
Let
A
be an alphabet.
A word over
A
is any finite sequence
\sigma=x_{1}\ldots x_{n}
of symbols in
A
, that is,
n\geq 0
and
x_{i}\in A
, for all
i=1,2,\ldots,n
.
When
n=0
,
\sigma
is the empty sequence, also indicated by
\varepsilon
.
The set of all finite sequences, or words, over
A
is denoted by
A^{\star}
, and the set of all nonempty finite words over
A
is indicated by
A^{+}
.
When we write
x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{n}\in A^{\star}
, it is implicitly assumed that
n\geq 0
and that
x_{i}\in A
,
1\leq i\leq n
, unless explicitly noted otherwise.
The length of a word
\alpha
over
A
is indicated by
|\alpha|
.
Hence,
|\varepsilon|=0
.
Let
\sigma=\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{n}
and
\rho=\rho_{1}\ldots\rho_{m}
be words over
A
. The concatenation of
\sigma
and
\rho
, indicated by
\sigma\rho
, is the word
\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{n}\rho_{1}\ldots\rho_{m}
.
Clearly,
|\sigma\rho|=|\sigma|+|\rho|
.
A language
G
over
A
is any set
G\subseteq A^{\star}
of words over
A
.
Let
G_{1}
,
G_{2}\subseteq A^{\star}
be languages over
A
. Their product, indicated by
G_{1}G_{2}
, is the language

\{\sigma\rho\,|\,\sigma\in G_{1},\rho\in G_{2}\}
.
If
G\subseteq A^{\star}
is a language over
A
, then its complement is the language
\overline{G}=A^{\star}-G
.

We will also need the notion of a morphism between alphabets.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
A
,
B
be alphabets.
A
homomorphism
, or just a
morphism
, from
A
to
B
is any function
h:A\rightarrow B^{\star}
.

A morphism
h:A\rightarrow B^{\star}
can be extended in a natural way to a function

\widehat{h}:A^{\star}\rightarrow B^{\star}
, thus

\widehat{h}(\sigma)=\begin{cases}\varepsilon\quad&\text{if $\sigma=\varepsilon$}\\
h(a)\widehat{h}(\rho)&\text{if $\sigma=a\rho$ with $a\in A$}.\end{cases}

We can further lift
\widehat{h}
to a function
\widetilde{h}:\mathcal{P}(A^{\star})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(B^{\star})
in a natural way, by letting
\widetilde{h}(G)=\bigcup\limits_{\sigma\in G}\widehat{h}(\sigma)
,
for all
G\subseteq A^{\star}
.
In order to avoid cluttering the notation, we may write
h
instead of
\widehat{h}
, or of
\widetilde{h}
, when no confusion can arise.
When
a\in A
, we define the simple morphism
h_{a}:A\rightarrow A-\{a\}
by letting
h_{a}(a)=\varepsilon
, and
h_{a}(x)=x
when
x\neq a
.
Hence,
h_{a}(\sigma)
erases all occurrences of
a
in the word
\sigma
.

2.2
Visibly Pushdown Automata

A Visibly Pushdown Automaton (VPA)
[
1
]
is, basically, a Pushdown Automaton (PDA)
[
9
]
, with a transition relation over an alphabet and a pushdown stack (or just a stack, for short) associated to it.
Thus a VPA can make use of a potentially infinite memory as with PDA models.
Any alphabet
L
is always partitioned into three disjoint subsets
L=L_{c}\cup L_{r}\cup L_{i}
.
Elements in the set
L_{c}
are “call symbols”, or “push symbols”, and specify push actions on the stack.
Elements in
L_{r}
are “return symbols”, or “pop symbols”, and indicate pop actions, and in
L_{i}
we find ‘‘simple symbols’’, that do not change the stack
1
1

1

In
[
1
]
symbols in
L_{i}
are called “internal action symbols”.
In this text we will reserve that denomination for another special symbol as will be apparent later.
.

The next definition is a slight extension of the similar notion appearing in
[
1
]
.
Here, we also allow
\varepsilon
-moves, that is, the VPA can change states without reading any symbol from the input.

Definition 2.2
.

A
Visibly Pushdown Automaton
(VPA)
[
1
]
over a finite input alphabet
A
is a tuple
\mathscr{A}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
, where:

—

A=A_{c}\cup A_{r}\cup A_{i}
and
A_{c}
,
A_{r}
,
A_{i}
are pairwise disjoint;

—

S
is a finite set of
states
;

—

S_{in}\subseteq S
is set of
initial states
;

—

\Gamma
is a finite
stack alphabet
, with
\bot\not\in\Gamma
the
initial stack symbol
;

—

The
transition relation
is
\rho=\rho_{c}\cup\rho_{r}\cup\rho_{i}
, where
\rho_{c}\subseteq S\times A_{c}\times\Gamma\times S
,
\rho_{r}\subseteq S\times A_{r}\times\Gamma_{\bot}\times S
, and

\rho_{i}\subseteq S\times(A_{i}\cup\{\varepsilon\})\times\{\sharp\}\times S
, where
\sharp\not\in\Gamma_{\bot}
is a place-holder symbol;

—

F\subseteq S
is the set of
final states
.

A transition
(p,\varepsilon,\sharp,q)\in\rho_{i}
is called an
\varepsilon
-move
of
\mathscr{A}
.
A
configuration
of
\mathscr{A}
is any triple
(p,\sigma,\alpha)\in S\times A^{\star}\times(\Gamma^{\star}\{\bot\})
, and the set of all configurations of
\mathscr{A}
it is indicated by
\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{A}}
.

A tuple
(p,a,Z,q)\in\rho_{c}
specifies a
push-transition
.
We have a
pop-transition
if
(p,a,Z,q)\in\rho_{r}
, and a
simple-transition
if
(p,a,Z,q)\in\rho_{i}
.
The intended meaning for a push-transition
(p,a,Z,q)\in\rho_{c}
is that
\mathscr{S}
, in state
p
and reading input
a
, changes to state
q
and pushes
Z
in the stack.
A pop-transition
(p,a,Z,q)\in\rho_{r}
makes
\mathscr{S}
, in state
p
and reading
a
, pop
Z
from the stack and change to state
q
.
A simple-transition
(p,a,\sharp,q)\in\rho_{i}
has the intended meaning of reading
a
and changing from state
p
to state
q
, no matter what the topmost stack symbol is.
An
\varepsilon
-transition
(p,\varepsilon,\sharp,q)\in\rho_{i}
indicates that
\mathscr{S}
must move from state
p
to state
q
, while reading no symbol from the input.

We can now define the relation
\underset{}{\mapsto}\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{A}}\times\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{A}}
which captures simple moves of a VPA
\mathscr{A}
.
Note that the model can make pop moves when the stack contains only the initial stack symbol
\bot
.
The semantics of a VPA is the language comprised by all input strings it accepts.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\mathscr{A}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
be a VPA.
For all
\sigma\in A^{\star}
:

1.

If
(p,a,Z,q)\in\rho_{c}
then
(p,a\sigma,\alpha\bot)\underset{}{\mapsto}(q,\sigma,Z\alpha\bot)
;

2.

If
(p,a,Z,q)\in\rho_{r}
then
(p,a\sigma,Z\alpha\bot)\underset{}{\mapsto}(q,\sigma,\alpha\bot)
when
Z\neq\bot
, or
(p,a\sigma,\bot)\underset{}{\mapsto}(q,\sigma,\bot)
when
Z=\bot
;

3.

If
(p,a,\sharp,q)\in\rho_{i}
then
(p,a\sigma,\alpha\bot)\underset{}{\mapsto}(q,\sigma,\alpha\bot)
.

The set
L(\mathscr{A})=\big\{\sigma\in A^{\star}\,|\,(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\beta),\,\,s_{0}\in S_{in},\,\,p\in F\big\}

is the
language accepted
by
\mathscr{A}
.
Two VPAs
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}
are said to be
equivalent
when
L(\mathscr{A})=L(\mathscr{B})
.

We may also write
(p,a\sigma,\alpha)\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}(q,\sigma,\gamma)
if it is important to explicitly mention the VPA
\mathscr{A}
.
It is clear that when
(p,a\sigma,\alpha)\underset{}{\mapsto}(q,\mu,\beta)
then we get
(q,\mu,\beta)\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{A}}
, so that
\mapsto
is a relation on the set
\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{A}}
.
The
n
-th power of the relation
\underset{}{\mapsto}
will be indicated by
\overset{n}{\underset{}{\mapsto}}
for all
n\geq 0
, and its reflexive and transitive closure will be indicated by
\overset{\star}{\underset{}{\mapsto}}
.

A language is said to be a Visibly Pushdown Language (VPL) when it is accepted by a VPA.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
A
be an alphabet and let
G\subseteq A^{\star}
be a language over
A
.
Then
G
is a
visibly pushdown language
if there is a VPA
\mathscr{A}
such that
L(\mathscr{A})=G
.

Given any VPA, we can always construct and equivalent VPA with no
\varepsilon
-moves and the same number of states as the original VPA.

Proposition 2.5
.

For any VPA we can effectively construct an equivalent VPA with no
\varepsilon
-moves and with the same number of states.

Proof.

We first install in the resulting VPA
\mathscr{B}
all transitions of
\mathscr{A}
that are not
\varepsilon
transitions.
Then, the main idea is to find sequences of
\varepsilon
-moves between pairs of states in
\mathscr{A}
. If there is such a sequence of
\varepsilon
-moves from
s
to
r
, and another such sequence from
p
to
q
, then for every transition of
\mathscr{A}
having
r
as the start state and
p
as the target state, we define a similar transition from
s
to
q
in
\mathscr{B}
.
A complete proof can be seen in Appendix
A.1
.
∎

Next we define deterministic VPAs.
Determinism captures the idea that there is at most one computation for a given input string.
Since our notion of a VPA extends the original notion in
[
1
]
, we also have to deal with
\varepsilon
-moves.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
\mathscr{A}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
be a VPA.
We say that
\mathscr{A}
is
deterministic
if
|S_{in}|\leq 1
, and the following conditions hold:

1.

(p,x,Z_{i},q_{i})\in\rho_{c}
with
i=1,2
, implies
Z_{1}=Z_{2}
and
q_{1}=q_{2}
;

2.

(p,x,Z,q_{i})\in\rho_{r}\cup\rho_{i}
with
i=1,2
, implies
q_{1}=q_{2}
;

3.

(p,x,Z,q_{1})\in\rho
with
x\neq\varepsilon
, implies
(p,\varepsilon,\sharp,q_{2})\not\in\rho
for all
q_{2}\in S
.

A language
L
is a
deterministic VPL
if
L=L(\mathscr{A})
for some deterministic VPA
\mathscr{A}
.

It is worth noticing that Definition
2.6
does not prohibit
\varepsilon
-moves in deterministic VPAs.
With deterministic VPAs, however, computations are always unique, as expected.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
\mathscr{A}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
be a deterministic VPA, let
(p,\sigma,\alpha)\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{A}}
, and take
n\geq 0
.
Then
(p,\sigma,\alpha)\overset{n}{\underset{}{\mapsto}}(q_{i},\mu_{i},\gamma_{i})
,
i=1,2
, implies

(q_{1},\mu_{1},\gamma_{1})=(q_{2},\mu_{2},\gamma_{2})
.

Proof.

By induction on
n\geq 0
. When
n=0
the result is immediate.
Take
n>0
.
Using the induction hypothesis we can write
(p,\sigma,\alpha)\overset{n-1}{\underset{}{\mapsto}}(q,\mu,\gamma)\overset{1}{\underset{}{\mapsto}}(q_{i},\mu_{i},\gamma_{i})
, where the last single steps
used the transitions
(q,x_{i},Z_{i},q_{i})\in\rho
,
i=1,2
.

First assume that
x_{1}=\varepsilon
. Then Definition
2.2
gives
(q,x_{1},Z_{1},q_{1})=(q,\varepsilon,\sharp,q_{1})\in\rho_{i}
.
From
(q,\mu,\gamma)\overset{}{\underset{}{\mapsto}}(q_{1},\mu_{1},\gamma_{1})
and Definition
2.3
, we conclude that
\mu_{1}=\mu
and
\gamma=\gamma_{1}
.
Since
\mathscr{A}
is deterministic, Definition
2.6
(3) forces

x_{2}=\varepsilon=x_{1}
, and then Definition
2.2
gives
(q,x_{2},Z_{2},q_{2})=(q,\varepsilon,\sharp,q_{2})\in\rho_{i}
.
So, from
(q,\mu,\gamma)\overset{}{\underset{}{\mapsto}}(q_{2},\mu_{2},\gamma_{2})
we now get
\mu=\mu_{2}
and
\gamma=\gamma_{2}
.
Since
\mathscr{A}
is deterministic, using Definition
2.6
(2) we get
q_{1}=q_{2}
, and then we have
(q_{1},\mu_{1},\gamma_{1})=(q_{2},\mu_{2},\gamma_{2})
.

Likewise when
x_{2}=\varepsilon
.
We can now assume
x_{1}
,
x_{2}\in A
.
From
(q,\mu,\gamma)\overset{}{\underset{}{\mapsto}}(q_{i},\mu_{i},\gamma_{i})
and Definition
2.3
we get
x_{1}\mu_{1}=\mu=x_{2}\mu_{2}
.
Hence,
x_{1}=x_{2}=x
, and
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}=\delta
.
It suffices to verify that
q_{1}=q_{2}
and
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}
.
We have
(q,x\delta,\gamma)\overset{}{\underset{}{\mapsto}}(q_{i},\delta,\gamma_{i})
using the transitions
(q,x,Z_{i},q_{i})\in\rho
.
There are three cases:

Case 1:

x\in A_{c}
.
Since
\mathscr{A}
is deterministic and
(q,x,Z_{i},q_{i})\in\rho_{c}
, Definition
2.6
(1) guarantees that
Z_{1}=Z_{2}
and
q_{1}=q_{2}
.
From
(q,x\delta,\gamma)\overset{}{\underset{}{\mapsto}}(q_{i},\delta,\gamma_{i})
and Definition
2.3
(1) we get

\gamma_{1}=Z_{1}\gamma
and
\gamma_{2}=Z_{2}\gamma
, so that
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}
, and we are done.

Case 2:

x\in A_{i}
.
From Definition
2.2
we get
(q,x_{i},Z_{i},q_{i})=(q,x,\sharp,q_{i})\in\rho
,
i=1,2
.
Then, Definition
2.6
(2) forces
q_{1}=q_{2}
.
Since
(q,x\delta,\gamma)\overset{}{\underset{}{\mapsto}}(q_{i},\delta,\gamma_{i})
Definition
2.3
(3) implies

\gamma_{1}=\gamma=\gamma_{2}
, concluding this case.

Case 3:

x\in A_{r}
.
First, assume
\gamma=\bot
.
Then
(q,x\delta,\bot)\overset{}{\underset{}{\mapsto}}(q_{i},\delta,\gamma_{i})
using the transitions
(q,x,Z_{i},q_{i})\in\rho_{r}
.
Definition
2.3
(2) gives
\gamma_{1}=\bot=\gamma_{2}
and
Z_{1}=\bot=Z_{2}
.
So,
(q,x,\bot,q_{1})
,
(q,x,\bot,q_{2})\in\rho_{r}
, and Definition
2.6
(2) implies
q_{1}=q_{2}
.
Now, assume
\gamma\neq\bot
.
Definition
2.3
(2) now gives
\gamma=Z_{1}\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma=Z_{2}\gamma_{2}
, so that
Z_{1}=Z_{2}
and
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}
.
Once again Definition
2.6
(2) implies
q_{1}=q_{2}
, and we conclude this case.

The proof is complete. ∎

The next result shows that we can remove
\varepsilon
-moves, while still preserving determinism.

Proposition 2.8
.

Given a deterministic VPA
\mathscr{A}
, we can obtain an equivalent deterministic VPA
\mathscr{B}
with no
\varepsilon
-moves and with the same number of states.

Proof.

The argument resembles the proof of Proposition
2.5
,
but now we have to maintain the determinism at every step.
The main strategy is, first, to remove
\varepsilon
-cycles in
\mathscr{A}
, and then proceed to remove any acyclic sequence of
\varepsilon
moves that can still be found in
\mathscr{A}
.
A complete proof is given in Appendix
A.2
.
∎

2.3
The Synchronous Product of VPAs

The product of two VPAs captures their synchronous behavior.
It will be useful when testing conformance between pushdown memory models.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
and
\mathscr{Q}=\langle Q,Q_{in},A,\Delta,\mu,G\rangle
be VPAs with a common alphabet.
Their product is the VPA

\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}=\langle S\times Q,S_{in}\times Q_{in},A,\Gamma\times\Delta,\nu,F\times G\rangle
, where
((s_{1},q_{1}),a,Z,(s_{2},q_{2}))\in\nu
if and only if either:

1.

a\neq\varepsilon
and we have
(s_{1},a,Z_{1},s_{2})\in\rho
and
(q_{1},a,Z_{2},q_{2})\in\mu
with
Z=(Z_{1},Z_{2})
or
Z=Z_{1}=Z_{2}\in\{\bot,\sharp\}
; or

2.

a=\varepsilon
,
Z=\sharp
and we have either
s_{1}=s_{2}
and
(q_{1},\varepsilon,\sharp,q_{2})\in\mu
; or
q_{1}=q_{2}
and
(s_{1},\varepsilon,\sharp,s_{2})\in\rho
.

We first note that the product construction preservers both determinism and the absence of
\varepsilon
-moves when the original VPAs satisfy such conditions.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
and
\mathscr{Q}=\langle Q,Q_{in},A,\Delta,\mu,G\rangle
be VPAs and let
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}
be their product as in Definition
2.9
.
Then the following conditions hold:

1.

If
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
have no
\varepsilon
-moves, then
\mathscr{P}
has no
\varepsilon
-moves;

2.

If
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
are deterministic with no
\varepsilon
-moves, then
\mathscr{P}
is also deterministic with no
\varepsilon
-moves.

Proof.

Write
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}=\langle S\times Q,S_{in}\times Q_{in},A,\Gamma\times\Delta,\nu,F\times G\rangle
.

When
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
have no
\varepsilon
-moves, only item (1) of Definition
2.9
applies, so that
\mathscr{P}
has no
\varepsilon
-moves too.
Hence, assertion (1) holds.

It remains to show that
\mathscr{P}
is deterministic when
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
are also deterministic and have no
\varepsilon
-moves.
We just argued that
\mathscr{P}
has no
\varepsilon
-moves, so that
\mathscr{P}
can not violate condition (3) of Definition
2.6
.
For the sake of contradiction, assume
\mathscr{P}
has transitions

((s,q),a,Z_{i},(p_{i},r_{i}))\in\nu
, where
Z_{i}\in\Gamma\times\Delta\cup\{\bot,\sharp\}
,
i=1,2
, and
a\neq\varepsilon
.
Using Definition
2.9
(1), from
((s,q),a,Z_{1},(p_{1},r_{1}))
we get

\displaystyle(s,a,X_{1},p_{1})\in\rho,\,\,\,(q,a,Y_{1},r_{1})\in\mu

(1)

where
X_{1}\in\Gamma\cup{\{\bot,\sharp\}}
,
Y_{1}\in\Delta\cup{\{\bot,\sharp\}}
.
Likewise, from
((s,q),a,Z_{2},(p_{2},r_{2}))
we get

\displaystyle(s,a,X_{2},p_{2})\in\rho,\,\,\,(q,a,Y_{2},r_{2})\in\mu

(2)

where
X_{2}\in\Gamma\cup{\{\bot,\sharp\}}
,
Y_{2}\in\Delta\cup{\{\bot,\sharp\}}
.

If
a\in A_{c}
, we must have
Z_{1},Z_{2}\in\Gamma\times\Delta
, and Definition
2.9
(1) forces
Z_{i}=(X_{i},Y_{i})
,
i=1,2
.
Since
a\in A_{c}
, the determinism of
\mathscr{S}
, together with Definition
2.6
(1) applied to Eqs. (
1
,
2
) gives
X_{1}=X_{2}
,
p_{1}=p_{2}
.
Likewise, the determinism of
\mathscr{Q}
gives
Y_{1}=Y_{2}
,
r_{1}=r_{2}
.
But then
Z_{1}=(X_{1},Y_{1})=(X_{2},Y_{2})=Z_{2}
and
(p_{1},r_{1})=(p_{2},r_{2})
.
We conclude that when
a\in A_{c}
,
\mathscr{P}
cannot violate condition (1) of Definition
2.6
.

Now let
a\in A_{r}\cup A_{i}
and
Z_{1}=Z_{2}
.
If
Z_{1}=(X,Y)\in\Gamma\times\Delta
, Eq. (
1
) and Definition
2.9
(1) imply
X_{1}=X
and
Y_{1}=Y
.
Since
Z_{2}=Z_{1}
, Eq. (
2
) gives
X_{2}=X
and
Y_{2}=Y
.
Hence,
X_{1}=X_{2}
,
Y_{1}=Y_{2}
.
Now, the determinism of
\mathscr{S}
applied to Eqs. (
1
,
2
), together with Definition
2.6
(2) implies
p_{1}=p_{2}
.
Likewise,
r_{1}=r_{2}
.
Thus,
(p_{1},r_{1})=(p_{2},r_{2})
and we conclude that
\mathscr{P}
does not violate Definition
2.6
(2).
Finally let
Z_{1}\in\{\bot,\sharp\}
.
Because
a\neq\varepsilon
, Definition
2.9
(1) and Eq. (
1
) say that

Z_{1}=X_{1}=Y_{1}
.
Likewise, now with Eq. (
2
) and knowing that
Z_{1}=Z_{2}
, we get and
Z_{1}=Z_{2}=X_{2}=Y_{2}
.
Hence, with
a\in A_{r}\cup A_{i}
, using Definition
2.6
(2) applied to Eqs. (
1
,
2
), the determinism of
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
implies
p_{1}=r_{1}
and
p_{2}=r_{2}
.
Again we have
(p_{1},r_{1})=(p_{2},r_{2})
and we conclude that
\mathscr{P}
can not violate Definition
2.6
in any circumstance.
That is,
\mathscr{P}
is deterministic.
∎

It is not hard to see that when
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
are deterministic and
\varepsilon
-moves are allowed in both of them, then the product
\mathscr{P}
need not be deterministic.
The following result links moves in the product VPA to moves in the original constituent VPAs.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
and
\mathscr{Q}=\langle Q,Q_{in},A,\Delta,\mu,G\rangle
be VPAs over the same input alphabet.
Then, for all
\sigma\in A^{\star}
, and all
i
,
k\geq 0
:

((s,q),\sigma,(X_{1},Y_{1})\ldots(X_{i},Y_{i})\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}((p,r),\varepsilon,(Z_{1},W_{1})\ldots(Z_{k},W_{k})\bot)

if and only if

(s,\sigma,X_{1}\ldots X_{i}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,Z_{1}\ldots Z_{k}\bot)\,\,\text{and}\,\,(q,\sigma,Y_{1}\ldots Y_{i}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,W_{1}\ldots W_{k}\bot).

Proof.

Since movements in VPAs are determined by the input string, except for possible
\varepsilon
-moves, this result is expected.
The argument, going from the product
\mathscr{P}
to the constituent VPAs
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
, uses a simple induction in the number of steps in the run over
\mathscr{P}
.
In the other direction, we have to allow for any of
\mathscr{S}
or
\mathscr{Q}
to make independent
\varepsilon
-moves.
In this case, we induct on the total number of steps that occur in both the runs over
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
.
Appendix
A.3
has the details.
∎

2.4
VPLs and closure properties

We look at some closure properties involving VPLs.
Similar results appeared elsewhere
[
1
]
, but here the VPA models are somewhat more general because they allow for
\varepsilon
-moves,
which can make a difference in the results.
Moreover, it will prove important to investigate if determinism, when present in the participant VPAs, can also be guaranteed for the resulting VPAs.
Further, since later on we will be analyzing the complexity of certain constructions, we also note how the sizes of the resulting models vary
as a function of the size of the given models.

First we report on a simple result on the stack size during runs of VPAs.

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
\mathscr{A}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
and
\mathscr{B}=\langle Q,Q_{in},A,\Delta,\mu,G\rangle
be VPAs over a common alphabet
A
.
Consider starting configurations
(s_{1},\sigma,\alpha_{1}\bot)
and
(q_{1},\sigma,\beta_{1}\bot)
of
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}
, respectively,
and where
|\alpha_{1}|=|\beta_{1}|
.
If
(s_{1},\sigma,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{2},\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot)
and
(q_{1},\sigma,\beta_{1}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(q_{2},\varepsilon,\beta_{2}\bot)
, then we must have
|\alpha_{2}|=|\beta_{2}|
.

Proof.

A simple induction on
n=|\sigma|
.
For details see Appendix
A.4
, at page
A.4
.
∎

Using the product construction, we can show that VPLs are also closed under intersection.

Proposition 2.13
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
and
\mathscr{Q}=\langle Q,Q_{in},A,\Delta,\mu,G\rangle
be VPAs with
n
and
m
states, respectively.
Then
L(\mathscr{S})\cap L(\mathscr{Q})
can be accepted by a VPA
\mathscr{P}
with
mn
states.
Moreover, if
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
are deterministic, then
\mathscr{P}
is also deterministic.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}=\langle S\times Q,S_{in}\times Q_{in},A,\Gamma\times\Delta,\nu,F\times G\rangle

be the product of
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
.
See Definition
2.9
.
It is clear that
\mathscr{P}
has
nm
states.

For the language equivalence, assume that
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{P})
, so that

((s,q),\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{P}}{\mapsto}}((p,r),\varepsilon,\gamma\bot)
with
(s,q)\in S_{in}\times Q_{in}
and

(p,r)\in F\times G
, for some
\gamma\in(\Gamma\times\Delta)^{\star}
.
Using Proposition
2.11
we get
(s,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)

for some
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
.
Since
s\in S_{in}
and
p\in F
, we conclude that
\sigma\in\ L(\mathscr{S})
.
Likewise,
\sigma\in\ L(\mathscr{Q})
, so that
\sigma\in\ L(\mathscr{S})\cap L(\mathscr{Q})
.
For the converse, assume that
s\in S_{in}
,
p\in F
and
(s,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)

for some
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
. Likewise, let
q\in Q_{in}
,
r\in G
and
(q,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)

for some
\beta\in\Delta^{\star}
.
From Proposition
2.12
we get
|\alpha|=|\beta|
.
We can now apply Proposition
2.11
and write

((s,q),\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{P}}{\mapsto}}((p,r),\varepsilon,\gamma\bot)
for some
\gamma\in(\Gamma\times\Delta)^{\star}
.
Since
(p,r)\in F\times G
we get
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{P})
, and the equivalence holds.

Finally, Proposition
2.10
guarantees that
\mathscr{P}
is deterministic when
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
are deterministic.
∎

Now, we investigate the closure of VPLs under union.
But first, in order to complete the argument for the union, we need to consider VPAs that can always read any string of input symbols when started at any state and with any stack configuration.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
\mathscr{A}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
be a VPA.
We say that
\mathscr{A}
is a
non-blocking
VPA if, for all
s\in S
, all
\sigma\in A^{\star}
and all
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
, there are
p\in S
and
\beta\in\Gamma^{\star}
such that
(s,\sigma,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
.

Any VPA can be easily turned into a non-blocking VPA, with almost no cost in the number of states.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
be a VPA with
n
states.
Then we can construct an equivalent VPA
\mathscr{Q}
with at most
n+1
states and which is also a non-blocking VPA.
Moreover,
\mathscr{Q}
is deterministic if
\mathscr{S}
is deterministic, and
\mathscr{Q}
has no
\varepsilon
-moves if so does
\mathscr{S}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{Q}=\langle S\cup\{p\},S_{in},A,\Gamma,\mu,F\rangle
where
p\not\in S
is a new state.
In order to construct the new transition set
\mu
as an extension of
\rho
, first pick some stack symbol
Z\in\Gamma
.
Then, for all
s\in S
such that there is no
\varepsilon
-transition out of
s
, that is
(s,\varepsilon,\sharp,q)
is not in
\rho
for any
q\in S
, we proceed as follows.
For any input symbol
a\in A
:

1.

a\in A_{i}
: if
(s,a,\sharp,r)\not\in\rho
for all
r\in S
, add the
transition
(s,a,\sharp,p)
to
\mu
;

2.

a\in A_{c}
: if
(s,a,W,r)\not\in\rho
for all
W\in\Gamma
and all
r\in S
, add the
transition
(s,a,Z,p)
to
\mu
;

3.

a\in A_{r}
: if
(s,a,W,r)\not\in\rho
for some
W\in\Gamma_{\bot}
and all
r\in S
, add the
transition
(s,a,W,p)
to
\mu
.

Finally, add to
\mu
the self-loops
(p,a,\sharp,p)
for all
a\in A_{i}
,
(p,a,Z,p)
for all
a\in A_{c}
, and

(p,a,W,p)
for all
a\in A_{r}
and all
W\in\Gamma_{\bot}
.

It is clear now that
\mathscr{S}
has
n+1
states.
Moreover, for all
s\in S
and all
a\in A
the construction readily allows for a move
(s,a,\alpha\bot)\overset{}{\underset{}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
for all
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
.
Hence, an easy induction on
|\sigma|\geq 0
shows that for all
s\in S
and all
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
there will always be a computation
(s,\sigma,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
, for some
r\in S
and some
\beta\in\Gamma^{\star}
.
That is, the modified version is a non-blocking VPA.

The construction adds no
\varepsilon
-moves, so it is clear that
\mathscr{Q}
has no
\varepsilon
-moves when we start with a VPA
\mathscr{S}
that already has no
\varepsilon
-moves.

Assume that
\mathscr{S}
is deterministic.
Since the construction adds no
\varepsilon
-moves, the new VPA
\mathscr{Q}
does not violate condition (3) of Definition
2.6
.
Also, none of the self-loops added at the new state
p
violate any of the conditions of Definition
2.6
.
When
a\in A_{c}
a new transition
(s,a,Z,p)
is only added to
\mathscr{Q}
when there are no transition
(s,a,X,r)
already in
\mathscr{S}
, for any
X\in\Gamma
,
r\in S
.
Hence,
\mathscr{Q}
does not violate condition (1) of Definition
2.6
.
Likewise, we only add
(s,a,\sharp,p)
, or
(s,a,W,p)
, to
\mu
when we find no
(s,a,\sharp,r)
, respectively we find no
(s,a,W,r)
, in
\rho
for any
r
in
S
.
Hence,
\mathscr{Q}
does not violate condition (2) of Definition
2.6
.
We conclude that
\mathscr{Q}
is deterministic when
\mathscr{S}
is already deterministic.

If
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
, with
s_{0}\in S_{in}
,
f\in F
,
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
then we also have
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
, because all transitions in
\rho
are also in
\mu
.
Hence,
L(\mathscr{S})\subseteq L(\mathscr{Q})
.
For the converse, assume
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
, with
s_{0}\in S_{in}
,
f\in F
,
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
.
Note that all new transitions added to
\mu
have the new state
p
as a target state.
Thus, since the new state
p
is not in
S_{in}
nor in
F
, we see that all transitions used in this run over
\mathscr{Q}
are also in
\rho
, and
we then get
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
Thus we also have
L(\mathscr{Q})\subseteq L(\mathscr{S})
, showing that
L(\mathscr{S})=L(\mathscr{Q})
.
∎

Now the closure of VPLs under union is at hand.

Proposition 2.16
.

Let
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
be two VPAs over an alphabet
A
, with
n
and
m
states, respectively.
Then, we can construct a non-blocking VPA
\mathscr{P}
over
A
with at most
(n+1)(m+1)
states and such that
L(\mathscr{P})=L(\mathscr{S})\cup L(\mathscr{Q})
.
Moreover, if
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
are deterministic, then
\mathscr{P}
is also deterministic and has no
\varepsilon
-moves.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
and
\mathscr{Q}=\langle Q,Q_{in},A,\Delta,\mu,G\rangle
.
Using Proposition
2.15
we can assume that
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
are non-blocking VPAs with
n+1
and
m+1
states, respectively.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be the product of
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
as in Definition
2.9
, except that we redefine the final states of
\mathscr{P}
as
(F\times Q)\cup(S\times G)
.
Clearly,
\mathscr{P}
has
(n+1)(m+1)
states.

We now argue that
\mathscr{P}
is also a non-blocking VPA.
Let
\sigma\in A^{\star}
,
(s,q)\in S\times Q
, and let
\gamma=(Z_{1},W_{1})\ldots(Z_{k},W_{k})\in(\Gamma\times\Delta)^{\star}
.
Since
\mathscr{S}
is a non-blocking VPA, we get
n\geq 0
,
p\in S
,
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
such that

(s,\sigma,Z_{1}\ldots Z_{k}\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
Likewise,
(q,\sigma,W_{1}\ldots W_{k}\bot)\overset{m}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
, for some

m\geq 0
,
r\in Q
,
\beta\in\Delta^{\star}
.
Applying Proposition
2.12
we get
|\alpha|=|\beta|
, and then using Proposition
2.11
we have

((s,q),\sigma,\gamma\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{P}}{\mapsto}}((p,r),\varepsilon,\delta\bot)
where
\gamma=(Z_{1},W_{1})\ldots(Z_{k},W_{k})
and
\delta\in(\Gamma\times\Delta)^{\star}
.
This shows that
\mathscr{P}
is a non-blocking VPA.

Now suppose that
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{P})
, that is
((s_{0},q_{0}),\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{P}}{\mapsto}}((p,r),\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
, where
(s_{0},q_{0})\in S_{in}\times Q_{in}
,
(p,r)\in(F\times Q)\cup(S\times G)
, and
\alpha\in(\Gamma\times\Delta)^{\star}
.
Take the case when
(p,r)\in(F\times Q)
.
We get
p\in F
and
s_{0}\in S_{in}
.
Using Proposition
2.11
we can also write
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
, for some
\beta\in\Gamma^{\star}
.
This shows that
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{S})
.
By a similar reasoning, when
(p,r)\in(S\times G)
we get
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{Q})
.
Thus,
L(\mathscr{P})\subseteq L(\mathscr{S})\cup L(\mathscr{Q})
.

Now let
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{S})\cup L(\mathscr{Q})
.
Take the case
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{S})
, the case
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{Q})
being similar.
Then we must have
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
for some
n\geq 0
, some
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
, and some
p\in F
.
Pick any
q_{0}\in Q_{in}
.
Since
\mathscr{Q}
is a non-blocking VPA, Definition
2.14
gives some
r\in Q
and some
\beta\in\Delta^{\star}
such that
(q_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{m}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
for some
m\geq 0
, some
r\in Q
and some
\beta\in\Delta^{\star}
.
Using Proposition
2.12
we conclude that
|\alpha|=|\beta|
.
The only-if part of Proposition
2.11
now yields

((s_{0},q_{0}),\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{P}}{\mapsto}}((p,r),\varepsilon,\gamma\bot)
, where
\gamma\in(\Gamma\times\Delta)^{\star}
.
Clearly,
(s_{0},q_{0})\in S_{in}\times Q_{in}
is an initial state of
\mathscr{P}
and
(p,r)\in(F\times Q)\cup(S\times G)
is a final state of
\mathscr{P}
.
Hence
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{P})
.
Thus,
L(\mathscr{S})\cup L(\mathscr{Q})\subseteq L(\mathscr{P})
, and we then have
L(\mathscr{S})\cup L(\mathscr{Q})=L(\mathscr{P})
.

Applying Proposition
2.10
(2) we see that when
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
are deterministic, then
\mathscr{P}
is also deterministic and has no
\varepsilon
-moves.

The proof is now complete.
∎

We can also show that deterministic VPLs are closed under complementation.
A related result appeared in
[
1
]
, but here we also allow for arbitrary
\varepsilon
-moves in any model.

Proposition 2.17
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
be a deterministic VPA with
n
states. Then, we can construct a non-blocking and deterministic VPA
\mathscr{Q}
over
A
with no
\varepsilon
-moves,
n+1
states, and such that
L(\mathscr{Q})=\overline{L(\mathscr{S})}=\Sigma^{\star}-L(\mathscr{S})
.

Proof.

Applying Propositions
2.8
and
2.15
, we can assume that
\mathscr{S}
is a non-blocking and deterministic VPA with
n+1
states and no
\varepsilon
-moves.

Let
\mathscr{Q}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,S-F\rangle
, that is, we switch the final states of
\mathscr{S}
.
Clearly, since
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
have the same set of initial states and the same transition relation,
we see that
\mathscr{Q}
is also a non-blocking and deterministic VPA with
n+1
states and no
\varepsilon
-moves.

The proof can be concluded with a simple argument to show that
L(\mathscr{Q})=\overline{L(\mathscr{S})}=\Sigma^{\star}-L(\mathscr{S})
.
See Appendix
A.6
, at page
A.6
for details.
∎

The next closure under a simple concatenation will prove useful in the sequel.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
be a VPA with
n
states, and let
B\subseteq A
.
Then we can construct a non-blocking VPA
\mathscr{Q}
with at most
2n+2
states and no
\varepsilon
-moves, and such that
L(\mathscr{Q})=L(\mathscr{S})B
.

Proof.

Using Proposition
2.15
we can assume that
\mathscr{S}
is non-blocking and has no
\varepsilon
-moves.

The main idea is to add new states
\hat{s}
for each state
s
of
\mathscr{S}
, and make these new states the only final states in
\mathscr{Q}
.
Next, we add transitions
(s,b,Z,\hat{r})
to
\mathscr{Q}
, where
s
is a final state in
\mathscr{S}
, and
b
is a symbol in
B
.
Since symbols of
B
can also occur in strings accepted by
\mathscr{S}
, we have to be careful about transitions out of the new states
\hat{s}
.
In particular, if
(t,a,Z,r)
is a transition of
\mathscr{S}
with
a\in B
and
t\in F
we add
(\hat{t},a,Z,\hat{r})
to
\mathscr{Q}
, otherwise we add

(\hat{t},a,Z,r)
to
\mathscr{Q}
.

A detailed construction is given in Appendix
A.7
, at page
A.7
.
∎

3
Reactive Pushdown Models

In this section we start with the Visibly Pushdown Labeled Transition System (VPTS) and state the relationship with its associated VPA.
Next, we discuss the notion of contracted VPTSs and then introduce the variation of Input/Output VPTSs.

3.1
Visibly Pushdown Labeled Transition Systems

A Visibly Pushdown Labeled Transition System (VPTS) extends the classical notion of Labeled Transition System (LTS), a formal model that is convenient to express asynchronous exchange of messages between participating entities, in the sense that outputs do not have to occur synchronously with inputs, but are generated as separated events.
Any LTS has only a finite memory, represented by its set of states.
A VPTS, on the other hand, has a stack memory associated to it, and thus can make use of a potentially infinite memory.

The definition of a VPTS is inspired by the close notion of a Visibly Pushdown Automaton
[
1
]
.
As will be apparent, any VPTS naturally induces a VPA.
In particular, internal transitions in the VPTS will correspond to
\varepsilon
-moves in the associated VPA.

Definition 3.1
.

A
Visibly Pushdown Labeled Transition System
(VPTS) over an input alphabet
L
is a tuple
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
, where:

—

S
is a finite set of
states
or
locations
;

—

S_{in}\subseteq S
is the set of
initial states
;

—

There is a special symbol
\varsigma\notin L
, the
internal action symbol
;

—

\Gamma
is a set of
stack symbols
.
There is a special symbol
\bot\not\in\Gamma
, the
bottom-of-stack symbol
;

—

T=T_{c}\cup T_{r}\cup T_{i}
, where
T_{c}\subseteq S\times L_{c}\times\Gamma\times S
,
T_{r}\subseteq S\times L_{r}\times\Gamma_{\bot}\times S
, and
T_{i}\subseteq S\times(L_{i}\cup\{\varsigma\})\times\{\sharp\}\times S
, where
\sharp\not\in\Gamma_{\bot}
is a place-holder symbol.

Let
t=(p,x,Z,q)\in T
.
If
t\in T_{c}
we say that it is a
push-transition
.
Its intended meaning is that
\mathscr{S}
, in state
p\in S
and reading input
x
, changes to state
q
and pushes
Z
onto the stack.
When
t\in T_{r}
we have a
pop-transition
, with the intended meaning that
\mathscr{S}
, in state
p\in S
and reading input
x\in L_{r}
, pops
Z
from the top of the stack and changes to state
q
.
Further, when the stack is reduced to the bottom of stack symbol,
\bot
, then a pop move can be taken, leaving the stack unchanged.
We have a
simple-transition
when
t\in T_{i}
and
x\in L_{i}
, and we have an
internal-transition
when
t\in T_{i}
and
x=\varsigma
.
The meaning of a simple-transition
t
is to change from state
p
to state
q
, while reading
x
from the input and leaving the stack unchanged. An internal-transition also changes from state
p
to state
q
leaving the stack unchanged, but does not read any symbols from the input.

In order to make these notions precise, we define the set of configurations and the elementary moves of a VPTS.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
be a VPTS.
A
configuration
of
\mathscr{S}
is a pair
(p,\alpha)\in S\times(\Gamma^{\star}\{\bot\})
. When
p\in S_{in}
and
\alpha=\bot
, we say that
(p,\alpha)
is an
initial configuration
of
\mathscr{S}
.
The set of all configurations of
\mathscr{S}
is indicated by
\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{S}}
.
Let
(q,\alpha)\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{S}}
, and let
\ell\in L_{\varsigma}
.
Then we write
(p,\alpha)\overset{\ell}{\rightarrow}(q,\beta)
if there is a transition
(p,\ell,Z,q)\in T
, and either:

1.

\ell\in L_{c}
, and
\beta=Z\alpha
;

2.

\ell\in L_{r}
, and either (i)
Z\neq\bot
and
\alpha=Z\beta
, or (ii)
Z=\alpha=\beta=\bot
;

3.

\ell\in L_{i}\cup\{\varsigma\}
and
\alpha=\beta
.

We call
(p,\alpha)\overset{\ell}{\rightarrow}(q,\beta)
an
elementary move
of
\mathscr{S}
, and we say that
(p,\ell,Z,q)\in T
is the transition
used
in the move
(p,\alpha)\overset{\ell}{\rightarrow}(q,\beta)
.

It is clear from the definition that after any elementary move
(p,\alpha)\overset{\ell}{\rightarrow}(q,\beta)
we have
(q,\beta)\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{S}}
, that is,
(q,\beta)
is also a configuration of
\mathscr{S}
.
Moreover, it also clear that we always have
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}\bot
and
\beta=\beta^{\prime}\bot
with
\alpha^{\prime}
,
\beta^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\star}
,

Remark 3.3
.

In figures depicting VPTSs, a push-transition
(s,x,Z,q)
will be graphically represented by
x/Z_{+}
next to the corresponding arc from
s
to
q
.
Similarly, the label
x/Z_{-}
next to an arc from
s
to
q
will indicate a pop-transition
(s,x,Z,q)
.
A simple- or internal-transition over
(s,x,\sharp,q)
will be indicate by the label
x
next to the corresponding arc.

Example 3.4
.

Figure
1
represents a VPTS
\mathscr{S}
where the set of states is
S=\{s_{0},s_{1}\}
,
S_{in}=\{s_{0}\}
.
Also, we have
L_{c}=\{b\}
,
L_{r}=\{c,t\}
, and
L_{i}=\{\}
, and
\Gamma=\{Z\}
.

s_{0}

s_{1}

c/Z_{-}
t/Z_{-}
b/Z_{+}
c/Z_{-},t/Z_{-}
\varsigma

Figure 1:
A VPTS
\mathscr{S}_{1}
, with
L_{c}=\{b\}
,
L_{r}=\{c,t\}
,
L_{i}=\emptyset
.

We have a push-transition
(s_{0},b,Z,s_{0})
, the pop-transitions

(s_{0},c,Z,s_{1})
,
(s_{0},t,Z,s_{1})
,
(s_{1},c,Z,s_{1})
,
(s_{1},t,Z,s_{1})
, and the internal-transition
(s_{1},\varsigma,\sharp,s_{0})
.
\Box

The semantics of a VPTS is given by its traces, or behaviors.
But first we need the notion of paths in VPTS models which are just chains of elementary moves.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
be a VPTS and let
(p,\alpha),(q,\beta)\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{S}}
.

1.

Let
\sigma=l_{1},\ldots,l_{n}
be a word in
L_{\varsigma}^{\star}
. We say that
\sigma
is a
path
from
(p,\alpha)
to
(q,\beta)
if there are configurations
(r_{i},\alpha_{i})\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{S}}
,
0\leq i\leq n
, such that
(r_{i-1},\alpha_{i-1})\overset{l_{i}}{\rightarrow}(r_{i},\alpha_{i})
,
1\leq i\leq n
, with
(r_{0},\alpha_{0})=(p,\alpha)
and
(r_{n},\alpha_{n})=(q,\beta)
.

2.

Let
\sigma\in L^{\star}
. We say that
\sigma
is an
observable path
from
(p,\alpha)
to
(q,\beta)
in
\mathscr{S}
if
there is a path
\mu
from
(p,\alpha)
to
(q,\beta)
in
\mathscr{S}
such that
\sigma=h_{\varsigma}(\mu)
.

In both cases we also say that the path starts at
(p,\alpha)
and ends at
(q,\beta)
, and we say that the configuration
(q,\beta)
is
reachable
from
(p,\alpha)
.
We also say that
(q,\beta)
is
reachable in

\mathscr{S}
if it is reachable from an initial configuration of
\mathscr{S}
.

Clearly, moves labeled by the internal symbol
\varsigma
can occur in a path.
An observable path is just a path from which
\varsigma
-labels were removed.
If
\sigma
is a path from
(p,\alpha)
to
(q,\beta)
, this can also be indicated by writing
(p,\alpha)\overset{\sigma}{\rightarrow}(q,\beta)
.
When
|\sigma|=1
this has exactly the same meaning as indicated in Definition
3.2
, so that no confusion can arise with this notation.
We may also write
(p,\alpha)\overset{\sigma}{\rightarrow}
to indicate that there is some
(q,\beta)\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{S}}
such that
(p,\alpha)\overset{\sigma}{\rightarrow}(q,\beta)
; likewise,
(p,\alpha)\overset{}{\rightarrow}(q,\beta)
means that there is some
\sigma\in L_{\varsigma}^{*}
such that
(p,\alpha)\overset{\sigma}{\rightarrow}(q,\beta)
.
Also
(p,\alpha)\overset{}{\rightarrow}
means
(p,\alpha)\overset{\sigma}{\rightarrow}(q,\beta)
for some
(q,\beta)\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{S}}
and some
\sigma\in L_{\varsigma}^{*}
.
When
\sigma
is an observable path from
(p,\alpha)
to
(q,\beta)
we may write
(p,\alpha)\overset{\sigma}{\Rightarrow}(q,\beta)
, with similar
shorthand notation also carrying over to the
\overset{}{\Rightarrow}
relation.
When we want to emphasize that the underlying VPTS is
\mathscr{S}
, we write
(p,\alpha)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(q,\beta)
, or
(p,\alpha)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\Rightarrow}}(q,\beta)
.

Paths starting at a given configuration
(p,\alpha)
are also called the traces of
(p,\alpha)
, or the traces starting at
(p,\alpha)
.
The semantics of a VPTS is related to traces starting at an initial configuration.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
be a VPTS and let
(p,\alpha)\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{S}}
.

1.

The set of
traces
of
(p,\alpha)
is
tr(p,\alpha)=\{\sigma\,|\,(p,\alpha)\overset{\sigma\,\,}{\rightarrow}\}
.
The set of
observable traces
of
(p,\alpha)
is
otr(p,\alpha)=\{\sigma\,|\,(p,\alpha)\overset{\sigma\,\,}{\Rightarrow}\}
.

2.

The
semantics
of
\mathscr{S}
is
\bigcup\limits_{q\in S_{in}}\!\!\!\!tr(q,\bot)
, and the
observable semantics
of
\mathscr{S}
is

\bigcup\limits_{q\in S_{in}}\!\!\!\!otr(q,\bot)
.

We will also indicate the semantics and, respectively, the observable semantics, of
\mathscr{S}
by

tr(\mathscr{S})
and
otr(\mathscr{S})
.

In any VPTS
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
, if
(s,\alpha)\overset{}{\Rightarrow}(p,\beta)
then we also have
(s,\alpha)\overset{}{\rightarrow}(p,\beta)
in
\mathscr{S}
, for all
(s,\alpha)
,
(p,\beta)\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{S}}
.
Moreover,
otr(\mathscr{S})=h_{\varsigma}(tr(\mathscr{S}))
.
When
\mathscr{S}
has no internal transitions we also have
otr(\mathscr{S})=tr(\mathscr{S})
.

We can also restrict the syntactic description of VPTS models somewhat, without loosing any descriptive capability, by removing states that are not reachable from any initial state, since these states
will play no role when considering any system behaviors.
Moreover, we can also eliminate
\varsigma
-labeled self-loops.
We formalize these observations in the following remark.

Remark 3.7
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
be a VPTS.
For any
s\in S
we postulate that
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\rightarrow}(s,\alpha\bot)
, for some
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
,

\sigma\in L_{\varsigma}^{\star}
, and
s_{0}\in S_{in}
.
Also, if
(s,\varsigma,\sharp,q)\in T
then
s\neq q
.

The intended meaning for
\varsigma
-moves in VPTSs is similar to that given for LTS models
[
3
]
, that is,
a VPTS can autonomously move along
\varsigma
-transitions, without consuming any input symbol.
However, in some situations such moves may not be desirable, or simply we might want no observable behavior leading to two distinct states.
This motivates the notion of determinism in VPTS models.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
be a VPTS.
We say that
\mathscr{S}
is
deterministic
if, for all
s
,
p\in S_{in}
,
s_{1}
,
s_{2}\in S
,
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\Gamma^{\star}
, and
\sigma\in L^{\star}
,
we have that
(s,\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\Rightarrow}}(s_{1},\beta_{1}\bot)
and
(p,\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\Rightarrow}}(s_{2},\beta_{2}\bot)
imply
s_{1}=s_{2}
and
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}
.

As a consequence, deterministic VPTSs do not have internal moves.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
be a deterministic VPTS.
Then
\mathscr{S}
has no
\varsigma
-labeled transitions.

Proof.

By contradiction, assume that
(s,\varsigma,\sharp,q)\in T
.
From Remark
3.7
we get
s\neq q
and we also get
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
,
\sigma\in\ L^{\star}
such that
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\rightarrow}(s,\alpha\bot)
, with
s_{0}\in S_{in}
.
Hence,
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\rightarrow}(s,\alpha\bot)\overset{\varsigma}{\rightarrow}(q,\alpha\bot)
.
Using Definition
3.6
we get
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\Rightarrow}(s,\alpha\bot)
and
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\Rightarrow}(q,\alpha\bot)
, where
\mu=h_{\varsigma}(\sigma)
.
Since
s\neq q
, this contradicts Definition
3.8
.
∎

3.2
Contracted VPTSs

It will also be useful, later on, to count on a guarantee that every transition in a VPTS can be exercised by some trace of the model.
Since every transition, except possibly for pop transitions, can always be taken, we concentrate on the pop transitions.

Definition 3.10
.

We say that a VPTS
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
is
contracted
if for every transition
(p,b,Z,r)\in T
with
b\in L_{r}
, there are some
s_{0}\in S_{in}
,
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
and
\sigma\in L^{\star}
such that
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\Rightarrow}(p,\alpha\bot)
, where either (i)
\alpha=Z\beta
for some
\beta\in\Gamma^{\star}
, or (ii)
\alpha=\varepsilon
and
Z=\bot
.

We can obtain contracted VPTSs using the next Proposition
3.11
.
The idea is to construct a context free grammar (CFG) based on the given VPTS, in such a way that the CFG generates strings where terminals represent VPTS transitions.
The productions of the CFG will indicate the set of transitions that can be effectively used in a trace over the VPTS.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
be a VPTS.
We can effectively construct a contracted VPTS
\mathscr{Q}=\langle Q,Q_{in},L,\Gamma,R\rangle
with
|Q|\leq|S|
,
and such that
tr(\mathscr{S})=tr(\mathscr{Q})
.Moreover, if
\mathscr{S}
is deterministic, then
\mathscr{Q}
is also deterministic.

Proof.

First we construct a context-free grammar
G
whose terminals represent transitions of
\mathscr{S}
.
Non-terminals are of the form
[s,Z,p]
where
s,p\in S
are states of
\mathscr{S}
and
Z\in\Gamma_{\bot}
is a stack symbol.
The main idea can be grasped as follows.
Let
t_{i}=[s_{i},a_{i},Z_{i},p_{i}]
,
1\leq i\leq n
be transitions of
\mathscr{S}
and let
\sigma=a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}
be an input string.
If
G
has a leftmost derivation

[s_{0},\bot,-]\overset{\star}{\underset{}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}\cdots t_{n}[r_{1},W_{1},r_{2}][r_{2},W_{2},r_{3}]\cdots[r_{m},W_{m},r_{m+1}][r_{m+1},\bot,-]

it must be the case that
\mathscr{S}
, starting at the initial configuration
(s_{0},\bot)
, can move along the transitions
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
., in that order, to reach the configuration
(r_{1},W_{1}W_{2}\cdots W_{m}\bot)
.
That is,
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\rightarrow}(r_{1},W_{1}W_{2}\cdots W_{m}\bot)
, where
\sigma=a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}
.
And vice-versa.
We then show that leftmost derivations of
G
faithfully simulate traces of
\mathscr{S}
and, conversely, that any trace of
\mathscr{S}
can be simulated by a leftmost derivation of
G
.
That done, we can easily extract from
G
a contracted VPTS
\mathscr{Q}
.
A simple argument then proves that
L(\mathscr{Q})=tr(\mathscr{S})
.

The complete construction and detailed proofs can be found in Appendix
A.8
. ∎

3.3
Relating VPTS and VPA models

Now we show that any VPTS
\mathscr{S}
gives rise to an associated VPA
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{A}}
in a natural way.
We convert any
\varsigma
-transition of
\mathscr{S}
into a
\varepsilon
-transition of
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{A}}
.
The set of final states of
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{A}}
is just the set of all locations of
\mathscr{S}
.
Conversely, we can associate a VPA to any given VPTS, provided that all states in the given VPA are final states.

Definition 3.12
.

We have the following two associations:

1.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
be a VPTS.
The VPA
induced
by
\mathscr{S}
is
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{S}}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,\rho,S\rangle
where, for all
p
,
q\in S
,
Z\in\Gamma
,
\ell\in L
, we have:

(a)

(p,\ell,Z,q)\in\rho
if and only if
(p,\ell,Z,q)\in T
;

(b)

(p,\varepsilon,\sharp,q)\in\rho
if and only if
(p,\varsigma,\sharp,q)\in T
.

2.

Let
\mathscr{A}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,\rho,S\rangle
be a VPA.
The VPTS
induced
by
\mathscr{A}
is
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{A}}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
where:

(a)

(p,\ell,Z,q)\in T
if and only if
(p,\ell,Z,q)\in\rho
;

(b)

(p,\varsigma,\sharp,q)\in T
if and only if
(p,\varepsilon,\sharp,q)\in\rho
.

The relationship between the associated models is given by the following result.

Proposition 3.13
.

\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
be a VPTS and
\mathscr{A}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,\rho,S\rangle
a VPA.
Assume that either
\mathscr{A}
is the VPA induced
\mathscr{S}
, or
\mathscr{S}
is the VPTS induced by
\mathscr{A}
.
Then, the following are equivalent, where
\sigma\in L^{\star}
,
\mu\in(L_{\varsigma})^{\star}
,
s,p\in S
,
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma^{\star}
,
n\geq 0
:

1.

(s,\alpha\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\Rightarrow}}(p,\beta\bot)

2.

(s,\alpha\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(p,\beta\bot)
,
h_{\varsigma}(\mu)=\sigma

3.

(s,\sigma,\alpha\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
,
n=|\mu|

Proof.

From Definition
3.5
we see that (1) and (2) are equivalent, for any VPTS
\mathscr{S}
.

If
\mathscr{A}
is the VPA induced by
\mathscr{S}
then, using Definition
3.12
(1), an easy induction on
|\mu|\geq 0
shows that if (2) holds then (3) also holds with
n=|\mu|
.
Likewise, an easy induction on
n\geq 0
shows that if (3) holds, then we get some
\mu
satisfying (2) and with
|\mu|=n
. If
\mathscr{S}
is the VPTS induced by the VPA
\mathscr{A}
then the reasoning is very similar, now using Definition
3.12
(2).
∎

The observable semantics of
\mathcal{S}
is just the language accepted by
A_{\mathcal{S}}
.
We note this as the next proposition.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
\mathscr{S}
be a VPTS and
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{S}}
the VPA induced by
\mathscr{S}
.
Then
otr(\mathscr{S})=L(\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{S}})
and, further, if
\mathscr{S}
is deterministic and contracted
then
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{S}}
is also deterministic.
Conversely, let
\mathscr{A}
be a VPA and
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{A}}
the VPTS induced by
\mathscr{A}
.
Then
L(\mathscr{A})=otr(\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{A}})

and, also,
if
\mathscr{A}
is deterministic and has no
\varepsilon
-moves, then
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{A}}
is deterministic.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
and
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{S}}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,\rho,S\rangle
.
Then,
otr(\mathscr{S})=L(\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{S}})
follows directly from Proposition
3.13
.

Now assume that
\mathscr{S}
is deterministic and contracted.
We show that
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{S}}
satisfies Definition
2.6
.
First, take
p
,
q\in S_{in}
.
Then, we have
(p,\bot)\overset{\varepsilon}{\Rightarrow}(p,\bot)
and
(q,\bot)\overset{\varepsilon}{\Rightarrow}(q,\bot)
in
\mathscr{S}
.
Since
\mathscr{S}
is deterministic, Definition
3.8
gives
p=q
.
This shows that
|S_{in}|\leq 1
, as required by Definition
2.6
.

Next, we look at the three assertions at Definition
2.6
.
First, let
(p,\ell,Z_{i},q_{i})\in\rho_{c}
,
i=1,2
.
Since
\ell\in L_{c}
, Definition
3.12
gives
(p,\ell,Z_{i},q_{i})\in T
for
i=1,2
. From Remark
3.7
we obtain some
\sigma\in L^{\star}
and
s_{0}\in S_{in}
such that
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\Rightarrow}}(p,\alpha\bot)
for some
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
.
Hence,

(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma\ell}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\Rightarrow}}(q_{i},Z_{i}\alpha\bot)
for
i=1,2
.
Since
\mathscr{S}
is deterministic, Definition
3.8
gives
q_{1}=q_{2}
and
Z_{1}=Z_{2}
.
We conclude that
A_{\mathscr{S}}
satisfies condition 1 at Definition
2.6
. Now suppose we have
(p,\ell,Z,q_{i})\in\rho_{r}\cup\rho_{i}
,
i=1,2
.
Since
\ell\in L_{r}\cup L_{i}
, Definition
3.12
gives
(p,\ell,Z,q_{i})\in T
,
i=1,2
.
When
\ell\in L_{i}
we get
Z=\sharp
, and proceed exactly as in the first case.
When
\ell\in L_{r}
, since
\mathscr{S}
is contracted, Proposition
3.11
gives
s_{0}\in S_{in}
,
\sigma\in L^{\star}
and
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
such that
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\Rightarrow}}(p,\alpha\bot)
.
Further,
\alpha=Z\beta
when
Z\neq\bot
, or
\alpha=\varepsilon
when
Z=\bot
.
In the first case,
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma\ell}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\Rightarrow}}(q_{i},\beta\bot)
and, in the second case,
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma\ell}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\Rightarrow}}(q_{i},\bot)
,
i=1,2
.
Thus, because
\mathscr{S}
is deterministic, Definition
3.8
forces
gives
q_{1}=q_{2}
.
We conclude that
A_{\mathscr{S}}
satisfies condition 2 at Definition
2.6
.
Finally suppose we have
(p,x,Z,q_{1})\in\rho
and
(p,\varepsilon,Z,q_{2})\in\rho
with
x\neq\varepsilon
.
Then, Definition
3.12
gives
(p,\varsigma,\sharp,q_{2})\in T

and
(p,x,Z,q_{1})\in T
.
Since
\mathscr{S}
is deterministic, Proposition
3.9
says that it has no
\varsigma
-transitions, and we reached a contradiction.
Hence,
A_{\mathscr{S}}
satisfies condition 3 at Definition
2.6
, and the proof is complete.

For the converse, let
\mathscr{A}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,\rho,S\rangle
and
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{A}}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
.
Again,
otr(\mathscr{A})=otr(\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{A}})
is immediate from Proposition
3.13
.
Now assume that
\mathscr{A}
is deterministic and has no
\varepsilon
-moves.
Clearly, from the construction, we know that
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{A}}
has no
\varsigma
-moves.
Let
(s,\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{A}}}{\Rightarrow}}(s_{1},\beta_{1}\bot)
and

(p,\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{A}}}{\Rightarrow}}(s_{2},\beta_{2}\bot)
with
p,s\in S_{in}
and
\sigma\in L^{\star}
.
Since
\mathscr{A}
is deterministic, we get
|S_{in}|\leq 1
, so that
p=s
.
Using Definition
3.5
, and since
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{A}}
has no
\varsigma
-moves, we get

(s,\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{A}}}{\rightarrow}}(s_{1},\beta_{1}\bot)
and

(s,\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{A}}}{\rightarrow}}(s_{2},\beta_{2}\bot)
.
From Proposition
3.13
we get

(s,\sigma,\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{1},\varepsilon,\beta_{1}\bot)
and

(s,\sigma,\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{2},\varepsilon,\beta_{2}\bot)
, where
n=|\sigma|
.
Now, since
\mathscr{A}
is deterministic, we can use Proposition
2.7
and conclude that
s_{1}=s_{2}
and
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}
.
This shows that
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{A}}
satisfies Definition
3.8
, completing the proof.
∎

Lemma
3.14
also says that
otr(\mathscr{S})
is a visibly pushdown language, and that
for any given VPTS
\mathscr{S}
, we can easily construct a VPA
\mathscr{A}
with
L(\mathscr{A})=otr(\mathscr{S})
.

3.4
Input Output Pushdown Transition Systems

The VPTS formalism can be used to model systems with a potentially infinite memory and with a capacity to interact asynchronously with an external environment.
In such situations, we may want to treat some action labels as symbols that the VPTS “receives” from the environment, and some other action labels as symbols that the VPTS “sends back” to the environment.
The next VPTS variation differentiates between input action symbols and output action symbols.

Definition 3.15
.

An Input/Output Visibly Pushdown Transition System (IOVPTS) over an alphabet
L
is a tuple
\mathscr{I}=\langle S,S_{in},L_{I},L_{U},\Gamma,T\rangle
, where

•

L_{I}
is a finite set of
input actions
, or
input labels
;

•

L_{U}
is a finite set of
output actions
, or
output labels
;

•

L_{I}\cap L_{U}=\emptyset
, and
L=L_{I}\cup L_{U}
is the set of
actions
or
labels
; and

•

\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
is an
underlying VPTS
over
L
, which is associated to
\mathscr{I}
.

We
denote the class of all IOVPTSs with input alphabet
L_{I}
and output alphabet
L_{U}
by
\mathscr{IOVP}(L_{I},L_{U})
.

Remark 3.16
.

In order to keep the number of definitions under control, we agree that in any reference to a notion based on IOVPTSs, and that has not been explicitly defined at some point, we substitute the IOVPTS model by its underlying VPTS.
As a case in point, if
\mathscr{S}
is any IOVPTS with
\mathscr{V}
as its underlying VPTS, then the VPA induced by
\mathscr{S}
is simply the VPA induced by
\mathscr{V}
, according to Definition
3.12
.
Likewise for any formal assertion involving IOVPTS models.
For example, using Proposition
3.14
we can just say that
otr(\mathscr{S})=L(\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{S}})
without any explicit mention to the underlying VPTS
\mathscr{V}
.

The semantics of an IOVPTS is just the set of its observable traces, that is, observable traces of its underlying VPTS.

Definition 3.17
.

Let
\mathscr{I}=\langle S,S_{in},L_{I},L_{U},\Gamma,T\rangle
be an IOVPTS.
The
semantics
of
\mathscr{I}
is the set
otr(\mathscr{I})=otr(\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{I}})
,
where
\mathscr{S}_{\mathscr{I}}
is the underlying VPTS associated to
\mathscr{I}
.

Also, when referring an IOVPTS
\mathscr{I}
, the notation
\underset{\mathscr{I}}{\rightarrow}
and
\underset{\mathscr{I}}{\Rightarrow}
are to be understood as
\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}
and
\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\Rightarrow}
, respectively, where
\mathscr{S}
is the underlying VPTS associated to
\mathscr{I}
.

Example 3.18
.

Figure
2
represents an IOVPTS that describes a machine that dispenses drinks.
Here we have
L_{I}=\{b\}
and
L_{U}=\{c,t\}
. From the context we can see that
L_{c}=\{b\}
,
L_{r}=\{c,t\}
and
L_{i}=\emptyset
.

s_{0}

s_{1}

c/Z_{-}
t/Z_{-}
b/Z_{+}
c/Z_{-},t/Z_{-}
\varsigma

Figure 2:
An IOVPTS with
L_{I}=\{b\}
e
L_{U}=\{c,t\}
.

The start state is
s_{0}
.
Symbol
b
stands for button an user can press when asking for a drink, namely a cup of coffee or a cup of tea, with corresponding buttons represented by the labels
c
and
t
, respectively.
The user can hit the
b
button while the machine stays at state
s_{0}
.
Each time the
b
button is activated, the machine pushes the symbol
Z
on the stack, so that the stack is used to count how many times the
b
button was hit by the user.

At any instant, after the user has activated the
b
button at least once, the machine moves to state
s_{1}
and starts dispensing either coffee or tea, indicated by the
c
and
t
buttons.
It decrements the stack each time a drink is dispensed, so that it will never deliver more drinks than the user asked for.

A move back to state
s_{0}
, over the internal label
\varsigma
, may interrupt the delivery of drinks, so that the user can, possibly, receive less drinks than originally asked for.
In this case, when the next user will operate the machine with residual number of
Z
symbols in the stack he could, eventually collect more drinks than asked for.
But the machine will never dispense more drinks than the total number of solicitations.
An alternative could be to use one more state
s_{2}
to interrupt the transition from
s_{1}
to
s_{0}
and install a self-loop at
s_{2}
that empties the stack.
A more complex and complete version of a drink dispensing machine is illustrated in Subsection
5.4
.
\Box

We register one more example which will be used later.

Example 3.19
.

Figure
3
depicts another IOVPTS, where
L_{I}=\{a,b\}
,
L_{U}=\{x\}
,
L_{c}=\{a\}
,
L_{r}=\{b,x\}
and
L_{i}=\emptyset
.
Also,
S_{in}=\{s_{0}\}
and
\Gamma=\{A\}
.
\Box

s_{0}

s_{1}

s_{2}

b/A_{-}
b/A_{-}
b/A_{-}

x/A_{-}
a/A_{+}

Figure 3:
An IOVPTS specification
\mathscr{S}
with
L_{I}=\{a,b\}
and
L_{U}=\{x\}
.

4
Conformance Checking and Visibly Pushdown Languages

In this section we define a more general conformance relation based on Visibly Pushdown Languages
[
1
]
, a proper subset of the more general class of context-free languages
[
9
]
, but a proper superset of the regular languages.
Next we study the notion of test suite completeness
and give a polynomial time complexity method to check conformance between an IUT and its specification, both based on VPTS models, and using the more general conformance relation over VPLs.

4.1
A General Conformance Relation for VPTS models

The more general conformance relation is defined on subset of words specified by a tester.
Informally, consider a language
D
, the set of “desirable” behaviors, and a language
F
, the set of “forbidden” behaviors, of a system.
If we have a specification VPTS
\mathscr{S}
and an implementation VPTS
\mathscr{I}
we want to say that
\mathscr{I}

conforms
to
\mathscr{S}
according to
(D,F)
if no undesired behavior in
F
that is observable in
\mathscr{I}
is specified in
\mathscr{S}
, and all desired behaviors in
D
that are observable in
\mathscr{I}
are specified in
\mathscr{S}
.
This leads to the following definition.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
L
be an alphabet, and let
D,F\subseteq L^{\star}
.
Let
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{I}
be VPTSs over
L
.
We say that
\mathscr{I}

(D,F)
-visibly conforms to
\mathscr{S}
, written
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,F}\,\,\mathscr{S}
, if and only if

1.

\sigma\in otr(\mathscr{I})\cap F
, then
\sigma\not\in otr(\mathscr{S})
;

2.

\sigma\in otr(\mathscr{I})\cap D
, then
\sigma\in otr(\mathscr{S})
.

We note an equivalent way of expressing these conditions that may also be useful.
Recall that the complement of
otr(\mathscr{S})
is
\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S})=L^{\star}-otr(\mathscr{S})
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{I}
be VPTSs over
L
and let
D,F\subseteq L^{\star}
.
Then
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,F}\,\,\mathscr{S}
if and only if

otr(\mathscr{I})\cap\big[(D\cap\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S}))\cup(F\cap otr(\mathscr{S}))\big]=\emptyset
.

Proof.

From Definition
4.1
we readily get
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,F}\,\,\mathscr{S}
if and only if
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap F\cap otr(\mathscr{S})=\emptyset
and

otr(\mathscr{I})\cap D\cap\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S})=\emptyset
.
And this holds if and only if

\emptyset=\big[otr(\mathscr{I})\cap F\cap otr(\mathscr{S})\big]\cup\big[otr(\mathscr{I})\cap D\cap\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S})\big]=otr(\mathscr{I})\cap\big[(D\cap\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S}))\cup(F\cap otr(\mathscr{S}))\big],

as desired.
∎

Example 4.3
.

Let
\mathscr{S}
be a specification depicted in Figure
3
.
Take the languages
D=\{a^{n}b^{n}x:n\geq 0\}
and
F=\{a^{n}b^{n+1}:n\geq 0\}
.
This says that any behavior consisting of a block of
a
s followed by an equal length block of
b
s and terminating by an
x
, is a desirable behavior.
Any block of
a
s followed by a lengthier block of
b
s is undesirable.
We want to check whether the implementation
\mathscr{I}
conforms to the specification
\mathscr{S}
with respect
to the sets of behaviors described by
D
and
F
.
That is, we want to check whether
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,F}\,\,\mathscr{S}
.

q_{0}

q_{1}

q_{2}

b/A_{-},x/\bot_{-}

An implementation IOVPTS
\mathscr{I}
with
L_{I}=\{a,b\}
and
L_{U}=\{x\}
.

First, we obtain the VPA
\overline{\mathscr{S}}
depicted in Figure
5
.
Since
\mathscr{S}
is deterministic and all its states are final, we just add a new state
err
to
\overline{\mathscr{S}}
, and for any missing transitions in
\mathscr{S}
we add corresponding transitions ending at
err
in
\overline{\mathscr{S}}
.
It is not hard to see that the language accepted by
\overline{\mathscr{S}}
is
\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S})
.

\bar{s_{0}}

\bar{s_{2}}

\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S})
for the IOVPTS
\mathscr{S}
of Figure

.

Again, it is easy to see from Figure
3
that
a^{n}b^{n+1}\not\in otr(\mathscr{S})
, for all
n\geq 0
.
So,
F\cap otr(\mathscr{S})=\emptyset
.
Also we see that the VPA
\mathscr{D}
, depicted at Figure
6
, accepts the language
D
and that
D\subseteq\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S})
.
Then the VPA
\mathscr{D}
accepts the language
T=D\cap\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S})=(D\cap\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S}))\cup(F\cap otr(\mathscr{S}))
.
