Figure 6:
The VPA
\mathscr{D}
accepting
D=a^{n}b^{n}x
.

Now let
\mathscr{I}
be the implementation depicted in Figure
4
.
A simple inspection also shows that
aabbx
is accepted by
\mathscr{I}
, and we also have
aabbx\in D
.
Hence,
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap D\cap\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S})=ort(\mathscr{I})\cap T\neq\emptyset
, and Proposition
4.2
implies that
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,F}\,\,\mathscr{S}
does not hold.

On the other hand, if we assume an implementation
\mathscr{I}
that is isomorphic to
\mathscr{S}
,
\mathscr{I}
would not have the transition
q_{2}\overset{x/\bot}{\rightarrow}q_{1}
and then
aabbx
would not be an observable behavior of
\mathscr{I}
.
Actually, in this case,
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap D\cap\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S})=\emptyset
.
So that now

otr(\mathscr{I})\cap\big[(D\cap\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S}))\cup(F\cap otr(\mathscr{S}))\big]=\emptyset
,
and therefore
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,F}\,\,\mathscr{S}
, as expected.

\Box

Depending on languages
D
and
F
, we can test conformance involving several distinct classes of behaviors:

1.

All observable behaviors of
\mathscr{I}
are of interest, and we are not concerned with any undesirable behaviors of
\mathscr{I}
.
Then, let
D\subseteq L^{\star}
and
F=\emptyset
.
We get
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,F}\,\,\mathscr{S}
if and only if
otr(\mathscr{I})\subseteq otr(\mathscr{S})
.

2.

Let
\mathscr{I}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
and let
C\subseteq S
be a subset of the locations of
\mathscr{I}
.
Behaviors of interest are all observable traces
\sigma
of
\mathscr{I}
that take the initial configuration
(s_{0},\bot)
of
\mathscr{I}
to a configuration
(q,\alpha)

and where
q\in C
.
Also, let
E
be another set of locations of
\mathscr{I}
with
E\cap C=\emptyset
, and the undesired behaviors of
\mathscr{I}
are observable traces
\sigma
that lead to a configuration whose location is in
E
.
Then,
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{C,E}\,\,\mathscr{S}
if and only if undesired observable traces of
\mathscr{I}
are not observable in
\mathscr{S}
and all desirable observable traces of
\mathscr{I}
are also observable in
\mathscr{S}
.

3.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
be a specification and let
\mathscr{I}=\langle Q,Q_{in},L,\Delta,R\rangle
be an implementation. Let
H\subseteq L
be a subset of
L
.
The desirable behaviors of
\mathscr{I}
are those observable traces that end in a label in
H
, and we are not interested in undesirable traces of
\mathscr{I}
.
In this case, choose
F=\emptyset
and
D=L^{\star}H
.

Note that
D
and
F
are VPLs in all cases listed above.

4.2
Test Suite Completeness over VPLs

We first state the general definition of a test suite.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
L
be a set of symbols.
A test suite
T
over
L
is a language over
L
,
i.e.

T\subseteq L^{\star}
.
Each
\sigma\in T
is called a
test case
.

A test suite
T
should be engineered to detect faulty observable behavior of any given IUT, when compared to what has been determined by a specification.
In this case,
T
can be seen as specifying a fault model, in the sense that test cases in
T
represent faulty observable behaviors.
In particular, if
T
is a VPL, then it can be specified by a VPA
\mathscr{A}
.
Alternatively, we could specify
T
by a finite set of VPAs, so that
the union of all the undesirable behaviors specified by these VPAs comprise the fault model.

Next we say that an implementation
\mathscr{I}
satisfies, or adheres, to a test suite
T
when
no observable behavior of
\mathscr{I}
is a harmful behavior present in
T
.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
T
be a test suite over an alphabet
L
.
A VPTS
\mathscr{Q}
over
L

adheres
to
T
if
\sigma\not\in T
for all
\sigma\in otr(\mathscr{Q})
.
Further, an IOVPTS
\mathscr{I}
over
L
adheres to
T
if its underlying VPTS adheres to
T
.

In general, we want test suites to be sound, in the sense that adherence always implies visual conformance.
Moreover, the converse is also desirable, that is, when we have visual conformance, then we also have adherence.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
L
be an alphabet and let
T
be a test suite over
L
.
Let
\mathscr{S}
be a VPTS over
L
, and let
D,F\subseteq L^{\star}
be languages over
L
.
We say that:

1.

T
is
sound
for
\mathscr{S}
and
(D,F)
if

\mathscr{I}
adheres to
T
implies
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,F}\,\,\mathscr{S}
, for all VPTS
\mathscr{I}
over
L
.

2.

T
is
exhaustive
for
\mathscr{S}
and
(D,F)
if
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,F}\,\,\mathscr{S}
implies that
\mathscr{I}
adheres to
T
, for all VPTS
\mathscr{I}
over
L
.

3.

T
is
complete
for
\mathscr{S}
and
(D,F)
if it is both sound and exhaustive
for
\mathscr{S}
and
(D,F)
.

Note that, for convenience, adherence is defined in the negative, that is,
\mathscr{I}
adheres to
T
if we have
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap T=\emptyset
.
Of course we could reverse the notions of soundness and exhaustiveness by substituting
L^{\star}-T
for
T
throughout.

It comes as no surprise that the test suite we can extract from Proposition
4.2
is always complete.
But, furthermore, we will also show that it is unique.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\mathscr{S}
be a specification VPTS over
L
, and let
D
,
F\subseteq L^{\star}
be a pair of languages over
L
.
Then, the set

\big[(D\cap\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S}))\cup(F\cap otr(\mathscr{S}))\big]
is the only complete test suite for
\mathscr{S}
and
(D,F)
.

Proof.

Write
T=\big[(D\cap\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S}))\cup(F\cap otr(\mathscr{S}))\big]
,
and let
\mathscr{I}
be any implementation VPTS over
L
.
From Definition
4.5
, we know that
\mathscr{I}
adheres to
T
if and only if
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap T=\emptyset
.
From Proposition
4.2
we get that
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,F}\,\,\mathscr{S}
if and only if
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap T=\emptyset
.
Hence,
\mathscr{I}
adheres to
T
if and only if
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,F}\,\,\mathscr{S}
.
Since
\mathscr{I}
was arbitrary, from Definition
4.6
we conclude that
T
is a complete test suite for
\mathscr{S}
and
(D,F)
.

Now, take another test suite
Z\subseteq L^{\star}
, with
Z\neq T
.
For the sake of contradiction, assume that
Z
is also complete for
\mathscr{S}
and
(D,F)
.
Fix any implementation
\mathscr{I}
.
Since
Z
is complete, Definition
4.6
says that

\mathscr{I}
adheres to
Z
if and only if
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,F}\,\,\mathscr{S}
.
Using Proposition
4.2
we know that
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,F}\,\,\mathscr{S}
if and only if
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap T=\emptyset
.
Hence,
\mathscr{I}
adheres to
Z
if and only if
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap T=\emptyset
.
From Definition
4.5
we know that
\mathscr{I}
adheres to
Z
if and only if
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap Z=\emptyset
.
We conclude that
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap Z=\emptyset
if and only if
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap T=\emptyset
.
But
Z\neq T
gives some
\sigma\in L^{\star}
such that
\sigma\in T
and
\sigma\not\in Z
.
The case
\sigma\not\in T
and
\sigma\in Z
is entirely analogous.
We now have
\sigma\in T\cap\overline{Z}
.
If we can construct an implementation VPTS
\mathscr{Q}
over
L
with
\sigma\in otr(\mathscr{Q})
, then we have reached a contradiction
because we would have
\sigma\in otr(\mathscr{Q})\cap T
and
\sigma\not\in otr(\mathscr{Q})\cap Z
.
But that is simple.
Let
\sigma=x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{k}
, with
k\geq 0
and
x_{i}\in L
(
1\leq i\leq k
).
Define
L_{c}=L_{r}=\emptyset
and
L_{i}=L
, and let
\mathscr{Q}=\langle Q,\{q_{0}\},L,\emptyset,R\rangle
, where
Q=\{q_{i}\,|\,0\leq i\leq k\}
, and
R=\{(q_{i-1},x_{i},\sharp,q_{i})\,|\,1\leq i\leq k\}
.
Clearly,
\sigma\in otr(\mathscr{Q})
, concluding the proof.
∎

Lemma
4.7
says that the test suite
T=\big[(D\cap\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S}))\cup(F\cap otr(\mathscr{S}))\big]
is complete for the specification
\mathscr{S}
and the pair of languages
(D,F)
.
So, given an implementation
\mathscr{I}
, checking if it
(D,F)
-visibly conforms to
\mathscr{S}
is equivalent to checking if
\mathscr{I}
adheres to
T
and, by Definition
4.5
, the latter is equivalent to checking that we have
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap T=\emptyset
.

We also note that, in Lemma
4.7
, in order to construct
\mathscr{Q}
with
\sigma\in otr(\mathscr{Q})
it was crucial that we had no restrictions on the size of
\mathscr{Q}
, since we have no control over the size of the witness
\sigma
.

4.3
Checking Visual Conformance for VPTS models

When testing conformance one important issue is the size of test suites, relatively to the size of the given specification.
Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
be a VPTS.
A reasonable measure of the size of
\mathscr{S}
would be the number of symbols required to write down a complete syntactic description of
\mathscr{S}
.
Assume that
\mathscr{S}
has
m=|T|
transitions,
n=|S|
states,
\ell=|L|
action symbols, and
p=|\Gamma|
stack symbols.
Since any transition can be written using
\mathcal{O}(\ln(n\ell p))
symbols,
the size of
\mathscr{S}
is
\mathcal{O}(m\ln(n\ell p))
.
From Remark
3.7
, we see that
n
is
\mathcal{O}(m)
and, clearly, so are
\ell
and
p
.
Thus, the size of
\mathscr{S}
is bounded by
\mathcal{O}(m\ln m)
.
If we fix the stack and action alphabets, then the size of the VPTS will be bounded by

\mathcal{O}(m)
.
In what follows, and with almost no prejudice, we will ignore the small logarithmic factor.
2
2

2

It is also customary to write
\mathcal{O}(m\ln m)
as
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(m)
.
In the sequel, we can always replace
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
by
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\cdot)
.

Given visibly pushdown languages
D
and
F
over
L
, and given a specification
\mathscr{S}
over
L
, Lemma
4.7

says that the fault model
T
is complete for
\mathscr{S}
and
(D,F)
, where

T=\big[(D\cap\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S}))\cup(F\cap otr(\mathscr{S}))\big]
.
Assume that
L(\mathscr{A}_{D})=D
and
L(\mathscr{A}_{F})=F
where
\mathscr{A}_{D}
and
\mathscr{A}_{F}
are deterministic VPAs with
n_{D}
and
n_{F}
states, respectively.
Also, assume that
\mathscr{S}
is deterministic with
n_{S}
states.
Proposition
3.11
says that we may as well take
\mathscr{S}
as a contracted and deterministic VPTS.
Now, Proposition
3.14

gives a deterministic VPA
\mathscr{A}_{1}
with
n_{S}
states and such that
L(\mathscr{A}_{1})=otr(\mathscr{S})
.
Using Proposition
2.17
,
we can construct a deterministic VPA
\mathscr{B}_{1}
with
n_{S}+1
states and such that
L(\mathscr{B}_{1})=\overline{L(\mathscr{A}_{1})}=\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S})
.
Using Proposition
2.13
we can obtain a deterministic VPA
\mathscr{A}_{2}
with at most
n_{S}n_{F}
states
and such that
L(\mathscr{A}_{2})=L(\mathscr{A}_{F})\cap L(\mathscr{A}_{1})=F\cap otr(\mathscr{S})
, and also a
deterministic VPA
\mathscr{B}_{2}
with
(n_{S}+1)n_{D}
states
such that
L(\mathscr{B}_{2})=L(\mathscr{A}_{D})\cap L(\mathscr{B}_{1})=D\cap\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S})
.
Proposition
2.16
gives a deterministic VPA
\mathscr{T}
with
(n_{S}n_{F}+1)(n_{S}n_{D}+n_{D}+1)
states
and such that
L(\mathscr{T})=L(\mathscr{A}_{2})\cup L(\mathscr{B}_{2})=T
.
Proposition
2.16
also says that
\mathscr{T}
is non-blocking and has no
\varepsilon
-moves.
Lemma
4.7
says that
L(\mathscr{T})
is a complete test suite for
\mathscr{S}
and
(D,F)
.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
L
be an alphabet with
|L|=n_{L}
.
Let
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{I}
be deterministic IOVPTSs over
L
with
n_{S}
and
n_{I}
states, respectively.
Let
\mathscr{A}_{D}
and
\mathscr{A}_{F}
be deterministic VPAs over
L
with
n_{D}
and
n_{F}
states, respectively,
with
L(\mathscr{A}_{D})=D
,
L(\mathscr{A}_{F})=F
.
Then, we can construct a deterministic, non-blocking VPA
\mathscr{T}
with at most
(n_{S}n_{F}+1)(n_{S}n_{D}+n_{D}+1)
states and no
\varepsilon
-moves,
and such that
L(\mathscr{T})
is a complete test suite for
\mathscr{S}
and
(D,F)
.
Moreover, there is an algorithm with polynomial time complexity
\mathcal{O}((n_{I}n_{S}^{2}n_{F}n_{D})^{3})
and that checks if
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,F}\,\,\mathscr{S}
.

Proof.

The preceding discussion gives a deterministic and non-blocking VPA
\mathscr{T}
with at most
n_{T}=(n_{S}n_{F}+1)(n_{S}n_{D}+n_{D}+1)
states and no
\varepsilon
-moves, and
such that
L(\mathscr{T})=T=\big[(D\cap\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S}))\cup(F\cap otr(\mathscr{S}))\big].

Since
\mathscr{I}
is deterministic, using Propositions
3.11
and
3.14
, we can get a deterministic VPA
\mathscr{A}
with at most
n_{I}
states,
and such that
otr(\mathscr{I})=L(\mathscr{A})
.
From Proposition
2.13

we can construct a deterministic VPA
\mathscr{B}
with at most
n_{I}n_{T}
states,
and such that
L(\mathscr{B})=L(\mathscr{A})\cap L(\mathscr{T})=otr(\mathscr{I})\cap T
.

The emptiness problem for a VPA is decidable in time
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
, where
n
is the number of states in the VPA
[
1
,
5
,
10
,
6
]
.
Hence, we can check whether
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,F}\,\,\mathscr{S}
in asymptotic time
\mathcal{O}((n_{I}n_{S}^{2}n_{F}n_{D})^{3}))
.
∎

Note that, if the specification
\mathscr{S}
and the model languages
D
and
F
are fixed, so that
n_{S}
,
n_{D}
and
n_{F}
are constants, the algorithm runs in polynomial time
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
, where
n
is the number of states in the implementation being tested for visual conformance.

5
An
ioco-like
Conformance Checking for IOVPTS Models

In this section we investigate whether IUTs, also described as IOVPTS models, conform to a given specification IOVPTS model.
Here we use a new notion of an
ioco-like
conformance relation for IOVPTSs.
That notion captures the same idea as the standard notion of conformance as studied by Tretmans
[
17
]
, but using Labeled Transition Systems (LTSs) which do not have access to a pushdown stack memory.
The idea is that, given a specification
\mathscr{S}
and an IUT
\mathscr{I}
, we say whether
\mathscr{I}

ioco-like
conforms to
\mathscr{S}
when, for any valid observable behavior
\sigma
of
\mathscr{S}
, any output symbol that
\mathscr{I}
can emit after running over
\sigma
is, necessarily, among the output symbols that
\mathscr{S}
can also emit after it runs over the same
\sigma
.

5.1
An ioco-like Conformance Relation for IOVPTS models

Here we define an ioco-like conformance relation
[
17
]
for IOVPTS models.
Let
\mathscr{S}
be a specification IOVPTS and let
\mathscr{I}
be an implementation IOVPTS.
The ioco-like relation essentially requires that any observable trace
\sigma
of
\mathscr{I}
is also an observable trace of
\mathscr{S}
and, further, if
\sigma
leads
\mathscr{I}
to a configuration from which
\mathscr{I}
can emit the output label
\ell
, then
\mathscr{S}
must also end up in a configuration from which the label
\ell
can also be output.
Next we formalize these ideas.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L_{I},L_{U},\Gamma,T\rangle
and
\mathscr{I}=\langle Q,Q_{in},L_{I},L_{U},\Delta,R\rangle
be IOVPTSs, with
L=L_{I}\cup L_{U}
.

1.

Define the function
\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\!\!\!:\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{S}}\times L^{\star}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{S}})
by letting

(s,\alpha)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\sigma=\big\{(q,\beta)\,|\,(s,\alpha)\overset{\sigma}{\Rightarrow}(q,\beta)\big\},\quad\text{for all $(s,\alpha)\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{S}}$, $\sigma\in L^{\star}$}.

2.

We say that
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
if for all
\sigma\in otr(\mathscr{S})
,
q_{0}\in Q_{in}
,
\ell\in\text{\,\bf out}((q_{0},\bot)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\sigma)
there is some
s_{0}\in S_{in}
such that

\ell\in\text{\,\bf out}((s_{0},\bot)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\sigma)
.

Now we show that the

ioco-like
conformance relation can be seen as an instance of the conformance relation based on languages given in Definition
4.1
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L_{I},L_{U},\Gamma,T\rangle
be a specification IOVPTS and let
\mathscr{I}=\langle Q,Q_{in},L_{I},L_{U},\Delta,R\rangle
be an implementation IOVPTS.
Then
D=otr(\mathscr{S})L_{U}
is a VPL, and we have that
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
if and only if

\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,\emptyset}\,\,\mathscr{S}
.

Proof.

From Definition
3.17
we know that the semantics of an IOVPTS is given by the semantics of its underlying VPTS.
So, for the remainder of this proof, when we write
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{I}
we will be referring to the underlying VPTSs
of the given IOVPTSs
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{I}
, respectively.
By Proposition
3.14
we see that
L(\mathscr{A})=otr(\mathscr{S})
, where
\mathscr{A}
is obtained using Definition
3.12
, is a VPL.
Using Proposition
2.18
we conclude that
D
is also a VPL.

Next, we argue that
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
if and only if
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,\emptyset}\,\,\mathscr{S}
.
First assume that we have
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,\emptyset}\,\,\mathscr{S}
.
Because
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap\emptyset\cap otr(\mathscr{S})=\emptyset
, it is clear from Definition
4.1
that
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,\emptyset}\,\,\mathscr{S}
is equivalent
to
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap D\subseteq otr(\mathscr{S})
.
Let
\sigma\in otr(\mathscr{S})
and let
\ell\in\text{\,\bf out}((q_{0},\bot)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\sigma)
for some
q_{0}\in Q_{in}
.
We must show that
\ell\in\text{\,\bf out}((s_{0},\bot)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\sigma)
for some
s_{0}\in S_{in}
.
Because
\ell\in\text{\,\bf out}((q_{0},\bot)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\sigma)
we get

\sigma,\sigma\ell\in otr(\mathscr{I})
.
Since
\ell\in L_{U}
, we get
\sigma\ell\in otr(\mathscr{S})L_{U}
and so
\sigma\ell\in D
.
We conclude that
\sigma\ell\in otr(\mathscr{I})\cap D
.
Since we already know that
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap D\subseteq otr(\mathscr{S})
, we now have

\sigma\ell\in otr(\mathscr{S})
.
So,
\ell\in\text{\,\bf out}((s_{0},\bot)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\sigma)
for some
s_{0}\in S_{in}
, as desired.

Next, assume that
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
and we want to show that
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,\emptyset}\,\,\mathscr{S}
holds.
Since
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap\emptyset\cap otr(\mathscr{S})=\emptyset
,
the first condition of Definition
4.1
is immediately verified.
We now turn to the second condition of Definition
4.1
.
In order to show that
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap D\subseteq otr(\mathscr{S})
,
let
\sigma\in otr(\mathscr{I})\cap D
.
Then,
\sigma\in D
and so
\sigma=\alpha\ell
with
\ell\in L_{U}
and
\alpha\in otr(\mathscr{S})
, because
D=otr(\mathscr{S})L_{U}
.
Also,
\sigma\in otr(\mathscr{I})
gives
\alpha\ell\in otr(\mathscr{I})
, and so
\alpha\in otr(\mathscr{I})
.
Then, because
\ell\in L_{U}
, we get
\ell\in\text{\,\bf out}((q_{0},\bot)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\alpha)
for some
q_{0}\in Q_{in}
.
Because we assumed
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
and we have
\alpha\in otr(\mathscr{S})
, we also get
\ell\in\text{\,\bf out}((s_{0},\bot)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\alpha)
, for some
s_{0}\in S_{in}
.
So
\alpha\ell\in otr(\mathscr{S})
. Because
\sigma=\alpha\ell
, we have
\sigma\in otr(\mathscr{S})
.
We have, thus, showed that
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap D\subseteq otr(\mathscr{S})
, as desired.
∎

Example 5.3
.

We illustrate the relationship between
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,\emptyset}\,\,\mathscr{S}
,
using the specification IOVPTS
\mathscr{S}
depicted in Figure
3
and the implementation
\mathscr{I}
depicted in Figure
4
.

We want to check whether
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,F}\,\,\mathscr{S}
holds.
Let
\sigma=aabb
.
From Figure
3
it is apparent that
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\Rightarrow}(s_{2},\bot)
and that

(s_{0},\bot)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\sigma=\{(s_{2},\bot)\}
.
From Figure
4
we get
(q_{0},\bot)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\sigma=\{(q_{2},\bot)\}
.
Also,
x\in\text{\,\bf out}((q_{2},\bot))
, but
x\not\in\text{\,\bf out}((s_{2},\bot))
.
So, by Definition
5.1
,
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
does not hold.

Now take
\sigma=aabbx
.
Since
aabb\in otr(\mathscr{S})
, we get
aabbx\in otr(\mathscr{S})L_{U}=D
.
Also,
aabbx\in otr(\mathscr{I})
and
aabbx\not\in otr(\mathscr{S})
, so that

aabbx\in otr(\mathscr{I})\cap D\cap\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S})
.
Using Proposition
4.2
, we conclude that
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,\emptyset}\,\,\mathscr{S}
does not hold also, as expected.
\Box

We can also characterize the
ioco-like
relation as follows.

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
\mathscr{S}
be a specification IOVPTS and let
\mathscr{I}
be an implementation IOVPTS.
Then
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
if and only if
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap T=\emptyset
, where
T=\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S})\cap\big[otr(\mathscr{S})L_{U}\big]
.

Proof.

From Lemma
5.2
we have that
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
if and only if
\mathscr{I}\,\text{\bf vconf}_{D,\emptyset}\,\,\mathscr{S}
, where

D=otr(\mathscr{S})L_{U}
.
From Proposition
4.2
we know that the latter holds if and only if

otr(\mathscr{I})\cap(D\cap\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S}))=\emptyset
.
∎

Example 5.5
.

Let the IOVPTS
\mathscr{S}
of Figure
3
be the specification model, with
L_{I}=\{a,b\}
, and
L_{U}=\{x\}
.
It is not hard to see that the VPA
\mathscr{TS}
, depicted in Figure
7
, is such that
L(\mathscr{TS})=D\cap\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S})
, where
D=otr(\mathscr{S})L_{U}
.

d_{0}

d_{1}

d_{2}

D

b/A_{-}
b/A_{-}
b/A_{-}
a/A_{+}
a/A_{+}
x/A_{-}
a/A_{-}
x/A_{-}
x/\bot_{-}
x/A_{-}
x/\bot_{-}
x/\bot_{-}

Figure 7:
The VPA that accepts the language
\overline{otr}(\mathscr{S})\cap\big[otr(\mathscr{S})L_{U}\big]
for the IOVPTS
\mathscr{S}
of Figure
3
.

According to Lemma
4.7
and Corollary
5.4
, the language accepted by
\mathscr{TS}
,
L(\mathscr{TS})
, is a complete test suite for
\mathscr{S}
and
(D,\emptyset)
.
\Box

5.2
IOVPTS Fault Models and Test Suite Completeness

Here we introduce the notion of an external tester environment that can also be formalized using an Input/Output Visibly Pushdown Transition System with a special
fail
state.
We call this model a
Visibly Pushdown Fault Model
.
Such a model,
\mathscr{T}
, operates in conjunction with an IUT,
\mathscr{I}
.
Their joint behavior can be interpreted as
\mathscr{T}
sending symbols to
\mathscr{I}
, and receiving symbols from it.
Therefore, the sets of input and output symbols in
\mathscr{T}
and
\mathscr{I}
are reversed.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
L_{I}
and
L_{U}
be sets of input and output symbols, respectively, with
L=L_{I}\cup L_{U}
.
A
visibly pushdown fault model
(
VPFM
) over
L
is any IOVPTS
\mathscr{T}\in\mathscr{IOVP}(L_{U},L_{I})
with a distinguished
fail
state.

Given a VPFM
\mathscr{T}
and an IUT
\mathscr{I}
, in order to formally describe the exchange of action symbols between
\mathscr{T}
and
\mathscr{I}
, we define their cross-product VPTS
\mathscr{T}\times\mathscr{I}
.
The cross-product is easily constructed by taking the product of the corresponding VPAs.
Recall Definitions
2.9
and
3.12
.

Definition 5.7
.

Let
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{I}
be two IOVPTSs over an alphabet
L
.
Their cross-product is the VPTS
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
induced by the product VPA
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{S}}\times\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{I}}
,
where
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{S}}
and
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{I}}
are the VPAs induced by
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{I}
, respectively.

Having an implementation
\mathscr{I}
and a VPFM
\mathscr{T}
as a tester, we need to say when a test run is successful with respect to a given specification
\mathscr{S}
.
Recalling that
\mathscr{T}
signals an unsuccessful run when it reaches a
fail
state, the following definition formalizes the verdict of a test run.
Recall Definition
5.1
.
Importantly, given a specification
\mathscr{S}
, when the test run is successful we need a guarantee that
\mathscr{I}
does
ioco-like
conform to
\mathscr{S}
, and conversely, that the test run surely fails when
\mathscr{I}
does not
ioco-like
conform to
\mathscr{S}
, for any implementation
\mathscr{I}
.
That is, we need a property of completeness.
It is also customary to specify that IUTs of interest are only taken from particular subclasses of IOVPTS models, and completeness is then defined when taking implementations from these subclasses only.

Definition 5.8
.

Let
\mathscr{T}=\langle Q,Q_{in},L_{U},L_{I},\Delta,R\rangle
be a VPFM and let
\mathscr{I}=\langle S,S_{in},L_{I},L_{U},\Gamma,T\rangle
be an implementation over the same alphabet

L=L_{I}\cup L_{U}
.
We say that
\mathscr{I}

passes

\mathscr{T}
if, for all
\sigma\in L^{\star}
and all initial configurations
((t_{0},q_{0}),\bot)
of their cross-product
\mathscr{T}\times\mathscr{I}
we do not have

((t_{0},q_{0}),\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{T}\times\mathscr{I}}{\Rightarrow}}((\text{fail},q),\alpha\bot)

for any configuration
((\text{fail},q),\alpha\bot)
of
\mathscr{T}\times\mathscr{I}
.

Now we construct a VPFM which is complete relatively to a given specification
\mathscr{S}
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\mathscr{B}\in\mathscr{IOVP}(L_{I},L_{U})
be a deterministic specification with
n
states.
We can effectively construct a fault model
\mathscr{T}
which is
ioco-like
complete for
\mathscr{B}
.
Moreover,
\mathscr{T}
is deterministic and has
n+1
states.

Proof.

From Corollary
5.4
we know that, for any IUT
\mathscr{I}
,
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{B}
if and only if

otr(\mathscr{I})\cap T=\emptyset
, where
T=\overline{otr}(\mathscr{B})\cap[otr(\mathscr{B})L_{U}]
.
The desired fault model
\mathscr{T}
can be constructed as follows.
If
\ell
is a push symbol in
L_{U}\cap L_{c}
add to
\mathscr{S}
a transition
(s,\ell,Z,\text{fail})
if we do not have in
\mathscr{S}
any other transitions like
(s,\ell,W,p)
in
\mathscr{S}
for any
p
and any
W
.
Likewise, when
\ell
is a pop symbol in
L_{U}
add a transition
(s,\ell,W,\text{fail})

given that we do not have any other transitions
(s,\ell,W,p)
, for any
p
.
Finally, for any simple symbol
\ell
in
L_{U}
, add
(s,\ell,\sharp,\text{fail})
, if we do not in
\mathscr{S}
have a transition
(s,\ell,\sharp,p)
, for any
p
.
Then, an argument can show that
\mathscr{I}
passes
\mathscr{T}
if and only if
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{B}
, for any IUT
\mathscr{I}
.

Details can be found in the complete proof in Appendix
A.9
, at page
A.9
.
∎

5.3
Testing IUT models for
ioco-like
conformance

Once we have a VPFM which is complete for a given specification, we can test whether IUTs
ioco-like
conform to that specification.
In order to do so, we introduce the notion of
balanced run
.
Let
\mathscr{V}
be any VPTS over
L
.
We say that a string
\sigma\in L^{\star}
induces a
balanced run
from
p
to
q
in
\mathscr{V}
if we have
(p,\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{V}}{\rightarrow}}(q,\bot)
.
The next theorem gives a decision procedure for testing
ioco-like
conformance.

Theorem 5.10
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S_{\mathscr{S}},\{s_{0}\},L_{I},L_{U},\Delta_{\mathscr{S}},T_{\mathscr{S}}\rangle\in\mathscr{IOVP}(L_{I},L_{U})
be a deterministic specification, and let
\mathscr{I}=\langle S_{\mathscr{I}},I_{in},L_{I},L_{U},\Delta_{\mathscr{I}},T_{\mathscr{I}}\rangle\in\mathscr{IOVP}(L_{I},L_{U})
be an IUT.
Then we can effectively decide whether
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,S
holds.
Further, if
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,S
does not hold, we can find
\sigma\in otr(\mathscr{S})
,
\ell\in L_{U}
that verify this condition,
i.e.
,
\ell\in\text{\,\bf out}((q_{0},\bot)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\sigma)
for some
q_{0}\in I_{in}
, and
\ell\not\in\text{\,\bf out}((s_{0},\bot)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\sigma)
.

Proof.

Let
L=L_{I}\cup L_{U}
.
From Corollary
5.4
we know that
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
does not hold if and only if there is some
\sigma\in otr(\mathscr{S})
,
\ell\in L_{U}
such that
\sigma\ell\in otr(\mathscr{I})\cap T
, where
T=\overline{otr(\mathscr{S})}\cap\big[otr(\mathscr{S})L_{U}\big]
.
The proof of Lemma
5.9
, and Proposition
3.9
, indicate how to obtain a deterministic fault model
\mathscr{T}=\langle S_{\mathscr{T}},\{t_{0}\},L_{U},L_{I},\Delta_{\mathscr{T}},T_{\mathscr{T}}\rangle
, with no
\varsigma
-moves, and such that
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
if and only if
\mathscr{I}
does not pass
\mathscr{T}
, that is,

((t_{0},q_{0}),\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{P}}{\Rightarrow}}((\text{fail},q),\alpha\bot)
for some
\sigma\in L^{\star}
,
where
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{T}\times\mathscr{I}
is the product IOVPTS, and
(t_{0},q_{0})
is an initial state of
\mathscr{P}
.

Write
\mathscr{A}=\langle A,I,L,\Gamma,\rho\rangle
for the underlying VPTS associated to
\mathscr{P}
.
In order to check for

ioco-like
conformance, and using Proposition
3.13
, it suffices to check whether a configuration
((\text{fail},q),\alpha\bot)
is reachable from some initial configuration of
\mathscr{A}
, and where
q
can be any state of
\mathscr{I}
.
We will modify
\mathscr{A}
in a simple way in order to make this reachability problem more amenable.

Emptying the stack.

First we move to a pop state after reaching
fail
in
\mathscr{T}
.
For all states
(\text{fail},q)
in
\mathscr{A}
, add the internal transition
((\text{fail},q),\varsigma,\sharp,f_{1})
to
\rho
, where
f_{1}
is a new state added to
A
.
Then, for all
W\in\Gamma
add the self-loops
(f_{1},b_{1},W,f_{1})
to
\rho
, where
b_{1}
is a new pop symbol added to
L
.
Next, add the transition
(f_{1},b_{1},\bot,f_{2})
to
\rho
, where
f_{2}
is another new state added to
A
.
Let
\mathscr{A}_{1}=\langle A_{1},I,L_{1},\Gamma,\rho_{1}\rangle
be the resulting VPTS obtained after these modifications to
\mathscr{A}
.
Since
(\text{fail},q)
is a sink state in
\mathscr{A}
, it is easy to see that we have
((t_{0},q_{0}),\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}((\text{fail},q),\alpha\bot)
in
\mathscr{A}
if and only if
((t_{0},q_{0}),\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(f_{2},\bot)
in
\mathscr{A}_{1}
, where
\mu=\sigma\varsigma b_{1}^{k}
with
k=|\alpha|+1
.

Eliminating moves on an empty stack.

Let
s_{0}
be a new state added to
A_{1}
,
a_{2}
a new push symbol added to
L_{1}
, and
Z_{2}
a new stack symbol added to
\Gamma
.
We make
s_{0}
the new (unique) initial state and add to
\rho_{1}
the self-loop
(s_{0},a_{2},Z_{2},s_{0})
.
Next we connect
s_{0}
to all initial states of
A_{1}
, by adding internal transitions
(s_{0},\varsigma,\sharp,s)
for all
s\in I
.
Finally, we replace any pop transition on an empty stack
(p,c,\bot,q)
by the new pop transition
(p,c,Z_{2},q)
.
Let
\mathscr{A}_{2}=\langle A_{2},\{s_{0}\},L_{2},\Gamma_{2},\rho_{2}\rangle
be the new VPTS after these modifications to
\mathscr{A}_{1}
.

Suppose we have
(s,\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{A}_{1}}{\rightarrow}}(q,\bot)
, where
s\in I
is an initial state, and assume that we have
0\leq k\leq|\sigma|
pop moves on the empty stack on this run of
\mathscr{A}_{1}
.
Then, a simple induction on
k
shows that in
\mathscr{A}_{2}
we have

(s_{0},\bot)\overset{a_{2}^{k}}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(s_{0},Z_{2}^{k}\bot)\overset{\varsigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(s,Z_{2}^{k}\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,\bot)
.
Conversely, if we have a run
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{A}_{2}}{\rightarrow}}(q,\bot)
, then
\sigma=a_{2}^{k}\varsigma\mu
for some
k\geq 0
, and a simple induction on
k
shows that in
\mathscr{A}_{1}
we have
(s,\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{\mathscr{A}_{1}}{\rightarrow}}(q,\bot)
where
s
is an initial state and in this run over
\mathscr{A}_{1}
we made
k
pop moves on an empty stack.

After these transformations, we see that
((t_{0},q_{0}),\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\rightarrow}}((\text{fail},q),\alpha\bot)
, with
(t_{0},q_{0})
initial in
\mathscr{A}
, if and only if
we have
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{\mathscr{A}_{2}}{\rightarrow}}(f_{2},\bot)
, where
\mu=a_{2}^{k}\varsigma\sigma\varsigma b_{1}^{n}
for some
k\geq 1
and
m\geq 0
.
Now, from the definition, we have
((t_{0},q_{0}),\bot)\overset{\eta}{\underset{\mathscr{P}}{\Rightarrow}}((\text{fail},q),\alpha\bot)
if and only if
((t_{0},q_{0}),\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\rightarrow}}((\text{fail},q),\alpha\bot)
where
\eta=h_{\varsigma}(\sigma)
.
Thus,
((t_{0},q_{0}),\bot)\overset{\eta}{\underset{\mathscr{P}}{\Rightarrow}}((\text{fail},q),\alpha\bot)
if and only if we have a balanced run
\mu
from
s_{0}
to
f_{2}
in
\mathscr{A}_{2}
and

\eta=h_{\{a_{2},b_{1},\varsigma\}}(\mu)
, that is,
\eta
is obtained from
\mu
by erasing from
\mu
all occurrences of
a_{2}
,
b_{1}
and
\varsigma
.
Putting it together, we have:
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
does not hold if and only if
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{\mathscr{A}_{2}}{\rightarrow}}(f_{2},\bot)
.

We have reduced the

ioco-like
conformance test to the following problem: given two states
p
and
q
of a VPTS, find a string
\sigma
that induces a balanced run from
p
to
q
, or indicate that such a string does not exist.
Next, we describe how to solve this problem.

The following construction was also inspired from ideas in
[
7
,
13
]
.
Let
\mathscr{P}=\langle Q,Q_{in},L,\Gamma,\rho\rangle
be a VPTS
given by an incidence vector
P
, indexed by
Q
, where
P[p]
points to a list of all transitions
(p,x,Z,q)\in\rho
where
p
is the source state.
We assume that
\mathscr{P}
has no pop transitions on the empty stack, that is, of the form
(p,x,\bot,q)
where
x
is a pop symbol.
Algorithm
1
shows the pseudo-code.

We will use two vectors of pointers,
In
and
Out
, both indexed by
Q
, and a queue
V
.
The entry
In[p]
will point to a list of triples
(s,a,Z)
corresponding to a transitions
(s,a,Z,p)
where
a\in L_{c}
, that is,
p
as the target state of a push transition.
Likewise, and entry in
Out[p]
will point to a list of triples
(a,Z,s)
corresponding to transitions
(p,a,Z,s)
where
a\in L_{r}
, that is,
p
is the source state of a pop transition.
We will also need a square matrix
R
, indexed by
Q\times Q
, where
R[p,q]
will contain: (i)
[a,p,q,b]
, or (ii)
[p,c,q]
, or (iii)
[p,s,q]
, or (iv)
0
, where
a\in L_{c}
,
b\in L_{r}
,
c\in L_{i}\cup\{\varsigma\}
, and
p
,
q
,
s
are states.
The general idea is that, when
R[p,q]\neq 0
then it will code for a string
\sigma
that induces a balanced run from
p
to
q
.

We now examine at Algorithm
1
.
Lines 1–10 initialize
V
,
In
,
Out
and
R
.
At line 6, note that a transition
(p,a,\sharp,q)
immediately induces a balanced run from
p
to
q
.
At line 10, we collect a simple balanced run from
p
to
r
that is induced by a push transition
(p,a,Z,q)
and a pop transition
(q,b,Z,r)
.
In the main loop, lines 11–19, removing
(p,q)
from
V
indicates that we already have a string, say
\sigma
, that induces a balanced run from
p
to
q
.
At lines 13–14, a string
\mu
that induces balanced run from
s
to
p
is encoded in
R[s,p]
.
Hence,
\mu\sigma
induces a balanced run from
s
to
q
.
If we still do not have
a balanced run from
s
to
q
, we can now encode the string
\mu\sigma
in
R[s,q]
and move the pair
(s,q)
to
V
so that it can be examined later.
Lines 15–16 do the same, but now we encode in
R[p,t]
a string that induces a balanced run from
p
to
t
.
At lines 17–19, we search for a push transition
(s,a,Z,p)
and a matching pop transition
(q,b,Z,t)
and, when successful, we encode
a\sigma b
as a string that induces a balanced run from
s
to
t
.
The cycle repeats until saturation when
V=null
, or until we find a balanced run from
s_{i}
to
s_{e}
, as requested.
Lines 22–25 list a simple recursive procedure that extracts the string encoded in
R[p,q]\neq 0
.
∎

Algorithm 1

Checking for balanced runs in a VPTS
\mathscr{P}=\langle Q,Q_{in},L,\Gamma,\rho\rangle

Now we argue for the correctness of Algorithm
1
.

Theorem 5.11
.

Let
\mathscr{P}=\langle Q,Q_{in},L,\Gamma,\rho\rangle
be a VPTS with no transitions of the form
(p,a,\bot,q)
in
\rho
.
Also let
s_{i}
,
s_{e}\in Q
, with
s_{i}\neq s_{e}
.
Suppose
\mathscr{P}
,
s_{i}
,
s_{e}
are input to Algorithm
1
.
Then it stops and returns a string
\sigma\in L^{\star}
such that
(s_{i},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{P}}{\rightarrow}}(s_{e},\bot)
, or
it indicates that such a string does not exist.

Proof.

From the structure of the code, it is simple to show that if
R[s,t]\neq 0
during an execution of Algorithm
1
, then there it codes for a string that induces a balanced run from
s
to
t
.
When the algorithm terminates, the simple recursive call
getstring(
s_{i},s_{e}
)
extracts a witness string.

For the other direction, assume that the main loop terminates with
R[s,t]=0
and we have a string
\sigma
that induces a balanced run from
s
to
t
, that is,
(s,\bot,)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(t,\bot)
.
A simple argument will show that we can always reach a contradiction by considering two cases, namely, an intermediate configuration
(r,\bot)
occurs, or does not occur,
in the run
(s,\bot,)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(t,\bot)
.

For details see the complete proof in Appendix
A.10
at page
A.10
.
∎

Now we examine the complexity of our testing approach.

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
\mathscr{S}\in\mathscr{IOVP}(L_{I},L_{U})
be a deterministic specification with
n_{s}
states and
m_{s}
transitions, and let
\mathscr{I}\in\mathscr{IOVP}(L_{I},L_{U})
be an IUT with
n_{i}
states and
m_{i}
transitions.
Then there is a procedure, with worst case asymptotic polynomial time complexity bounded by
\mathcal{O}(n_{s}^{3}n_{i}^{3}+n_{s}^{2}m_{s}^{4}m_{i}^{2})
, that verifies whether
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
. Moreover, if
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,S
does not hold, the procedure finds an input string that proves this condition.

Proof.

Write
L=L_{I}\cup L_{U}
,
|L|=\ell
and let
g_{s}
,
g_{i}
be the number of stack symbols in
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{I}
, respectively.
We now follow the argument in the proof of Theorem
5.10
.

First, the fault model
\mathscr{T}
is constructed in Lemma
5.9
.
From Eqs. (
30
)–(
32
) we see that
n_{t}=n_{s}+1
and
m_{t}\leq m_{s}+n_{s}g_{s}\ell
, where
n_{t}
and
m_{t}
are the number of states and transitions in
\mathscr{T}
, respectively.
It is easy to see that
\mathscr{T}
can be effectively constructed from
\mathscr{S}
by an algorithm with worst case time complexity bounded by
\mathcal{O}(m_{s}+n_{s}g_{s}\ell)
.
We can safely assume
m_{s}\geq g_{s}
and
m_{s}\geq\ell
.
Hence,
m_{t}
and
n_{t}
can be bounded by
\mathcal{O}(n_{s}m_{s}^{2})
and
\mathcal{O}(n_{s})
, respectively, and the worst case time complexity to obtain
\mathscr{T}
can also be bounded by
\mathcal{O}(n_{s}m_{s}^{2})
.

Next, we construct the product
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{T}\times\mathscr{I}
.
Let
n_{p}
,
m_{p}
and
g_{p}
be the number of states, transitions and stack symbols in
\mathscr{P}
, respectively.
Using Definition
2.9
, and since
\mathscr{T}
has no
\varsigma
-moves, we see that
n_{p}=n_{t}n_{i}
,
m_{p}\leq m_{t}m_{i}+n_{t}m_{i}
, and
g_{p}=g_{s}g_{i}
.
As before, a simple algorithm with worst case time complexity bounded by
\mathcal{O}(m_{t}m_{i}+n_{t}m_{i})
can construct
\mathscr{P}
given
\mathscr{T}
and
\mathscr{I}
.
Hence,
n_{p}
,
m_{p}
and
g_{p}
can be bounded by
\mathcal{O}(n_{s}n_{i})
,
\mathcal{O}(n_{s}m_{s}^{2}m_{i})
and
\mathcal{O}(m_{s}m_{i})
, respectively, and the worst case time complexity to obtain
\mathscr{P}
can be bounded by
\mathcal{O}(n_{s}m_{s}^{2}m_{i})
.

Finally, Theorem
5.10
requires that we construct a VPTS
\mathscr{A}=\langle S_{a},\{s_{0}\},L_{a},\Gamma_{a},\rho_{a}\rangle
from
\mathscr{P}
, and guarantees
that
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,S
does not hold if and only if we have
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\rightarrow}}(f,\bot)
for some
\mu\in(L_{a}\cup\{\varsigma\})^{\star}
, where
f
is a specific state in
S_{a}
with
s_{0}\neq f
.
Moreover, if such is the case, the final steps in the proof of Theorem
5.10
indicate how to obtain the desired string
\sigma
that proves that
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,S
fails.
Let
n_{a}=|S_{a}|
and
m_{a}=|\rho_{a}|
.
From the proof of Theorem
5.10
, it is easy to get
n_{a}=n_{p}+3
and
m_{a}=m_{p}+2n_{i}+g_{p}+2
.
Also, a simple procedure, running in worst case time complexity
\mathcal{O}(m_{a}+n_{a})
, can be used construct the VPTS
\mathscr{A}
given the product
\mathscr{P}
.
Then,
n_{a}
can be bounded by
\mathcal{O}(n_{s}n_{i})
,
m_{a}
can be bounded by
\mathcal{O}(n_{s}m_{s}^{2}m_{i})
, and
the worst case time complexity to construct
\mathscr{A}
can be bounded by
\mathcal{O}(n_{s}m_{s}^{2}m_{i})
.

The final step is to submit
\mathscr{A}
and the two states
s_{0}
,
f
to Algorithm
1
.
Theorem
5.11
guarantees that Algorithm
1
correctly produces the desired string
\mu
or indicates that no such string exists.

We now derive an asymptotic upper bound on the number of steps required for Algorithm
1
in the worst case.
Clearly, the number of steps for lines 1–3 can be bounded by
\mathcal{O}(n_{a}^{2})
.

For each state
p\in S_{a}
, let
s_{p}
,
t_{q}
be the number of transitions in
\mathscr{A}
that have
p
as a source and
q
as a target state, respectively.
Thus,
\sum_{p\in Q}s_{p}\leq m_{a}
and
\sum_{p\in Q}t_{p}\leq m_{a}
for all
p\in S_{a}
.
The number of steps relative to lines 4–10 can be bound by

\sum_{p\in S_{a}}\big(s_{p}\sum_{q\in S_{a}}t_{q}\big)\leq\sum_{p\in S_{a}}(s_{p}m_{a})=m_{a}\sum_{p\in S_{a}}s_{p}\leq m_{a}^{2}.

From the proof of Theorem
5.11
, we have that each pair
(p,q)
can enter the queue
V
at most once.
Hence, a state
p
will appear in a pair
(p,q)
removed from
V
at most
n_{a}
times.
Since the number of steps at each execution of lines 13–14 can be bounded by
\mathcal{O}(n_{a}^{2})
, the total effort spent for lines 13–14 is bound by
\mathcal{O}(n_{a}^{3})
.
Likewise for lines 15–16.
Now we bound the total number of steps for lines 17–19.
For each pair
(p,q)
removed from
V
, the cost relative to lines 17–19 is
\mathcal{O}(t_{p}s_{q})
.
Since each pair of
(p,q)
can enter
V
at most once, the total cost is bound by

\sum_{p,q\in Q}t_{p}s_{q}=\sum_{p\in Q}t_{p}\big(\sum_{q\in Q}s_{q}\big)\leq\sum_{p\in Q}(t_{p}m_{a})=m_{a}\sum_{p\in Q}t_{p}\leq m_{a}^{2}.

Hence, the total number of steps to execute the main loop at lines 12–19 is bound by

\mathcal{O}(n_{a}^{3}+m_{a}^{2})
.
We can now conclude that the total number of steps to execute Algorithm
1
is bound by
\mathcal{O}(n_{a}^{3}+m_{a}^{2})
.
Using the previously computed values, we see that a worst case asymptotic time complexity for Algorithm
1
is bounded by
\mathcal{O}(n_{s}^{3}n_{i}^{3}+n_{s}^{2}m_{s}^{4}m_{i}^{2})
.
Since this bound dominates all the preprocessing steps needed to construct
\mathscr{T}
,
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{A}
, the overall worst case time complexity of the
ioco-like
checking procedure is
\mathcal{O}(n_{s}^{3}n_{i}^{3}+n_{s}^{2}m_{s}^{4}m_{i}^{2})
.
∎

In some practical situations we may assume that the number of stack and alphabet symbols can be assumed to be a constant, for specifications and IUTs models that will be considered.
In these situations, the number of transitions of the fault model
\mathscr{T}
can be bounded by
\mathcal{O}(m_{s})
, in the proof of Theorem
5.12
.
As a consequence, the worst case time complexity for Algorithm
1
can be seen to be bounded by
\mathcal{O}(n_{s}^{3}n_{i}^{3}+m_{s}^{2}m_{i}^{2})
.

5.4
A Drink Dispensing Machine

Now we want to apply the results from previous sections in a real-world system, a drink dispensing machine.
because the overall testing procedure is not yet fully implemented, the example has to be somewhat contrived.
In the following subsections we describe the drink machine and its specification IOVPTS model.
Next we construct the fault model for the given specification and, in the sequel, we test some possible IUT models for
ioco-like
conformance.

5.4.1
A Drink Dispensing Machine

In a typical dispensing machine, a customer puts in some money and then order the desire beverage.
After choosing the beverage, the right amount of money will be charged and the machine will dispense the chosen drink.
If the amount of money was in excess, the customer can ask for the balance.
If the amount of money already in the machine is not enough, the customer has to add more money or the customer can decide to get a full refund.
Usually, real machines accept several payment methods such as coins, cash and credit card.
In order to ease our modeling we specify only unit coins as payment method in our models.

The complete IOVPTS specification model
\mathscr{S}\mathscr{S}=\langle S_{\mathscr{S}},S_{in},L_{I},L_{U},\Delta_{\mathscr{S}},T_{\mathscr{S}}\rangle
is depicted in Figure
8
, where

s_{1}

s_{1}

s_{2}

s_{4}

s_{5}

s_{6}

s_{7}

s_{8}

s_{3}

Figure 8:
A drink dispensing machine
\mathscr{S}
.

L_{I}=\{coi,rch,crd,wtr,tea,cof,deb\}
is the set of input events and
L_{U}=\{chg,dwt,dte,dco\}
is the set of output events.
The alphabet
L=L_{I}\cup L_{U}
is partitioned into the push events
L_{c}=\{coi\}
, pop events

L_{r}=\{crd,chg,deb,dwt,dte,dco\}
and simple events
L_{i}=\{rch,wtr,tea,cof\}
.
We have split state
s_{1}
to make the figure clearer.
Recall Remark
3.3
for the notation.
The underlying VPTS is
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{S}}=\langle S_{\mathscr{S}},S_{in},L,\Delta_{\mathscr{S}},T_{\mathscr{S}}\rangle
.

The system starts at state
s_{1}
where the customer can either insert coins into the machine — event labeled
coi
—, request his change — event labeled
rch
—, or ask for a drink, namely, label
wtr
for water, label
tea
for tea, or label
cof
for coffee.
Inserting coins is represented by the self-loop labeled
coi/C_{+}
at state
s_{1}
.
Note that the pushdown stack keeps track of the number of coins inserted into the machine.
At state
s_{1}
the customer can also request a refund, or the remaining change, by activating the
rch
event.
The machine will then return the correct balance via the pop self-loop
crd/C_{-}
at state
s_{3}
.

The customer orders a drink by pushing the bottom for water, tea, or coffee, moving the machine to states
s_{2}
,
s_{4}
and
s_{6}
, respectively.
The price associated to water is one coin, for tea it is two coins and for coffee it is three coins.
When the customer asks for water, the pop transition
dwt/C_{-}
is taken, returning to state
s_{1}
, and the correct charge is applied subtracting one coin from the total amount.
The event
dwt
indicates that water have been dispensed.
However, if not enough coins have been inserted, the transition from state
s_{2}
back to state
s_{1}
is blocked.
The customer can proceed by inserting more coins using the self-loop
coi/C_{+}
at state
s_{2}
.
For simplicity, once the customer has made a commit to order some of the beverages, the machine will wait until enough coins have been inserted to pay for the chosen drink.

The behavior when ordering tea or coffee follows similar paths.

Figure
9
depicts the fault model
\mathscr{T}
that is constructed for the specification
\mathscr{S}
using Lemma
5.9
.

in order keep the figure unclutered.

, collect the label of several transitions, as indicated in the figure caption.

, so that the only symbols we need to check for transitions going into the

.

(x,y)
for all

and all

.

Testing Some Implementations to the Drink Dispensing Machine

depicted in Figure

, and whose fault model
\mathscr{T}

, depicted in Figure

.
Notice that in this IUT we have
(s_{3},chg/C_{-},s_{1})
instead of
(s_{3},chg/\bot,s_{1})
as in the specification model.
This error may cause the customer to receive less than the correct balance for the money inserted in machine.

Consider the sequence of events
\sigma=coi\,coi\,coi\,rch\,crd\,crd\,chg
.
Note that in the IUT
\mathscr{I}_{a}

However, on the specification
\mathscr{S}
, all three coins would be credited.
It is easy to see that
\sigma
leads
\mathscr{T}
to the

state, while

reaches state

.
According to Lemma
5.9
,
\mathscr{I}_{a}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
does not hold.

More formally, write
\sigma=\mu\,chg
, where
\mu=coi\,coi\,coi\,rch\,crd\,crd
.
Then, from the figures, it follows easily that
(s_{1},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\Rightarrow}{(s_{3},C\bot)}\overset{chg}{\Rightarrow}{}
in
\mathscr{I}_{a}
. Since
chg\in L_{U}
, we have
chg\in\text{\,\bf out}((s_{1},\bot)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\sigma)
in

.
However, in
\mathscr{S}
we also have
(s_{1},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\Rightarrow}{(s_{3},C\bot)}
, but then it is not the case that
(s_{3},C\bot)\overset{chg}{\Rightarrow}{}
in
\mathscr{S}
, so that

chg\not\in\text{\,\bf out}((s_{1},\bot)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\sigma)
in
\mathscr{S}
.
From Definition
5.1
, it follows that
\mathscr{I}_{a}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
does not hold.
Observe that, in the product
\mathscr{T}\times\mathscr{I}_{a}
, we get the corresponding run

((s_{1},s_{1}),\bot)\overset{\mu}{\Rightarrow}{((s_{3},s_{3}),C\bot)}\overset{chg}{\Rightarrow}{((\text{fail},s_{1}),\bot)},

as precognized by Lemma
5.9
.

.

.
This allows the machine to subtract any number of extra coins after the customer has orderd a drink, given that more than enough coins have been inserted.

coi/C_{+}

deb/C_{-}
coi/C_{+}
dte/C_{-}
coi/C_{+}

deb/C_{-}
deb/C_{-}
dco/C_{-}
coi/C_{+}
coi/C_{+}
coi/C_{+}
rch
crd/C_{-}
chg/\bot

Figure 13:
An IUT
\mathscr{I}_{d}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
.

Consider the sequence of events
coi\,coi\,coi\,tea\,deb\,deb\,dte\,rch\,chg
, signaling that
the customer initially has inserted three coins, then decided to order tea.
According to the IUT
\mathscr{I}_{d}
, however, when requesting the remaining change the customer get no coins back, and the net effect was that the customer was charged three coins for a cup of tea.
Even in face of that, we show below that
\mathscr{I}_{d}
does conform to the specification
\mathscr{S}
.

Recall the specification
\mathscr{S}
shown in Figure
8
.
We note that
\mathscr{I}_{d}
differs from
\mathscr{S}
only by the extra transition at state
s_{5}
.
Further, for each symbol
x\in L_{U}
and state
s_{i}
, there is at most one transition out of
s_{i}
on
x
, both on
\mathscr{I}_{d}
and on
\mathscr{S}
.
Reasoning more formally,
it is easy to see that for any sequence of events
\sigma
a simple induction on
|\sigma|\geq 0
shows that if we have
(s_{i},\alpha\bot)\in(s_{1},\bot)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\sigma
in
\mathscr{S}
and
(s_{j},\beta\bot)\in(s_{1},\bot)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\sigma
in
\mathscr{I}_{d}
, then
i=j
and
\alpha=\beta
.
According to Definition
5.1
, if
\mathscr{I}_{d}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
did not hold, we would need
\ell\in L_{U}
and a sequence
\sigma
such that
\ell\in\text{\,\bf out}((s_{i},\alpha\bot))
where
(s_{i},\alpha\bot)\in(s_{1},\bot)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\sigma
in
\mathscr{I}_{d}
and
\ell\not\in\text{\,\bf out}((s_{j},\beta\bot))
where
(s_{j},\beta\bot)\in(s_{1},\bot)\text{\,\,\bf after}\,\,\sigma
in
\mathscr{S}
.
Since the transitions of
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{I}_{d}
are identical, except at state
s_{5}
, we conclude that
s_{i}=s_{j}=s_{5}
and
\ell=deb
.
Because
deb\not\in L_{U}
we reached a contradiction, and must conclude that
\mathscr{I}_{d}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
does hold.

We note that
deb\not\in L_{U}
was crucial to the preceding argument.
In fact, if we move
deb
from
L_{I}
to
L_{U}
, then we clearly would get that
\mathscr{I}_{d}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{S}
fails.
This is because the nature of the
ioco-like
relation checks only that the IUT
may not
emit any
output symbol
that was not enabled in the specification, after they both experience any sequence of events that runs on the specification.
On the other hand, the definition of the
ioco-like
relation says nothing about
input symbols
that may be emitted by the IUT and the specification after a common run in both models.

6
Concluding Remarks

Testing conformance of reactive systems implementations is usually a hard task, due to the intrinsic nature of reactive systems, which allows for the asynchronous interactions of messages with an external environment.
In such situations, rigorous approaches capable of generating test suites for these models, are indicated.
Several methods have been developed in order to generate test suites for reactive systems that can be modeled by formal systems which have access only to a finite memory, or a finite number of states.

Here we study a more powerful class of reactive systems, those can make use of a potentially infinite memory, the form of a pushdown stack.
We defined a corresponding more general conformance relation for these systems.
We also showed the existence, and how to generate, test suites that can be used to verify whether this more general conformance relation holds between a given specification and a proposed implementation.
Further, we argued that these test suites can be generated in polynomial time, and that they are also complete, that is,
a given implementation does conform to a given specification if and only if the test suite verdict indicates that this is the case.

For such reactive systems with a pushdown stack, we also defined a conformance relation, called
ioco-like
, that embodies the same idea as the classic
ioco
conformance relation that is used for testing simpler systems, namely, those cannot make use of a potentially infinite memory.
Essentially, these conformance relations say that the implementation can only emit an output signal that is already present in the specification, after a common exchange of symbols has taken place in both models.
We showed that
ioco-like
conformance is a special case of the more general conformance relation we treat in this work.
We also developed, and proved correct, a polynomial time algorithm that can be used to test for
ioco-like
conformance between an implementation and a given specification.
In common practical situations the algorithm exhibits an asymptotic worst case time complexity that can be bounded by
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}+m^{3})
where
n
and
m

are proportional to the cube of the product of the number of states and transitions, respectively, present in the implementation and in the specification.

Appendix A
Constructions and Proofs

In this appendix we present complete constructions and detailed proofs.

A.1
Proof of Proposition
2.5

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{A}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
.
Define the mapping
E:S\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(S)
by

E(s)=\{p\,|\,(s,\varepsilon,\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\bot),n\geq 0\},

that is,
E(s)
is the set of all states that can be reached from
s
through
\varepsilon
-moves.
A simple inductive algorithm guarantees that we can effectively compute
E
.

Consider the VPA
\mathscr{B}=\langle S,Q_{in},A,\Gamma,\mu,F\rangle
where

Q_{in}=\bigcup\limits_{s\in S_{in}}E(s)
, and
the set of transitions
\mu
is obtained from
\rho
by removing all
\varepsilon
-transitions from
\rho
, and then adding to
\mu
all transitions
(r,a,Z,p)
where
a\neq\varepsilon
,
(s,a,Z,q)
is a transition in
\rho
and, by means of
\varepsilon
-transitions alone, we can reach
s
from
r
and
p
from
q
, that is,

\mu=\left[\rho-\{(s,\varepsilon,\sharp,p)\,|\,s,p\in S\}\right]\bigcup\left\{(r,a,Z,p)\,|\,(s,a,Z,q)\in\rho,a\neq\varepsilon,s\in E(r),p\in E(q)\right\}.

Clearly,
\mathscr{B}
has no
\varepsilon
-moves, and has the same number of states as
\mathscr{A}
.

The next two claims will be used to show that
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}
are equivalent VPAs.

Claim 1:

If
\sigma\neq\varepsilon
and
(s,\sigma,\alpha)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta)
, then

(s,\sigma,\alpha)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta)
.

Proof:
Let
(s,\sigma,\alpha)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta)
,
n\geq 1
and
\sigma=a\delta
where
a\in A
.
When
n=1
, we have
\delta=\varepsilon
and there is a transition
(s,a,Z,t)
in
\rho
.
Since
s\in E(s)
and
t\in E(t)
, the construction gives
(s,a,Z,t)
in
\mu
and the result follows immediately.
When
n>1
we may write

(s,\sigma,\alpha)=(s,a\delta,\alpha)\overset{k}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(r,a\delta,\alpha)\overset{}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(q,\delta,\gamma)\overset{m}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta),

where
k\geq 0
and
k+1+m=n
, that is, the first
k
moves of
\mathscr{A}
are
\varepsilon
-moves, while the next one is a move over
a
, through a transition
(r,a,Z,q)\in\rho
, for some
Z\in\Gamma_{\bot}
.
Then,
r\in E(s)
.
If
\delta=\varepsilon
, the last
m
moves of
\mathscr{A}
are also
\varepsilon
-moves, and we get
\gamma=\beta
and
t\in E(q)
.
In this case, the construction gives
(s,a,Z,t)\in\mu
.
Since
\sigma=a\delta=a
we have

(s,\sigma,\alpha)=(s,a,\alpha)\overset{}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\gamma)=(t,\varepsilon,\beta)
, as desired.
Now let
\delta\neq\varepsilon
. Since
q\in E(q)
and we already have
r\in E(s)
, the construction gives
(s,a,Z,q)\in\mu
, so that
(s,\sigma,\alpha)=(s,a\delta,\alpha)\overset{}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(q,\delta,\gamma)
.
But
m<n
, and since
\delta\neq\varepsilon
, the induction gives
(q,\delta,\gamma)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta)
.
Hence,
(s,\sigma,\alpha)\overset{}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\gamma)
and the claim holds.

Now let
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{A})
.
Then,
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\gamma)
where
s_{0}\in S_{in}
and
f\in F
.
When
\sigma=\varepsilon
we get
\gamma=\bot
and
f\in E(s_{0})
.
Since
s_{0}\in S_{in}
we get
f\in Q_{in}
.
Clearly,
(f,\varepsilon,\bot)\overset{0}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\bot)
and so
\sigma=\varepsilon\in L(\mathscr{B})
.
When
\sigma\neq\varepsilon
, Claim 1 gives
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\gamma)
.
Since
s_{0}\in E(s_{0})
, the construction gives
s_{0}\in Q_{in}
.
Hence,
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{B})
.
We may now conclude that
L(\mathscr{A})\subseteq L(\mathscr{B})
.

For the converse, we state

Claim 2:

If
(s,\sigma,\alpha)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta)
with
n\geq 0
, then

(s,\sigma,\alpha)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta)
.

Proof:
When
n=0
the result follows immediately. Let
n>0
.
Since
\mathscr{B}
has no
\varepsilon
-moves, we must have
\sigma=a\delta
with
a\in A
, and

(s,\sigma,\alpha)=(s,a\delta,\alpha)\overset{}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(r,\delta,\gamma)\overset{n-1}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta),

where the first move was through a transition
(s,a,Z,r)\in\mu
.
By the construction, we must have
(p,a,Z,q)\in\rho
with
p\in E(s)
and
r\in E(q)
.
This gives
(s,a\delta,\alpha)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(p,a\delta,\alpha)
and
(q,\delta,\gamma)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(r,\delta,\gamma)
.
Composing we get

(s,\sigma,\alpha)=(s,a\delta,\alpha)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(p,a\delta,\alpha)\overset{}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(q,\delta,\gamma)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(r,\delta,\gamma).

Using the induction hypothesis we get
(r,\delta,\gamma)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta)
.
So,
(s,\sigma,\alpha)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta)
and the claim holds.

Now let
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{B})
, so that
(q_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\gamma)
, with
q_{0}\in Q_{in}
and
t\in F
.
Using Claim 2, we may write
(q_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\gamma)
.
Also, the construction gives some
s_{0}\in S_{in}
with
q_{0}\in E(s_{0})
.
Hence,
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(q_{0},\sigma,\bot)
.
Composing we get
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\gamma)
, that is
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{A})
.
This shows that
L(\mathscr{B})\subseteq L(\mathscr{A})
.

We now have
L(\mathscr{A})=L(\mathscr{B})
, and the proof is complete. ∎

A.2
Proof of Proposition
2.8

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{A}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
.
First we eliminate cycles of
\varepsilon
-transitions, and in a second step we eliminate the remaining
\varepsilon
-transitions.
Let

\displaystyle(s_{1},\varepsilon,\sharp,s_{2}),(s_{2},\varepsilon,\sharp,s_{3}),\ldots,(s_{k-1},\varepsilon,\sharp,s_{k}),(s_{k},\varepsilon,\sharp,s_{k+1}),

(3)

with
s_{k+1}=s_{1}
and
k\geq 1
, be a
\varepsilon
-cycle in
\mathscr{A}
.
We construct the new VPA
\mathscr{B}=\langle Q,Q_{in},A,\Gamma,\mu,E\rangle
, mapping the cycle into a state that occurs in it, say into
s_{1}
.
Let
T=\{(s_{j},\varepsilon,\sharp,s_{j+1}):j=1,\ldots,k\}
,
J=\{s_{j}:j=1,\ldots,k\}
.
Start with
\mathscr{B}=\mathscr{A}
and transform
\mathscr{B}
as follows:

(a)

Transitions: (i) remove
T
from
\mu
; (ii) for all
(p,x,Z,q)\in\rho
with
p\not\in J
and
q\in J

remove
(p,x,Z,q)
from
\mu
and add
(p,x,Z,s_{1})
to
\mu
.

(b)

States: (i) remove
s_{j}
from
Q
and
E
,
j=2,\ldots,k
; (ii) if
F\cap J\neq\emptyset
, then add
s_{1}
to
E
; (iii)
if
S_{in}\cap J\neq\emptyset
, then let
Q_{in}=\{s_{1}\}
.

We have to show that
\mathscr{B}
is equivalent to
\mathscr{A}
, and that
\mathscr{B}
is deterministic.

Claim 1:

\mathscr{B}
is deterministic.

Proof.

Since
\mathscr{A}
is deterministic,
|S_{in}|\leq 1
.
Hence, it is clear that
|Q_{in}|\leq 1
.
We now look at each conditions in Definition
2.6
.

Suppose that
t_{i}=(p,x,Z_{i},q_{i})\in\mu_{c}
,
i=1,2
.
If
t_{1},t_{2}\in\rho
, the determinism of
\mathscr{A}
immediately gives
Z_{1}=Z_{2}
,
q_{1}=q_{2}
and so condition (1) in Definition
2.6
holds.
With no loss of generality assume
t_{1}\not\in\rho
.
Then item (a) of the construction forces
p\not\in J
,
q_{1}=s_{1}
, and
t^{\prime}_{1}=(p,x,Z_{1},s_{j})\in\rho
for some
s_{j}\in J
.
If
t_{2}\in\rho
then, because
\mathscr{A}
is deterministic, condition (1) in Definition
2.6
implies
Z_{1}=Z_{2}
and
q_{2}=s_{j}
, so that
t_{2}=(p,x,Z_{2},s_{j})\in\mu_{c}
.
If
j\geq 2
, we see that
(p,x,Z_{1},s_{j})\in\mu_{c}
contradicts item (a) of the construction.
Hence,
j=1
and then
q_{2}=s_{j}=s_{1}=q_{1}
.
Together with
Z_{1}=Z_{2}
we see that condition (1) in Definition
2.6
holds.
Assume now
t_{2}\not\in\rho
.
We obtain again
q_{2}=s_{1}
and
t^{\prime}_{2}=(p,x,Z_{2},s_{\ell})\in\rho
with
s_{\ell}\in J
.
Now
t^{\prime}_{1},t^{\prime}_{2}\in\rho
and the determinism of
\mathscr{A}
forces
Z_{1}=Z_{2}
.
We now have
q_{1}=s_{1}=q_{2}
,
Z_{1}=Z_{2}
and condition (1) in Definition
2.6
holds again.

Suppose that
t_{i}=(p,x,Z,q_{i})\in\mu_{r}\cup\mu_{i}
,
i=1,2
.
An argument entirely similar to the one in the preceding paragraph shows that condition (2) in Definition
2.6
holds.

Now let
t_{1}=(p,x,Z,q_{1})\in\mu
and
t_{2}=(p,\varepsilon,\sharp,q_{2})\in\mu
, with
x\neq\varepsilon
.
If
t_{1},t_{2}\in\rho
we get an immediate contradiction to the determinism of
\mathscr{A}
.
Assume
t_{1}\not\in\rho
.
As before the construction gives
t^{\prime}_{1}=(p,x,Z,s_{j})\in\rho
for some
s_{j}\in J
.
Since
x\neq\varepsilon
, if
t_{2}\in\rho
we get a contradiction to the determinism of
\mathscr{A}
.
Hence,
t_{2}\not\in\rho
implies, by the construction, that
t^{\prime}_{2}=(p,\varepsilon,\sharp,s_{\ell})\in\rho
for some
s_{\ell}\in J
.
Again,
x\neq\varepsilon
leads to a contradiction to the determinism of
\mathscr{A}
.
Finally, when
t_{1}\in\rho
the determinism of
\mathscr{A}
will force
t_{2}\not\in\rho
, and the construction will give
t^{\prime}_{2}=(p,\varepsilon,\sharp,s_{\ell})\in\rho

for some
s_{\ell}\in J
.
Then,
t_{1},t^{\prime}_{2}\in\rho
contradicts the determinism of
\mathscr{A}
again because
x\neq\varepsilon
.
We conclude that
t_{1}=(p,x,Z,q_{1})\in\mu
and
t_{2}=(p,\varepsilon,\sharp,q_{2})\in\mu
, with
x\neq\varepsilon
, cannot happen and condition (3) in Definition
2.6
also holds.

Next, we want to argue for language equivalence. First we show that
\mathscr{A}
can imitate runs of
\mathscr{B}
.

Claim 2:

If
(q,\sigma,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot)
then we also have
(q,\sigma,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot)
.

Proof:
Write
(q,\sigma,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot)
, where
n\geq 0
.
If all transitions used in the run over
\mathscr{B}
are in
\rho
, there is nothing to prove.
Let
t=(u,x,Z,v)
, with
x\in A
and
Z\in\Gamma\cup\{\varepsilon\}
, be the first transition not in
\rho
used in the run over
\mathscr{B}
.
By item (a) of the construction we get
v=s_{1}
and
t^{\prime}=(u,x,Z,s_{j})\in\rho
, where
s_{j}\in J
.
Then,
\sigma=\sigma_{1}x\sigma_{2}
and we may write

(q,\sigma_{1}x\sigma_{2},\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}{(u,x\sigma_{2},\beta\bot)}\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(s_{1},\sigma_{2},\gamma\bot)\overset{k}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot)
, with
0\leq k<n
.
Using the transitions in the
\varepsilon
-cycle at Eq.(
3
) we get
(s_{j},\sigma_{2},\gamma\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}{(s_{1},\sigma_{2},\gamma\bot)}
.
Thus, using
t^{\prime}
we have

(q,\sigma_{1}x\sigma_{2},\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}{(u,x\sigma_{2},\beta\bot)}\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{j},\sigma_{2},\gamma\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{1},\sigma_{2},\gamma\bot)\overset{k}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot).

Inductively, we have
(s_{1},\sigma_{2},\gamma\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot)
.
Putting it together, we now have
(q,\sigma_{1}x\sigma_{2},\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot)
.

Next, we want to show that if we have a run of
\mathscr{A}
starting at
S_{in}
and ending in
F
, then we can extend the run to start at
Q_{in}
and end in
E
.

Claim 3:

If
(r,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
with
r\in S_{in}
,
f\in F
, then
(u,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(v,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
with
u\in Q_{in}
,
v\in E
.

Proof.

Assume
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{A})
so that
(r,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
with
r\in S_{in}
,
f\in F
.
First, we want to show that we also have
(u,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(v,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
with
u\in Q_{in}
and
v\in E
.
If
r\in Q_{in}
let
u=r
.
If
r\not\in Q_{in}
, because
|S_{in}|\leq 1
item (b) of the construction implies that
Q_{in}=\{s_{1}\}
and
r=s_{\ell}\in S_{in}
for some
s_{\ell}\in J
.
Since
\mathscr{A}
is deterministic, condition (3) in Definition
2.6
says that
(s_{i},\varepsilon,\sharp,s_{i+1})
is the unique transition out of
s_{i}
, for all
s_{i}\in J
.
Let
u=s_{1}
.
Hence, we must have
(r,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(u,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
with
u\in Q_{in}
.
If
f\in E
, let
v=f
.
If
f\not\in E
, item (b) of the construction says that
f=s_{\ell}
, for some
s_{\ell}\in J
, and
s_{1}\in E
.
Let
v=s_{1}
.
We now have
(u,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\sigma,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(v,\sigma,\alpha\bot)
with
v\in E
.
We can now assume
(r,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
with
r\in Q_{in}
,
f\in E
.

Now we are ready for language equivalence.

Claim 4:

L(\mathscr{A})=L(\mathscr{B})
.

Proof.

Now let
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{B})
, so that we have
(q,\sigma,\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
with
q\in Q_{in}
,
p\in E
,
n\geq 0
.
Using Claim 2, we can write
(q,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
If
q\not\in S_{in}
then by item (b) of the construction we must have
s_{\ell}\in S_{in}
for some
s_{\ell}\in J
and
q=s_{1}
.
From the
\varepsilon
-cycle at Eq. (
3
) we have
(s_{\ell},\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{1},\sigma,\bot)=(q,\sigma,\bot)
, so that
(s_{\ell},\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
Likewise, if
p\not\in F
then by item (b) again we must have
p=s_{1}
and
s_{i}\in F
for some
s_{i}\in J
.
Again, the
\varepsilon
-cycle gives
(p,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)=(s_{1},\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{i},\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
Composing, we obtain
(s_{\ell},\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{i},\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
with
s_{\ell}\in S_{in}
and
s_{i}\in F
.
Thus we always have
(u,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(v,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
, with
u\in S_{in}
and
v\in F
.
Hence,
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{A})
.

Now let
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{A})
.
Using Claim 3, we can write
(r,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
with
r\in Q_{in}
,
f\in E
.
If no state
s_{j}\in J
occurs in this run, then all transitions are also in
\mu
, and we get
(r,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
Next, let
\sigma=\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}
with
(r,\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2},\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{j},\sigma_{2},\beta\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
, where this is the first occurrence of a state of
J
in the run over
\mathscr{A}
.
Invoking condition (3) in Definition
2.6
again, we must have
f=s_{i}\in J
,
\sigma_{2}=\varepsilon
and
\beta=\alpha
.
Since
f\in E
, we must have
f=s_{1}\in E
.
We now have
\sigma=\sigma_{1}
and
(r,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{1},\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
,
r\in Q_{in}
,
s_{1}\in E
.
When
n=0
we get
(r,\sigma,\bot)\overset{0}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(s_{1},\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
and we are done.
Else, we must have
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}x
with
x\in\Gamma\cup\{\varepsilon\}
and
(r,\sigma^{\prime}x,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(t,x,\gamma\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{1},\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
with a transition
(t,x,Z,s_{1})\in\rho
used in the last move.
Because
s_{j}
is the first occurrence of a state in
J
we get
t\not\in J
, and all transitions in
(r,\sigma^{\prime}x,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(t,x,\gamma\bot)
are in
\mu
.
Hence,
(r,\sigma^{\prime}x,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(t,x,\gamma\bot)
.
Item (a) of the construction readily gives
(t,x,Z,s_{1})\in\mu
.
Thus,
(r,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(t,x,\gamma\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(s_{1},\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
,
r\in Q_{in}
,
s_{1}\in E
.
We conclude that
L(\mathscr{A})\subseteq L(\mathscr{B})
.

At this point we know how to remove an
\varepsilon
-cycle from
\mathscr{A}
, while maintain language equivalence and determinism.
Thus, we can repeat the construction for all
\varepsilon
-cycles in
\mathscr{A}
, so that we can now assume that there is no
\varepsilon
-cycle in
\mathscr{A}
.
As a final step, we show how to remove all remaining
\varepsilon
-transitions from
\mathscr{A}
.

Let
\mathscr{A}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
be deterministic and with no
\varepsilon
-cycles.
Let
t=(p,\varepsilon,\sharp,q)\in\rho
.
Since
\mathscr{A}
has no
\varepsilon
-cycles, we can assume that for all transitions
(q,x,Z,r)\in\rho
we have
x\neq\varepsilon
.
Start with
\mathscr{B}=\mathscr{A}
and transform
\mathscr{B}
as follows.

(c)

Transitions: (i)
\mu=\rho-\{t\}
; (ii) for all
(q,a,Z,r)\in\rho
, add
(p,a,Z,r)
to
\mu
.

(d)

States: (i) if
p\in S_{in}
,
p\not\in F
, let
Q_{in}=\{q\}
; (ii) if
q\in F
, let
E=F\cup\{p\}
.

We still have determinism.

Claim 5:

\mathscr{B}
is deterministic.

Proof.

We want to show that
\mathscr{B}
is also deterministic.
It is clear that
|Q_{in}|\leq 1
because
\mathscr{A}
is deterministic.
Assume that
\mathscr{B}
is not deterministic.
Since
\mathscr{A}
is already deterministic and the only transitions added to
\mu
are of the form
(p,a,Z,r)
, we see that the only possibility for
\mathscr{B}
not deterministic is that we have two transitions
t_{i}=(p,a_{i},Z_{i},r_{i})\in\mu
,
i=1,2
in violation Definition
2.6
.
Since
t\in\rho
and
\mathscr{A}
is deterministic, according to condition (3) of Definition
2.6
, this is the
only
transition out of
p
in
\rho
.
Moreover
t
was removed from
\rho
according to item (c) of the construction.
Therefore,
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
are new transitions added to
\mu
by the construction.
Hence, we must have transitions
t^{\prime}_{i}=(q,a_{i},Z_{i},r_{i})\in\rho
,
i=1,2
.
But these two transitions in
\rho
also violate Definition
2.6
, and so they contradict the determinism of
\mathscr{A}
.
We conclude that
\mathscr{B}
must be deterministic too.

We complete the proof arguing for language equivalence.
Again, we must first relate runs in
\mathscr{A}
to runs in
\mathscr{B}
, and vice-versa.

Claim 6:

If
(s,\sigma,\beta\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
, then we also have
(s,\sigma,\beta\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.

Proof.

First, we show that for any run in
\mathscr{B}
there exists a similar run in
\mathscr{A}
.
Let
(s,\sigma,\beta\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
If
n=0
we can immediately write
(s,\sigma,\beta\bot)\overset{0}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
Now let
x\in A_{\varepsilon}
,
\sigma=x\sigma^{\prime}
and write

\displaystyle(s,x\sigma^{\prime},\beta\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(r,\sigma^{\prime},\gamma\bot)\overset{n-1}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot),

(4)

with
t^{\prime}=(s,x,Z,r)
the first transition used in this run.
Inductively,
(r,\sigma^{\prime},\gamma\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
If
t^{\prime}\in\rho
, we have
(s,x\sigma^{\prime},\beta\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(r,\sigma^{\prime},\gamma\bot)
, so that

(s,\sigma,\beta\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
If
t^{\prime}\not\in\rho
, the construction gives
s=p
,
t^{\prime}=(p,x,Z,r)
and
(q,x,Z,r)\in\rho
.
Then, using
t=(p,\varepsilon,\sharp,q)
we have

(s,x\sigma^{\prime},\beta\bot)=(p,x\sigma^{\prime},\beta\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(q,x\sigma^{\prime},\beta\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(r,\sigma^{\prime},\gamma\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot).

Now we examine how
\mathscr{B}
can imitate runs in
\mathscr{A}
.

Claim 7:

Let
(s_{1},\sigma,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{2},\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot)
with
n\geq 0
.
We show that
(s_{1},\sigma,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(s^{\prime}_{2},\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot)
where
s^{\prime}_{2}=p
if the last transition in the run from
\mathscr{A}
was
t
, else
s_{2}^{\prime}=s_{2}
.

Proof.

If
n=0
the result is immediate, with
s^{\prime}_{2}=s_{2}
.
Next, let
n\geq 1
,
x\in A_{\varepsilon}
,
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}x
and

\displaystyle(s_{1},\sigma^{\prime}x,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{n-1}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{3},x,\alpha_{3}\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{2},\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot),

(5)

where
t^{\prime}=(s_{3},x,Z,s_{2})
is the transition used in the last move in Eq. (
5
).

The first simple case is when
t^{\prime}=t
.
Then,
s_{3}=p
,
x=\varepsilon
,
s_{2}=q
and
\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{2}
.
Since
s_{3}=p
, the last transition in
(s_{1},\sigma x,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{n-1}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{3},x,\alpha_{3}\bot)
cannot be
t
, otherwise we would have
s_{3}=p=q
and then
t
would be a simple
\varepsilon
-cycle in
\mathscr{A}
, a contradiction.
Inductively from Eq. (
5
) we
can now write
(s_{1},\sigma^{\prime}x,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(s_{3},x,\alpha_{3}\bot)=(p,\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot)
.
Picking
s^{\prime}_{2}=p
, we are done with this case.

Let now that
t^{\prime}\neq t
.
This gives
t^{\prime}=(s_{3},x,Z,s_{2})\in\mathscr{B}
.
If
n-1=0
in Eq. (
5
) we get
s_{1}=s_{3}
,
\sigma^{\prime}=\varepsilon
,
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{3}
.
The last move in Eq. (
5
) gives
(s_{3},x,\alpha_{3}\bot)=(s_{1},x,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{2},\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot)
.
Since
t^{\prime}\in\mathscr{B}
we also have
(s_{1},x,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(s_{2},\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot)
, and the result follows because
t^{\prime}\neq t
.
We now proceed under the hypothesis that
n-1>0
.
Inductively, from Eq. (
5
) we get

(s_{1},\sigma^{\prime}x,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(s_{3}^{\prime},x,\alpha_{3}\bot)
, where
s^{\prime}_{3}=p
if the last transition, say
t^{\prime\prime}
, in
(s_{1},\sigma^{\prime}x,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{n-1}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{3},x,\alpha_{3}\bot)
was
t
, otherwise we must have
s^{\prime}_{3}=s_{3}
.
If
t^{\prime\prime}\neq t
, then
t^{\prime}\in\mathscr{B}
and
s^{\prime}_{3}=s_{3}
give
(s^{\prime}_{3},x,\alpha_{3}\bot)=(s_{3},x,\alpha_{3}\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(s_{2},\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot)
.
Thus,
(s_{1},\sigma^{\prime}x,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(s_{2},\varepsilon,\alpha_{3}\bot)
.
Picking
s^{\prime}_{2}=s_{2}
and recalling that
t^{\prime}
is not
t
, we have the desired result.
Lastly, let
t^{\prime\prime}=t
.

We can rewrite the first
n-1
moves in Eq. (
5
) thus

\displaystyle(s_{1},\sigma^{\prime},\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{n-2}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha_{3}\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(q,\varepsilon,\alpha_{3}\bot)=(s_{3},\varepsilon,\alpha_{3}\bot),

(6)

so that
q=s_{3}
.
Inductively, we get

(s_{1},\sigma^{\prime},\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha_{3}\bot)
, so that we also have
(s_{1},\sigma^{\prime}x,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(p,x,\alpha_{3}\bot)
.
We now have
t^{\prime}=(s_{3},x,Z,s_{2})=(q,x,Z,s_{2})
in
\mathscr{A}
.
By item (c) of the construction, we must have
(p,x,Z,s_{2})
in
\mathscr{B}
.
Since
(s_{3},x,\alpha_{3}\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{2},\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot)
using
t^{\prime}
, we also get

(p,x,\alpha_{3}\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(s_{2},\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot)
.
Composing, we obtain
(s_{1},\sigma^{\prime}x,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(s_{2},\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot)
.
Picking
s^{\prime}_{2}=s_{2}
and remembering that we assumed
t^{\prime}\neq t
, we have the result again.

The first half of language equivalence now follows.

Claim 8:

L(\mathscr{B})\subseteq L(\mathscr{A})
.

Proof.

Let
(s,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
with

s\in Q_{in}
and
f\in E
.
Claim 6 gives

\displaystyle(s,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot).

(7)

If
f\in F
, let
f^{\prime}=f
and we get
(s,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f^{\prime},\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
with
f^{\prime}\in F
.
If
f\not\in F
, item (d) of the construction says that
f=p
and
q\in F
. Picking
f^{\prime}=q
we have
f^{\prime}\in F
. Using
t
again, we have

(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)=(p,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(q,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)=(f^{\prime},\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
with
f^{\prime}\in F
.
So, we can assume that
f\in F
in Eq. (
7
).
If
s\in S_{in}
, then Eq. (
7
) says that
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{A})
, as desired.
Now assume
s\not\in S_{in}
.
Since
s\in Q_{in}
, item (d) of the construction implies that
s=q
,
p\in S_{in}
.
Using transition
t
again, we now have

(p,\sigma,\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(q,\sigma,\bot)=(s,\sigma,\bot)
.
Composing with Eq. (
7
) we get
(p,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
Because
p\in S_{in}
and
f\in F
, we see that
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{A})
.

The next claim completes the proof.

Claim 9:
