L(\mathscr{A})\subseteq L(\mathscr{B})
.

Proof.

Let
s\in S_{in}
,
f\in F
,
n\geq 0
, and
(s,\sigma,\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
Assume that
s\not\in Q_{in}
.
Since
s\in S_{in}
, then item (d) of the construction gives
Q_{in}=\{q\}
,
p\in S_{in}
and
p\not\in F
.
Since
\mathscr{A}
is deterministic, we get
S_{in}=\{p\}=\{s\}
, so that
p=s
.
Hence,
s\not\in F
and then
s\neq f
.
Thus,
n\geq 1
.
Since
p=s
and
\mathscr{A}
is deterministic, we see that
t
is the first transition in the run
(s,\sigma,\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
Thus, we can write
(s,\sigma,\bot)=(p,\sigma,\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(q,\sigma,\bot)\overset{n-1}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
, with
q\in Q_{in}
and
n-1\geq 0
.
We can, thus, take
s\in Q_{in}
in the run
(s,\sigma,\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.

If
n=0
we get
\sigma=\varepsilon
,
\alpha=\varepsilon
and
s=f
.
We can then write
(s,\sigma,\bot)=(s,\varepsilon,\bot)\overset{0}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\bot)
.
Because
F\subseteq E
we get
f\in E
.
Since
s\in Q_{in}
we get
\sigma=\varepsilon\in L(\mathscr{B})
.

We proceed now with
s\in Q_{in}
,
f\in E
,
n\geq 1
and
(s,\sigma,\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(f,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
Use Claim 7 to write

(s,\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(f^{\prime},\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
,
where
f^{\prime}=p
if
t
was the last transition in the run over
\mathscr{A}
, otherwise we have
f^{\prime}=f
.
If
f^{\prime}=f
, we get
f^{\prime}\in E
and then
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{B})
.
Assume now that
f^{\prime}=p
and
t
was the last transition in the run over
\mathscr{A}
.
This gives
q=f
, and then item (d) of the construction says that
p\in E
.
Thus,
f^{\prime}\in E
and we get again
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{B})
.

∎

A.3
Proof of Proposition
2.11

Proof.

Let
((s,q),\sigma,\gamma_{0})\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}((p,r),\varepsilon,\gamma_{n})
, for some
n\geq 0
, and where

\gamma_{0}=(X_{1},Y_{1})\ldots(X_{i},Y_{i})\bot
and
\gamma_{n}=(Z_{1},W_{1})\ldots(Z_{k},W_{k})\bot
.
When
n=0
the result is immediate.
Proceeding inductively, let
n\geq 1
,
\sigma=\delta a
with
a\in A\cup\{\varepsilon\}
, and

\displaystyle((s,q),\delta a,\gamma_{0})\overset{n-1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}((u,v),a,\gamma_{n-1})\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}((p,r),\varepsilon,\gamma_{n}),

(8)

where
\gamma_{n-1}=(U_{1},V_{1})\ldots(U_{j},V_{j})\bot
, for some
j\geq 0
.
In order to ease the notation, let
\alpha_{0}=X_{1}\ldots X_{i}\bot
,
\alpha_{n-1}=U_{1}\ldots U_{j}\bot
,
\alpha_{n}=Z_{1}\ldots Z_{k}\bot
,
\beta_{0}=Y_{1}\ldots Y_{i}\bot
,
\beta_{n-1}=V_{1}\ldots V_{j}\bot
, and
\beta_{n}=W_{1}\ldots W_{k}\bot
.
Then, using the induction hypothesis we get

\displaystyle(s,\delta,\alpha_{0})\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(u,\varepsilon,\alpha_{n-1}),\quad(q,\delta,\beta_{0})\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(v,\varepsilon,\beta_{n-1}).

(9)

Let
((u,v),a,Z,(p,r))
be the transition used in the last
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}
move.
From Definition
2.9
, there are two possibilities:

Case 1:

a\neq\varepsilon
,
(u,a,X,p)\in\rho
,
(v,a,Y,r)\in\mu
, and either
Z=(X,Y)
, or
Z=X=Y\in\{\bot,\sharp\}
.

If
a\in A_{c}
, then from Eq. (
8
) we get
Z\not\in\{\bot,\sharp\}
, so that we must have
Z=(X,Y)
.
With
a\in A_{c}
, Eq (
8
) gives
\gamma_{n}=(X,Y)\gamma_{n-1}=(X,Y)(U_{1},V_{1})\cdots(U_{j},V_{j})\bot
.
But now, with
(u,a,X,p)\in\rho
and Eq. (
9
) we also get

(u,a,\alpha_{n-1})\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,X\alpha_{n-1})
.
Using Eq. (
9
) and composing, we get
(s,\sigma,\alpha_{0})\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,X\alpha_{n-1})
, where
X\alpha_{n-1}=XU_{1}\cdots U_{j}\bot
.
Likewise, we obtain
(q,\sigma,\beta_{0})\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,Y\beta_{n-1})
, with

Y\beta_{n-1}=YV_{1}\cdots V_{j}\bot
, as needed.

When
a\in A_{r}
, given that
((u,v),a,Z,(p,r))
was the last transition used Eq. (
8
) with

\gamma_{n-1}=(U_{1},V_{1})\ldots(U_{j},V_{j})\bot
, we get either (i)
j\geq 1
,
Z=(U_{1},V_{1})
and
\gamma_{n}=(U_{2},V_{2})\cdots(U_{j},V_{j})\bot
, or (ii)
j=0
and
Z=\bot
and
\gamma_{n}=\bot
.
Since
Z=(X,Y)
, the first case gives
X=U_{1}
and
Y=V_{1}
.
We can now repeat the argument above when
a\in A_{c}
and reach the desired result.
In the second case,
Z=\bot
forces
Z=X=Y=\bot
.
Also,
\alpha_{n-1}=U_{1}\cdots U_{j}\bot
reduces to
\alpha_{n-1}=\bot
.
Since
a\in A_{r}
and
(u,a,X,p)\in\rho
, we can write
(u,a,\alpha_{n-1})\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\bot)
.
Using Eq. (
9
) and composing, we get
(s,\sigma,\alpha_{0})\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\bot)
.
Since
\gamma_{n}=\bot
we have the desired result for
\mathscr{S}
.
A similar reasoning also gives
(q,\sigma,\beta_{0})\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\bot)
, as needed.

If
a\in A_{i}
, since
((u,v),a,Z,(p,r))
was the last transition used Eq. (
8
), we get
Z=\sharp
and
\gamma_{n}=\gamma_{n-1}=(U_{1},V_{1})\cdots(U_{j},V_{j})\bot
.
Since
Z\in\{\bot,\sharp\}
, we must also have
Z=X=Y=\sharp
.
Thus,
(u,a,\sharp,p)\in\rho
.
Using Eq. (
9
) and composing, we get
(s,\sigma,\alpha_{0})\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha_{n-1})
.
Since
\alpha_{n-1}=U_{1}\ldots U_{j}\bot
, we get the desired result for
\mathscr{S}
.
By a similar reasoning we also get
(q,\sigma,\beta_{0})\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\beta_{n-1})
, completing this case.

Case 2:

a=\varepsilon
,
Z=\sharp
with either
u=p
and
(v,\varepsilon,\sharp,r)\in\mu
, or
v=r
and
(u,\varepsilon,\sharp,p)\in\rho
.
We look at the first case, the other being entirely similar.
Since
((u,v),\varepsilon,\sharp,(u,r))
was the transition used in Eq. (
8
) we get
\gamma_{n-1}=\gamma_{n}=(U_{1},V_{1})\cdots(U_{j},V_{j})\bot
.
From Eq. (
9
) and
u=p
, we know that
(s,\sigma,\alpha_{0})\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha_{n-1})
,
and because
\alpha_{n-1}=U_{1}\ldots U_{j}\bot
we get the desired result for
\mathscr{S}
.
Since
(v,\varepsilon,\sharp,r)\in\mu
, from Eq. (
9
) we obtain

(q,\sigma,\beta_{0})\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\beta_{n-1})
, and we have the result also for
\mathscr{Q}
because
\beta_{n-1}=V_{1}\ldots V_{j}\bot
.
This case is now complete.

Next, we look at the converse.
Let
(s,\sigma,\alpha_{0})\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha_{n})
and

(q,\sigma,\beta_{0})\overset{m}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\beta_{m})
, with
n,m\geq 0
, where
\alpha_{0}=X_{1}\ldots X_{i}\bot
,
\beta_{0}=Y_{1}\ldots Y_{i}\bot
,
\alpha_{n}=Z_{1}\ldots Z_{k}\bot
and
\beta_{m}=W_{1}\ldots W_{k}\bot
, for some
i,k\geq 0
.
Write
\gamma_{0}=(X_{1},Y_{1})\ldots(X_{i},Y_{i})\bot
and
\gamma=(Z_{1},W_{1})\ldots(Z_{k},W_{k})\bot
.

When
n+m=0
we get
m=0
and
n=0
, so that the result is immediate.

With no loss, let
n\geq 1
.
Then,
\sigma=a\delta
and a transition
(s,a,Z,t)\in\rho
used in the first step in
\mathscr{S}
. We now have

(s,a\delta,\alpha_{0})\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(t,\delta,\alpha_{1})\overset{n-1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha_{n}),\quad(q,\sigma,\beta_{0})\overset{m}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(q,\varepsilon,\beta_{m}).

(10)

We first look at the case when
a=\varepsilon
.
Then,
\sigma=\delta
,
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{1}
and Definition
2.3
implies
Z=\sharp
.
Because
(s,\varepsilon,\sharp,t)\in\rho
, Definition
2.9
item (2) says
((s,q),\varepsilon,\sharp,(t,q))
is in
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle((s,q),\sigma,\gamma_{0})\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}((t,q),\delta,\gamma_{0}).

(11)

Now we have
(t,\delta,\alpha_{0})\overset{n-1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha_{n})
and
(q,\delta,\beta_{0})\overset{m}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\beta_{m})
.
Since
n-1+m<n+m
, inductively, we may write

((t,q),\delta,\gamma_{0})\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}((p,r),\varepsilon,\gamma)
.
From Eq. (
11
) we get
((s,q),\sigma,\gamma_{0})\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}((p,r),\varepsilon,\gamma)
.
Recalling the definitions of
\alpha_{0}
,
\beta_{0}
,
\alpha_{n}
,
\beta_{n}
, and of
\gamma_{0}
,
\gamma
, we see that the result holds in this case.

We now turn to the case
a\neq\varepsilon
.
If
m=0
, from
(q,\sigma,\beta_{0})\overset{m}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\beta_{m})
we obtain
\sigma=\varepsilon
.
But then
\sigma=a\delta
gives
a=\varepsilon
, a contradiction.
Thus,
m\geq 1
.
This gives

(q,a\delta,\beta_{0})\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(u,\delta,\beta_{1})\overset{m-1}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\beta_{m}),

(12)

and we must have a transition
(q,a,W,u)\in\mu
which was used in the first step.
There are three cases.

Case 3:

a\in A_{i}
.
Then, Eqs. (
10
) and (
12
) imply
(s,a,\sharp,t)\in\rho
,
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{1}
and
(q,a,\sharp,u)\in\mu
,
\beta_{0}=\beta_{1}
.
From the same equations, we now obtain

(t,\delta,\alpha_{0})\overset{n-1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha_{n})
and
(u,\delta,\beta_{0})\overset{n-1}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\beta_{m})
.
The induction hypothesis now implies
((t,u),\delta,\gamma_{0})\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}((p,r),\varepsilon,\gamma)
.
Since
(s,a,\sharp,t)\in\rho
and
(q,a,\sharp,u)\in\mu
and
a\neq\varepsilon
, Definition
2.9
item (1)
gives
((s,q),a,\sharp,(t,u))
in
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}
, and we may write
((s,q),a\delta,\gamma_{0})\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}((t,q),\delta,\gamma_{0})
.
Composing, we get the result.

Case 4:

a\in A_{c}
.
From Eq. (
10
) we get
(s,a,X,t)\in\rho
,
(s,a\delta,\alpha_{0})\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(t,\delta,X\alpha_{0})
for some
X\in\Gamma
.
And from Eq. (
12
) we get
(q,a,Y,u)\in\mu
,
(q,a\delta,\beta_{0})\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(u,\delta,Y\beta_{0})
for some
Y\in\Delta
.

Definition
2.9
item (1) says that
((s,q),a,(X,Y),(t,u))
is in
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}
, and we may write

((s,q),a\delta,\gamma_{0})\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}((t,u),\delta,(X,Y)\gamma_{0})
.
Inductively, Eqs. (
10
) and (
12
) also imply
((t,u),\delta,(X,Y)\gamma_{0})\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}((p,r),\varepsilon,\gamma)
.
Composing again, the result follows.

Case 5:

a\in A_{r}
, and recall that
\alpha_{0}=X_{1}\ldots X_{i}\bot
,
\beta_{0}=Y_{1}\ldots Y_{i}\bot
, for some
i\geq 0
.
When
i\geq 1
, the reasoning is very similar to Case 4.

Let
i=0
.
From Eq. (
10
) we see that
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{1}=\bot
,
(s,a,\bot,t)\in\rho
and
(t,\delta,\bot)\overset{n-1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha_{n})
.
Likewise, From Eq. (
12
) gives
\beta_{0}=\beta_{1}=\bot
,
(q,a,\bot,u)\in\mu
and
(u,\delta,\bot)\overset{m-1}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\beta_{m})
.
Inductively, we have

((t,u),\delta,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}((p,r),\varepsilon,\gamma)
.
Because
(s,a,\bot,t)\in\rho
,
(q,a,\bot,u)\in\mu
,
Definition
2.9
item (1) says that
((s,q),a,\bot,(t,u))

is in
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}
.
Since
\sigma=a\delta
, composing we get
((s,q),\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}((p,r),\varepsilon,\gamma)
, which is the desired result.

The proof is now complete.
∎

A.4
Proof of Proposition
2.12

Proof.

A simple induction on
n=|\sigma|
.
When
n=0
we get
\sigma=\varepsilon
.
According to Definition
2.3
,
\varepsilon
-moves do not change the stack, and so we get
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}
and
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}
, and the result follows.

Assume
n\geq 1
and
\sigma=\delta a
, where
a\in A
and
|\delta|=n-1
.
Then computations can be separated thus

\displaystyle(s_{1},\delta a,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{3},a,\alpha_{3}\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{4},\varepsilon,\alpha_{4}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{2},\varepsilon,\alpha_{2}\bot)

\displaystyle(q_{1},\delta a,\beta_{1}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(q_{3},a,\beta_{3}\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(q_{4},\varepsilon,\beta_{4}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(q_{2},\varepsilon,\beta_{2}\bot).

Clearly, we get
(s_{1},\delta,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\mapsto}}(s_{3},\varepsilon,\alpha_{3}\bot)
and
(q_{1},\delta,\beta_{1}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\mapsto}}(q_{3},\varepsilon,\beta_{3}\bot)
.
The induction hypothesis implies
|\alpha_{3}|=|\beta_{3}|
.
The proof will be complete if we can show that
|\alpha_{4}|=|\beta_{4}|
, because from these configurations onward we have only
\varepsilon
-moves, which would imply that
|\alpha_{2}|=|\beta_{2}|
.

Let
(s_{3},a,Z,s_{4})
and
(q_{3},a,W,q_{4})
be the
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}
transitions, respectively, used in the one-step
computations, as indicated above.
From Definition
2.3
we have three simple cases: (i) when
a\in A_{i}\cup\{\varepsilon\}
we get
\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{4}
and
\beta_{3}=\beta_{4}
;
(ii) when
a\in A_{c}
we get
\alpha_{4}=Z\alpha_{3}
and
\beta_{4}=W\beta_{3}
;
and (iii) when
a\in A_{r}
, since
|\alpha_{3}|=|\beta_{3}|
, we get either
\alpha_{3}\neq\varepsilon\neq\beta_{3}
and then
\alpha_{3}=Z\alpha_{4}
and
\beta_{3}=W\beta_{4}
, or

\alpha_{3}=\varepsilon=\beta_{3}
and then
\alpha_{4}=\varepsilon=\beta_{4}
.
In any case we see that
|\alpha_{4}|=|\beta_{4}|
, because we already have
|\alpha_{3}|=|\beta_{3}|
.
∎

A.5
Proof of Proposition
2.16

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
and
\mathscr{Q}=\langle Q,Q_{in},A,\Delta,\mu,G\rangle
.
Using Proposition
2.15
we can assume that
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
are non-blocking VPAs with
n+1
and
m+1
states, respectively.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be the product of
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
as in Definition
2.9
, except that we redefine the final states of
\mathscr{P}
as
(F\times Q)\cup(S\times G)
.
Clearly,
\mathscr{P}
has
(n+1)(m+1)
states.

We now argue that
\mathscr{P}
is also a non-blocking VPA.
Let
\sigma\in A^{\star}
,
(s,q)\in S\times Q
, and let
\gamma=(Z_{1},W_{1})\ldots(Z_{k},W_{k})\in(\Gamma\times\Delta)^{\star}
.
Since
\mathscr{S}
is a non-blocking VPA, we get
n\geq 0
,
p\in S
,
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
such that

(s,\sigma,Z_{1}\ldots Z_{k}\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
Likewise,
(q,\sigma,W_{1}\ldots W_{k}\bot)\overset{m}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
, for some

m\geq 0
,
r\in Q
,
\beta\in\Delta^{\star}
.
Applying Proposition
2.12
we get
|\alpha|=|\beta|
, and then using Proposition
2.11
we have

((s,q),\sigma,\gamma\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{P}}{\mapsto}}((p,r),\varepsilon,\delta\bot)
where
\gamma=(Z_{1},W_{1})\ldots(Z_{k},W_{k})
and
\delta\in(\Gamma\times\Delta)^{\star}
.
This shows that
\mathscr{P}
is a non-blocking VPA.

Now suppose that
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{P})
, that is
((s_{0},q_{0}),\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{P}}{\mapsto}}((p,r),\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
, where
(s_{0},q_{0})\in S_{in}\times Q_{in}
,
(p,r)\in(F\times Q)\cup(S\times G)
, and
\alpha\in(\Gamma\times\Delta)^{\star}
.
Take the case when
(p,r)\in(F\times Q)
.
We get
p\in F
and
s_{0}\in S_{in}
.
Using Proposition
2.11
we can also write
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
, for some
\beta\in\Gamma^{\star}
.
This shows that
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{S})
.
By a similar reasoning, when
(p,r)\in(S\times G)
we get
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{Q})
.
Thus,
L(\mathscr{P})\subseteq L(\mathscr{S})\cup L(\mathscr{Q})
.

Now let
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{S})\cup L(\mathscr{Q})
.
Take the case
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{S})
, the case
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{Q})
being similar.
Then we must have
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
for some
n\geq 0
, some
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
, and some
p\in F
.
Pick any
q_{0}\in Q_{in}
.
Since
\mathscr{Q}
is a non-blocking VPA, Definition
2.14
gives some
r\in Q
and some
\beta\in\Delta^{\star}
such that
(q_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{m}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
for some
m\geq 0
, some
r\in Q
and some
\beta\in\Delta^{\star}
.
Using Proposition
2.12
we conclude that
|\alpha|=|\beta|
.
The only-if part of Proposition
2.11
now yields

((s_{0},q_{0}),\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{P}}{\mapsto}}((p,r),\varepsilon,\gamma\bot)
, where
\gamma\in(\Gamma\times\Delta)^{\star}
.
Clearly,
(s_{0},q_{0})\in S_{in}\times Q_{in}
is an initial state of
\mathscr{P}
and
(p,r)\in(F\times Q)\cup(S\times G)
is a final state of
\mathscr{P}
.
Hence
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{P})
.
Thus
L(\mathscr{S})\cup L(\mathscr{Q})\subseteq L(\mathscr{P})
, and so
L(\mathscr{S})\cup L(\mathscr{Q})=L(\mathscr{P})
.

Applying Proposition
2.10
(2) we see that when
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
are deterministic, then
\mathscr{P}
is also deterministic and has no
\varepsilon
-moves.

The proof is now complete.
∎

A.6
Proof of Proposition
2.17

Proof.

Applying Propositions
2.8
and
2.15
, we can assume that
\mathscr{S}
is a non-blocking and deterministic VPA with
n+1
states and no
\varepsilon
-moves.

Let
\mathscr{Q}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,S-F\rangle
, that is, we switch the final states of
\mathscr{S}
.
Clearly, since
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
have the same set of initial states and the same transition relation,
we see that
\mathscr{Q}
is also a non-blocking and deterministic VPA with
n+1
states and no
\varepsilon
-moves.

Pick any
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{Q})
, and let
n=|\sigma|
.
Since
\mathscr{Q}
has no
\varepsilon
-moves, we must have
(q_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
for some
q_{0}\in S_{in}
,
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
, and some
r\in S-F
.
For the sake of contradiction, assume that
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{S})
.
Then, because
\mathscr{S}
has no
\varepsilon
-moves, we must have
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
for some
s_{0}\in S_{in}
,
\beta\in\Gamma^{\star}
, and some
p\in F
.
Since
\mathscr{S}
is deterministic we have
|S_{in}|=1
, so that
q_{0}=s_{0}
.
Since
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
have exactly the same set of transitions, we can now write

(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
Together with
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
and Proposition
2.7
, we conclude that
p=r
, which is a contradiction since
r\in S-F
and
p\in F
.
Thus,
L(\mathscr{Q})\subseteq\overline{L(\mathscr{S})}
.

Now let
\sigma\not\in L(\mathscr{S})
.
Since
\mathscr{S}
is non-blocking, we have
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
for some
p\in S
and
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
.
Because
\sigma\not\in L(\mathscr{S})
we need
p\in S-F
.
Since
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
have the same set of transitions, we can write
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{n}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(p,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
Thus,
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{Q})
because
p\in S-F
.
Hence,
\overline{L(\mathscr{S})}\subseteq L(\mathscr{Q})
, and the proof is complete. ∎

A.7
Proof of Proposition
2.18

Proof.

We have
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\rho,F\rangle
and
B\subseteq A
.
From Proposition
2.15
we can assume that
\mathscr{S}
has
n+1
states, is non-blocking and has no
\varepsilon
-moves.
Define
Q=S\cup\hat{S}
, where
\hat{S}=\{\hat{s}\,|\,s\in S\}
is a set of new, distinct states.
These new states are the only final states of
\mathscr{Q}
, that is, we let
\mathscr{Q}=\langle Q,S_{in},A,\Gamma,\mu,\hat{S}\,\rangle
.
We start with
\mu=\rho
and apply the following steps:

1.

Replace some original transitions:
For all
s\in F
and all
b\in B
:
if
(s,b,Z,t)\in\rho
, then add
(s,b,Z,\hat{t}\,)
to
\mu
, and remove
(s,b,Z,t)
from
\mu
;

2.

Create new transitions:
For all
(t,a,Z,r)\in\rho
, if

(a)

a\in B
and
t\in F
: we add
(\hat{t},a,Z,\hat{r})
to
\mu
;

(b)

a\not\in B
or
t\not\in F
: we add
(\hat{t},a,Z,r)
to
\mu
.

It is clear that
\mathscr{Q}
has
2n+2
states.
Since no
\varepsilon
-moves are added to
\mu
, we see that
\mathscr{Q}
has no
\varepsilon
-moves.

In order to show that
L(\mathscr{Q})=L(\mathscr{S})B
we need to relate the computations of
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{Q}
.
To ease the notation we let
\tilde{r}
stand for either
r
or
\hat{r}
, that is,
\tilde{r}\in\{r,\hat{r}\}
.

Claim 1:

If
(s,\sigma,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
, then for all
\tilde{s}\in\{s,\hat{s}\}
we have some
\tilde{t}\in\{t,\hat{t}\}
such that
(\tilde{s},\sigma,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(\tilde{t},\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
.

Proof.
We induct on
|\sigma|=n\geq 0
.
When
n=0
, since
\mathscr{S}
has no
\varepsilon
-moves, we get
(s,\sigma,\alpha\bot)=(t,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
and the result follows easily.

Now, let
\sigma=\delta a
where
a\in A
, and

(s,\delta a,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(r,a,\gamma\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
,
where
(r,a,Z,t)\in\rho
is the transition used in the last step.
The induction gives
\tilde{r}\in\{r,\hat{r}\}
and
(\tilde{s},\delta,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(\tilde{r},\varepsilon,\gamma\bot)
.

Assume that
\tilde{r}=r
. Since
(r,a,Z,t)\in\rho
, either
(r,a,Z,t)
remains in
\mu
, or step (1) of the construction gives
(r,a,Z,\hat{t}\,)\in\mu
.
In any case, there is some
\tilde{t}\in\{t,\hat{t}\,\}
such that
(r,a,Z,\tilde{t})
is in
\mu
.
This gives
(r,a,\gamma\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(\tilde{t},\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
.
Composing, we get

(\tilde{s},\delta a,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(r,a,\gamma\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(\tilde{t},\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
.
Now assume
\tilde{r}=\hat{r}
.
Since
(r,a,Z,t)\in\rho
, step (2) of the construction implies
(\hat{r},a,Z,\tilde{t}\,)\in\mu
, for some
\tilde{t}\in\{t,\hat{t}\,\}
.
Repeating the argument we get again
(\tilde{s},\sigma,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(\tilde{t},\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
, proving the claim.

Now let
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{S})
and
b\in B
.
Then, we get
s\in F
and
s_{0}\in S_{in}
such that
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(s,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
Using Claim 1 we get
\tilde{s}\in\{s,\hat{s}\}
such that
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(\tilde{s},\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
.
Since
\mathscr{S}
is non-blocking, we get
t\in S
and
\beta\in\Gamma^{\star}
such that

(s,b,\alpha\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
.
Let
(s,b,Z,t)\in\rho
be the transition used in this step.
First, assume that
\tilde{s}=s
.
Since
s\in F
and
b\in B
, step (1) of the construction gives
(s,b,Z,\hat{t}\,)\in\mu
.
Thus,
(\tilde{s},b,\alpha\bot)=(s,b,\alpha\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(\hat{t},\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
.
Alternatively, if
\tilde{s}=\hat{s}
, from step (2) and with
s\in F
and
b\in B
we get
(\hat{s},b,Z,\hat{t}\,)\in\mu
.
Thus,
(\tilde{s},b,\alpha\bot)=(\hat{s},b,\alpha\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(\hat{t},\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
.
So, in any case, we can compose and obtain

(s_{0},\sigma b,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(\tilde{s},b,\alpha\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(\hat{t},\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
.
Since
\hat{t}
is final in
\mathscr{Q}
we see that
\sigma b\in L(\mathscr{Q})
.
Thus,
L(\mathscr{S})B\subseteq L(\mathscr{Q})
.

For the other direction, we need to relate computations in
\mathscr{Q}
to computations in
\mathscr{S}
.

Claim 2:

If
(s,\sigma,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(\tilde{t},\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
where
\tilde{t}\in\{t,\hat{t}\,\}
, then either:

1.

\tilde{t}=t
and
(s,\sigma,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)

2.

\tilde{t}=\hat{t}
,
\sigma=\delta b
with
b\in B
, and
(s,\delta b,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(r,b,\gamma\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
with
r\in F
,
\gamma\in\Gamma^{\star}
.

Proof.

When
\sigma=\varepsilon
, since
\mathscr{Q}
has no
\varepsilon
-moves, we get
s=t
and
\alpha=\beta
.
The result follows easily.

Now let
\sigma=\delta a
with
a\in A
.
Since
\mathscr{Q}
has no
\varepsilon
-moves, there are states
\tilde{r}
and
\tilde{t}
such that

(s,\delta a,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(\tilde{r},a,\gamma\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(\tilde{t},\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
,
where
\tilde{r}\in\{r,\hat{r}\}
and
\tilde{t}\in\{t,\hat{t}\,\}
.

Assume first that
\tilde{r}=r
.
Then, inductively, item (1) gives
(s,\delta a,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(r,a,\gamma\bot)
.
Let
(r,a,Z,\tilde{t})
be the transition in
\mu
used in the last step
(r,a,\gamma\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(\tilde{t},\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
.
If
\tilde{t}=t
it follows that
(r,a,Z,t)
is also in
\rho
, and then we also have
(r,a,\gamma\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
.
Composing we get
(s,\sigma,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
, and item (1) of the Claim holds.
If
\tilde{t}=\hat{t}
we get
(r,a,Z,\hat{t}\,)\in\mu
.
According to item (1) of the construction, we get
r\in F
,
a\in B
and
(r,a,Z,t)\in\rho
.
Again, we may write
(r,a,\gamma\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
.
Composing, we see that now item (2) of the Claim holds.

Alternatively, let
\tilde{r}=\hat{r}
.
Now, from
(s,\delta,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(\tilde{r},\varepsilon,\gamma\bot)=(\hat{r},\varepsilon,\gamma\bot)
, the induction and item (2) of the Claim give
\delta=\eta b
,
b\in B
and

(s,\eta b,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(q,b,\theta\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\gamma\bot)
for some
q\in F
and
\theta\in\Gamma^{\star}
.
Let
(\hat{r},a,Z,\tilde{t}\,)\in\mu
be the transition used in the last move of
\mathscr{Q}
, namely
(\tilde{r},a,\gamma\bot)=(\hat{r},a,\gamma\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(\tilde{t},\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
.
Item (2) of the construction gives
(r,a,Z,t)\in\rho
.
Then we may also write
(r,a,\gamma\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
.
If
\tilde{t}=t
, recalling that
\sigma=\delta a=\eta ba
, we compose to get

(s,\eta ba,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(q,ba,\theta\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(r,a,\gamma\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
,
thus showing that item (1) of the Claim holds.
Finally, when
\tilde{t}=\hat{t}
we get
(\hat{r},a,Z,\hat{t}\,)\in\mu
and item (2a) of the construction gives
a\in B
and
r\in F
.
Putting it together, we have
\sigma=\eta ba
,
a\in B
,
r\in F
and now

(s,\eta ba,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(r,a,\gamma\bot)\overset{1}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(t,\varepsilon,\beta\bot)
.
We see that item (2) of the Claim holds, completing the proof.

Now assume that
\sigma\in L(Q)
.
Since
S_{in}
is the set of initial states and
\hat{S}
is the set of final states of
\mathscr{Q}
,
we must have
(s_{0},\sigma,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\mapsto}}(\hat{t}\,,\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
for some
s_{0}\in S_{in}
,
\hat{t}\,\in\hat{S}
, and
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
.
From Claim 2, item 2, we get
\sigma=\delta b
,
b\in B
and
(s_{0},\delta,\alpha\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\mapsto}}(r,\varepsilon,\gamma\bot)
with
r\in F
.
Clearly,
\delta\in L(\mathscr{S})
, so that
\sigma\in L(\mathscr{S})B
.
We now have
L(\mathscr{Q})\subseteq L(\mathscr{S})B
.

Since we already had
L(\mathscr{S})B\subseteq L(\mathscr{Q})
, we conclude that
L(\mathscr{Q})=L(\mathscr{S})B
, as expected.
∎

A.8
Proof of Proposition
3.11

Proof.

We have
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
.
We first construct a context-free grammar (CFG)
[
13
,
9
]

G
whose leftmost
derivations will simulate traces of
\mathscr{S}
, and vice-versa.

The terminals of
G
are the transitions in
T
.
The non-terminals are of the form
[s,Z,p]
where
s,p\in S
are states of
\mathscr{S}
and
Z\in\Gamma_{\bot}
is a stack symbol.
If state
p
is not important, we may write
[s,Z,-]
.
For the main idea, let
t_{i}=[s_{i},a_{i},Z_{i},p_{i}]
,
1\leq i\leq n
be transitions of
\mathscr{S}
and let
\sigma=a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}
.
Then, if in
G
we have a leftmost derivation

[s_{0},\bot,-]\overset{\star}{\underset{}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}\cdots t_{n}[r_{1},W_{1},r_{2}][r_{2},W_{2},r_{3}]\cdots[r_{m},W_{m},r_{m+1}][r_{m+1},\bot,-]

it must be the case that
\mathscr{S}
starting at the initial configuration
(s_{0},\bot)
can move along the transitions
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
, in that order, to reach the configuration
(r_{1},W_{1}W_{2}\cdots W_{m}\bot)
,
that is,
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(r_{1},W_{1}W_{2}\cdots W_{m}\bot)
and vice-versa.
We are also guessing that, when
\mathscr{S}
removes some
W_{i}
from the stack — if it eventually does, — then it will enter state
r_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,m
.

Formally, let
G=(V,T,P,I)
where
V
is the set of non-terminals,
T
is the set of terminals
P
is the set of productions and
I
is the initial non-terminal of
G
.
The set of terminals is the same set
T
of transition of
\mathscr{S}
.
The sets
V
and
P
are constructed as follows, and where
NV
is an auxiliary set.
Start with
V=\{I\}
,

NV=\{[s_{0},\bot,-]:\,\,\text{for all }s_{0}\in S_{in}\}
, and

P=\{I\overset{}{\underset{}{\leadsto}}[s_{0},\bot,-]:\,\,[s_{0},\bot,-]\in NV\}
.
Next, we apply the following simple algorithm:

While
NV\neq\emptyset
:

1.

Let
[s,Z,p]\in NV
. Remove
[s,Z,p]
from
NV
and add it to
V
.

2.

For all
t\in T
:

(a)

If
t=(s,a,W,q)\in T_{c}
, add

[s,Z,p]\overset{}{\underset{}{\leadsto}}t[q,W,r][r,Z,p]
to
P
, for all
r\in S
.
If
[q,W,r]\notin V\cup NV
, add
[q,W,r]
to
NV
, and if
[r,Z,p]\notin V\cup NV
add
[r,Z,p]
to
NV
;

(b)

If
t=(s,a,\sharp,q)\in T_{i}
, add
[s,Z,p]\overset{}{\underset{}{\leadsto}}t[q,Z,p]
to
P
.
If
[q,Z,p]\notin V\cup NV
, add
[q,Z,p]
to
NV
;

(c)

If
t=(s,a,Z,q)\in T_{r}
, then

i.

if
Z\neq\bot
and
p=q
add
[s,Z,p]\overset{}{\underset{}{\leadsto}}t
to
P
; and

ii.

if
Z=\bot
and
p=-
, add
[s,Z,p]\overset{}{\underset{}{\leadsto}}t[q,\bot,-]
to
P
, and if

[q,\bot,-]\notin V\cup NV
add
[q,\bot,-]
to
NV

We indicate by
\hookrightarrow
the leftmost derivation relation induced by
G

over
(V\cup T)^{\star}
.

The next claim says that leftmost derivations of
G
faithfully simulate traces of
\mathscr{S}
.

Claim 1:

Let
t_{i}=(p_{i},a_{i},Z_{i},q_{i})
,
1\leq i\leq n
,
n\geq 0
,
and
\sigma=a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}
.
Assume that

[s,\bot,-]\overset{n}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}t_{2}\cdots t_{n}[u_{0},W_{1},u_{1}][u_{1},W_{2},u_{2}]\cdots[u_{m-1},W_{m},u_{m}].

(13)

Then we must have:

\displaystyle m\geq 1,u_{m}=-,W_{m}=\bot,\,\text{and}\,\,W_{i}\neq\bot,1\leq i<m

(14)

\displaystyle(s,\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(u_{0},W_{1}\cdots W_{m})

(15)

\displaystyle\text{either (i) $n=0$ with $s=u_{0}$; or (ii) $n\geq 1$ with $s=p_{1}$, $q_{n}=u_{0}$}.

(16)

Proof:

If
n=0
then
\sigma=\varepsilon
,
[s,\bot,-]=[u_{0},W_{1},u_{1}]
and
m=1
.
Hence,
W_{m}=W_{1}=\bot
,
u_{m}=u_{1}=-
and
s=u_{0}
.
Since we can write
(s,\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(u_{0},W_{1})
,
the result follows.

Proceeding inductively, fix some
n\geq 1
and assume the assertive is true for
n-1
.
Now suppose that Eq. (
13
) holds.
Since derivations in
G
are leftmost, and each production in
G
has exactly one terminal symbol as the leftmost symbol in the right-hand side, using the induction hypothesis we can write

\displaystyle[s,\bot,-]

\displaystyle\overset{n-1}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}\cdots t_{n-1}[u_{0},W_{1},u_{1}][u_{1},W_{2},u_{2}]\cdots[u_{m-1},W_{m},u_{m}]

\displaystyle\overset{1}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}\cdots t_{n-1}t_{n}\beta[u_{1},W_{2},u_{2}]\cdots[u_{m-1},W_{m},u_{m}],

(17)

where
[u_{0},W_{1},u_{1}]\overset{}{\underset{}{\leadsto}}t_{n}\beta
was the production used in the last step.
We also get

\displaystyle m\geq 1,u_{m}=-,W_{m}=\bot,\,\text{and}\,\,W_{i}\neq\bot,1\leq i<m

(18)

\displaystyle(s,\bot)\overset{\sigma_{1}}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(u_{0},W_{1}\cdots W_{m}),\,\,\sigma_{1}=a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n-1}

(19)

\displaystyle\text{either (i) $n-1=0$, $s=u_{0}$; or (ii) $n-1\geq 1$ with $s=p_{1}$, $q_{n-1}=u_{0}$}.

(20)

By the construction of
G
there are three cases.

As a first alternative, assume
a_{n}\in L_{c}
, so that
t_{n}=[p_{n},a_{n},Z_{n},q_{n}]\in T_{c}
.
Then by item (2a) of the construction of
G
we must have

[u_{0},W_{1},u_{1}]\overset{}{\underset{}{\leadsto}}t_{n}[q_{n},Z_{n},r][r,W_{1},u_{1}]
, where
r\in S
, and
p_{n}=u_{0}
.
Thus,
\beta=[q_{n},Z_{n},r][r,W_{1},u_{1}]
.
Together with Eq. (
17
) we get

\displaystyle[s,\bot,-]

\displaystyle\overset{n}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}\cdots t_{n-1}t_{n}[q_{n},Z_{n},r][r,W_{1},u_{1}][u_{1},W_{2},u_{2}]\cdots[u_{m-1},W_{m},u_{m}].

(21)

Now define
v_{0}=q_{n}
,
X_{1}=Z_{n}
,
v_{1}=r
.
Also, let
v_{i+1}=u_{i}
and
X_{i+1}=W_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq m
.
We get

[s,\bot,-]\overset{n}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}\cdots t_{n}[v_{0},X_{1},v_{1}][v_{1},X_{2},v_{2}]\cdots[v_{m},X_{m+1},v_{m+1}].

Clearly, using condition (
18
) we get
m+1\geq 1
,
X_{m+1}=W_{m}=\bot
and
v_{m+1}=u_{m}=-
.
Also
X_{1}=Z_{n}
and since
t_{n}\in T_{c}
we get
Z_{n}\neq\bot
, so that
X_{1}\neq\bot
.
Using condition (
18
) we get
X_{i+1}=W_{i}\neq\bot
,
1\leq i\leq m
, and we conclude that condition (
14
) holds.
Next, we examine condition (
16
).
We already have
n\geq 1
,
p_{n}=u_{0}
and
v_{0}=q_{n}
.
If condition (
20
.i) holds, then
n=1
and
s=u_{0}
, so that
s=p_{n}=p_{1}
, and condition (
16
) holds.
On the other hand, if condition (
20
.ii) holds, we immediately get
s=p_{1}
and, because
v_{0}=q_{n}
, we conclude
condition (
16
) holds again.
Finally, since
t_{n}=[p_{n},a_{n},Z_{n},q_{n}]\in T_{c}
and
p_{n}=u_{0}
, together with condition (
19
), and because
\sigma=\sigma_{1}a_{n}
, we can write

\displaystyle(s,\bot)

\displaystyle\overset{\sigma_{1}}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(u_{0},W_{1}\cdots W_{m})=(p_{n},W_{1}\cdots W_{m})

\displaystyle\overset{a_{n}}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(q_{n},Z_{n}W_{1}\cdots W_{m})=(v_{0},X_{1}\cdots X_{m+1}).

Hence, condition (
15
) is verified, and we conclude that the claim holds in this case.

For the second alternative, let
a_{n}\in L_{i}\cup\{\varsigma\}
, so that
t_{n}=[p_{n},a_{n},Z_{n},q_{n}]\in T_{i}
.
The reasoning is entirely similar to the preceding case.

As a final alternative, let
a_{n}\in L_{r}
, so that
t_{n}=[p_{n},a_{n},Z_{n},q_{n}]\in T_{r}
.
Looking at item (2c) of the construction of
G
, and recalling that
[u_{0},W_{1},u_{1}]\overset{}{\underset{}{\leadsto}}t_{n}\beta
is the production used to get Eq. (
17
), we need
u_{0}=p_{n}
and
Z_{n}=W_{1}
.

The first sub-case is when we followed step (2c.i).
We must then have
W_{1}\neq\bot
,
u_{1}=q_{n}
and
\beta=\varepsilon
.
From Eq. (
17
) we get

[s,\bot,-]\overset{n}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}\cdots t_{n-1}t_{n}[u_{1},W_{2},u_{2}]\cdots[u_{m-1},W_{m},u_{m}]
.
Since
W_{m}=\bot
and
W_{1}\neq\bot
we must have
m\geq 2
.
Define

v_{i-1}=u_{i}
,
X_{i}=W_{i+1}
,
1\leq i\leq m-1
.
Now we have

\displaystyle[s,\bot,-]\overset{n}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}\cdots t_{n}[v_{0},X_{1},v_{1}][v_{1},X_{2},v_{2}]\cdots[v_{m-2},X_{m-1},v_{m-1}].

(22)

Note that
m\geq 2
implies
m-1\geq 1
, and
v_{m-1}=u_{m}=-
and
X_{m-1}=W_{m}=\bot
.
Together with condition (
18
) we see that

X_{i}=W_{i+1}\neq\bot
,
1\leq i\leq m-2
, so that condition (
14
) holds.
Since
m\geq 2
,
u_{0}=p_{n}
,
W_{1}=Z_{n}
,
u_{1}=q_{n}
, and
[p_{n},a_{n},Z_{n},q_{n}]\in T_{r}
we get

(u_{0},W_{1}W_{2}\cdots W_{m})\overset{a_{n}}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(u_{1},W_{2}\cdots W_{m})=(v_{0},X_{1}X_{2}\cdots X_{m-1}).

Because
\sigma=\sigma_{1}a_{n}
, we can compose with Eq. (
19
) and get

(s,\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(u_{1},W_{2}\cdots W_{m})=(v_{0},X_{1}X_{2}\cdots X_{m-1}),

so that the condition (
15
) is satisfied.
We already have
v_{0}=u_{1}
and
u_{1}=q_{n}
, so that
v_{0}=q_{n}
.
It is clear that
n\geq 1
, so in order to verify condition (
16
) we need
s=p_{1}
.
If
n=1
, condition (
20
) gives
s=u_{0}
, and since we already have
u_{0}=p_{n}=p_{1}
, we get
s=p_{1}
.
If
n\geq 2
we have
n-1\geq 1
and condition (
20
) immediately gives
s=p_{1}
,
concluding this sub-case.

For the last sub-case, assume we followed step (2c.ii).
In this case we get
u_{0}=p_{n}
,
W_{1}=Z_{n}=\bot
,
u_{1}=-
, and
\beta=[q_{n},\bot,-]
.
Since
W_{1}=\bot
, condition (
18
) says that
m=1
.
We see that condition (
14
) is immediately satisfied.
From Eq. (
17
) we get

[s,\bot,-]\overset{n}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}\cdots t_{n-1}t_{n}[q_{n},\bot,-]
.
Let
v_{0}=q_{n}
,
v_{1}=-
, and
X_{1}=\bot
.
Thus,

[s,\bot,-]\overset{n}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}\cdots t_{n}[v_{0},X_{1},v_{1}]
.
Since
m=1
, condition (
19
) reduces to

(s,\bot)\overset{\sigma_{1}}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(u_{0},W_{1})
.
Since
u_{0}=p_{n}
,
W_{1}=Z_{n}=\bot
, and
[p_{n},a_{n},Z_{n},q_{n}]\in T_{r}
we get

(s,\bot)\overset{\sigma_{1}}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(u_{0},W_{1})=(p_{n},\bot)\overset{a_{n}}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(q_{n},\bot)=(v_{0},\bot),

and we see that condition (
15
) holds.
As a final step, we verify that condition (
16
) also holds.
Clearly,
n\geq 1
, and we already have
v_{0}=q_{n}
.
Again, if
n=1
, condition (
20
) gives
s=u_{0}
, and since we already have
u_{0}=p_{n}=p_{1}
, we get
s=p_{1}
.
If
n\geq 2
we have
n-1\geq 1
and condition (
20
) immediately gives
s=p_{1}
,
concluding the argument for this sub-case.
This completes the argument for the last alternative.

Since we examined all three alternatives in case (2c), we conclude that the claim holds.
\Box

For the converse, we show that any trace of
\mathscr{S}
can be simulated by a leftmost derivation of
G
.

Claim 2:

Let
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(u_{0},W_{1}\cdots W_{m}\bot)
with
s_{0}\in S_{in}
,
m\geq 0
,

\sigma=a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}\in L_{\varsigma}^{\star}
, and
n\geq 0
.
Assume that the transitions used in this trace were, in order,
t_{i}=(p_{i},a_{i},Z_{i},q_{i})\in T
,
1\leq i\leq n
.
Then for all
u_{i}\in S
,
1\leq i\leq m
, we have

\displaystyle[s_{0},\bot,-]\overset{n}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}t_{2}\cdots t_{n}[u_{0},W_{1},u_{1}][u_{1},W_{2},u_{2}]\cdots[u_{m-1},W_{m},u_{m}][u_{m},\bot,-]

(23)

If
n\geq 1
then
s_{0}=p_{1}
,
u_{0}=q_{n}
.

(24)

Proof:

Assume first that
n=0
, so that
\sigma=\varepsilon
.
Then
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(u_{0},W_{1}\cdots W_{m}\bot)
implies
s_{0}=u_{0}
,
m=0
.
Since we can write
s_{0}\in S_{in}
, we get that
[s_{0},\bot,-]
is a non-terminal of
G
.
Also,
Since
[s_{0},\bot,-]\overset{0}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}[u_{0},\bot,-]
, condition (
23
) holds with
m=0
.
Condition (
24
) holds vacuously.

Now assume
n\geq 1
.
Since
t_{n}
was the transition used in the last step, we can write

\displaystyle(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma_{1}}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(p_{n},X_{1}\cdots X_{k}\bot)\overset{a_{n}}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(q_{n},\beta\bot),

(25)

where
\sigma_{1}=a_{1}\cdots a_{n-1}
,
k\geq 0
, and
u_{0}=q_{n}
.
From the induction hypothesis, condition (
23
), for all
u_{i}\in S
,
1\leq i\leq k
, we get

\displaystyle[s_{0},\bot,-]\overset{n-1}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}\cdots t_{n-1}[p_{n},X_{1},u_{1}]\cdots[u_{k-1},X_{k},u_{k}][u_{k},\bot,-].

(26)

If
n=1
, so that
n-1=0
, Eq. (
26
) says that

s_{0}=p_{n}=p_{1}
.
If
n-1>0
, the induction hypothesis in Eq. (
26
)
says that
s_{0}=p_{1}
.
In any case,
s_{0}=p_{1}
.
Since we already have
u_{0}=q_{n}
, we conclude that condition (
24
) always holds.

Next, we argue that either
k>0
and
[p_{n},X_{1},u_{1}]
was added to the set
NV
, or
k=0
and
[p_{n},\bot,-]
was added to
NV
during the construction of
G
.
If
n-1=0
then
[p_{n},X_{1},u_{1}]=[s_{0},\bot,-]
.
Since
s_{0}\in S_{in}
we know that
[p_{n},\bot,-]
was added to the initial
NV
set.
If
n-1>1
then
[p_{n},X_{1},u_{1}]
was on the right-hand side of a production of
G
and so it was also added to
NV
during the construction of
G
.
In any case, we can assume that
[p_{n},X_{1},u_{1}]
was added to the set
NV
.

Following step (2) in the construction of
G
, we break the argument in there cases:

a_{n}\in L_{c}
:

in this case we have
t_{n}=(p_{n},a_{n},Z_{n},q_{n})\in T_{c}
.
Pick any
v\in S
.
Following item (2a) in the construction of
G
, we must have

[p_{n},X_{1},u_{1}]\overset{}{\underset{}{\leadsto}}t_{n}[q_{n},Z_{n},v][v,X_{1},u_{1}]
as a production of
G
.
Together with Eq. (
26
) we can now write

\displaystyle[s_{0},\bot,-]

\displaystyle\overset{n}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}\cdots t_{n-1}t_{n}[q_{n},Z_{n},v][v,X_{1},u_{1}]\cdots[u_{k-1},X_{k},u_{k}][u_{k},\bot,-],

for all
v\in S
, and all
u_{i}\in S

i=1,\cdots,k
.
Because
t_{n}
was the last transition used in Eq. (
25
), we must have
\beta=Z_{n}X_{1}\cdots X_{k}
.
Hence, we can use Eq. (
25
) and write

(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(q_{n},Z_{n}X_{1}\cdots X_{k}\bot)
, where
|\sigma|=n
.
We conclude that condition (
23
) holds.

a_{n}\in L_{i}\cup\{\varsigma\}
:

we have
t_{n}=(p_{n},a_{n},Z_{n},q_{n})\in T_{i}
and we can reason as in the preceding case.

a_{n}\in L_{r}
:

now we have
t_{n}=(p_{n},a_{n},Z_{n},q_{n})\in T_{r}
.

First assume that
k=0
in Eq. (
25
).
Since
t_{n}
was the last transition used in that equation, we need

Z_{n}=\bot
and
\beta=\varepsilon
.
We now have
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(q_{n},\bot)
.
We already have
u_{0}=q_{n}
and, with
k=0
, Eq. (
26
) reduces to

[s_{0},\bot,-]\overset{n-1}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}\cdots t_{n-1}[p_{n},\bot,-]
.
We now have
[p_{n},\bot,-]
as a non-terminal of
G
, and
t_{n}=(p_{n},a_{n},\bot,q_{n})\in T_{r}
.
By item (2c.ii) of the construction of
G
, it follows that

[p_{n},\bot,-]\overset{}{\underset{}{\leadsto}}t_{n}[q_{n},\bot,-]
is a production of
G
.
Composing, we get
[s_{0},\bot,-]\overset{n}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}\cdots t_{n}[q_{n},\bot,-]
, and condition (
23
) holds with
m=0
.

Lastly, assume
k\geq 1
in Eq. (
25
).
From Definition
3.2
, it is clear that
X_{1}\neq\bot
.
Again, because
t_{n}
was the last transition used in Eq. (
25
), we must have
Z_{n}=X_{1}\neq\bot
and
\beta=X_{2}\cdots X_{k}
, so that we now have
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(q_{n},X_{2}\cdots X_{k}\bot)
.
Recall that
[p_{n},X_{1},u_{1}]
is a non-terminal of
G
, and Eq. (
26
) holds for all
u_{i}\in S
,
1\leq i\leq k
.
Choose
u_{1}=q_{n}
, so that we have
[p_{n},X_{1},u_{1}]
as a non-terminal of
G
, and
t_{n}=(p_{n},a_{n},X_{1},u_{1})
.
Now, using item (2c.i) of the construction of
G
we see that

[p_{n},X_{1},u_{1}]\overset{}{\underset{}{\leadsto}}t_{n}
is a production of
G
.
Composing with Eq. (
26
) we obtain

[s_{0},\bot,-]\overset{n}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}\cdots t_{n}[q_{n},X_{2},u_{2}]\cdots[u_{k-1},X_{k},u_{k}][u_{k},\bot,-],

and condition (
23
) holds with

m=k-1\geq 0
, concluding this case.

Now we can extract a contracted VPTS
\mathscr{Q}=\langle Q,Q_{in},A,\Gamma,R\rangle
from the original VPTS
\mathscr{S}
.
First, we determine the set
LN
of all non-terminals of
G
that
appear in the leftmost position in a derivation of
G
, that is,

\displaystyle LN=\big\{[s,Z,p]\in NV\,|\,I\overset{\star}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}t_{2}\cdots t_{n}[s,Z,p]\alpha,\alpha\in V^{\star}\big\}.

(27)

This can be accomplished by a backward search on the productions of
G
to find all non-terminals
[s,Z,p]
of
G
that generate at least one string
of terminals, that is,
[s,Z,p]\overset{\star}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}t_{2}\cdots t_{n}
for some
t_{i}\in R
,
1\leq i\leq n
.
Next, a forward search on the productions of
G
collects all non-terminals in
LN
.

In a second step, we collect the transitions in
R
as follows.

Start with
R=\emptyset
.

Next, for all
[s,Z,p]\in LN
and all

t=(s,a,W,q)\in T
, add
t
to
R
if:

3.

a\in L_{c}\cup L_{i}\cup\{\varsigma\}
, or

4.

a\in L_{r}
and either (i)
Z=W\neq\bot
and
p=q
; or (ii)
Z=W=\bot
and
p=-
.

In the resulting directed graph formed by all productions in
R
, remove any state that is not reachable from an initial state in
S_{in}
, and name
Q
the set of remaining states.
Finally, let

Q_{in}=S_{in}
.

We can now show that
\mathscr{Q}
is contracted and equivalent to
\mathscr{S}
, as needed.

Claim 3:

\mathscr{Q}
is a contracted VPTS.

Proof:

Let
t=(s,a,W,q)\in R_{r}
, so that
a\in\ L_{r}
.
By step (4) above, we need
[s,W,p]
leftmost in
G
, and either
(i)
W\neq\bot
and
p=q
, or (ii)
W=\bot
and
p=-
.

Since
[s,W,p]
is leftmost in
G
, by the form of the productions in
G
, we need
t_{i}=(p_{i},a_{i},Z_{i},q_{i})
,
1\leq i\leq n
,
n\geq 0
,
[u_{j-1},W_{j},u_{j}]
,
1\leq j\leq m
,
m\geq 1
, such that

[s_{0},\bot,-]\overset{n}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}t_{2}\cdots t_{n}[u_{0},W_{1},u_{1}][u_{1},W_{2},u_{2}]\cdots[u_{m-1},W_{m},u_{m}],

with
s_{0}\in Q_{in}
and
[s,W,p]=[u_{0},W_{1},u_{1}]
.
Hence,
s=u_{0}
and
W_{1}=W
.

Using Claim 1 we can write
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(u_{0},W_{1}W_{2}\cdots W_{m})=(s,WW_{2}\cdots W_{m})
, and where
\sigma=a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}
.
Let
\mu=h_{\varsigma}(\sigma)
.
We now have
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\Rightarrow}}(s,WW_{2}\cdots W_{m})
.
If
m>1
, and remembering that
t=(s,a,W,q)
, we see that condition (i) of Definition
3.10
is immediately satisfied.
When
m=1
, condition
14
in Claim 1 says that
W_{m}=\bot
, and we now have

W_{m}=W_{1}=W=\bot
and
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\Rightarrow}}(s,\bot)
.
Since
t=(s,a,\bot,q)
, we see that condition (ii) of Definition
3.10
can also be satisfied.

Claim 4:

tr(\mathscr{Q})=tr(\mathscr{S})
and
otr(\mathscr{Q})=otr(\mathscr{S})
.

Proof:

We trivially obtain that
tr(\mathscr{Q})\subseteq tr(\mathscr{S})
since
R\subseteq T
,
Q_{in}=S_{in}
, and
Q\subseteq S
.

Now assume
\sigma\in tr(\mathscr{S})
with
\sigma=a_{1}\cdots a_{n}
,
n\geq 0
.
Then we have

(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{S}}{\rightarrow}}(f,W_{1}\cdots W_{m}\bot)

(28)

with
m\geq 0
, and
s_{0}\in S_{in}
.
Let
t_{i}=(p_{i},a_{i},Z_{i},q_{i})\in T
,
1\leq i\leq n
, be the transitions used in this trace of
\mathscr{S}
, and in this order.
Using Claim 2, we get
r_{i}\in S
,
1\leq i\leq m
,
such that

[s_{0},\bot,-]\overset{n}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}t_{2}\cdots t_{n}[f,W_{1},r_{1}][r_{1},W_{2},r_{2}]\cdots[r_{m-1},W_{m},r_{m}][r_{m},\bot,-].

(29)

We want to show that
t_{k}
is also a transition of
\mathscr{Q}
,
1\leq k\leq n
.
That is, we want to show that
t_{k}
was added to
R
according to rules (3) and (4) above.
Fix some
k
,
1\leq k\leq n
, with
t_{k}=(p_{k},a_{k},Z_{k},q_{k})
.
Since the derivation in Eq. (
29
) is leftmost, and
G
has exactly one terminal in the right-hand side of any production, we must have

[s_{0},\bot,-]\overset{k-1}{\underset{G}{\hookrightarrow}}t_{1}\cdots t_{k-1}[u_{1},X_{1},u_{2}][u_{2},X_{2},u_{3}]\cdots[u_{\ell},X_{\ell},-],

and the next production used in Eq. (
29
) was
[u_{1},X_{1},u_{2}]\overset{}{\underset{}{\leadsto}}t_{k}\beta
, for some
\beta\in V^{\star}
.
From the construction of
G
we always have
u_{1}=p_{k}
, so that
[u_{1},X_{1},u_{2}]=[p_{k},X_{1},u_{2}]
is leftmost in
G
.
From Eq. (
27
) we get
[p_{k},X_{1},u_{2}]\in LN
.
We now look at rules (3) and (4).
If
a_{k}\in L_{c}\cup L_{i}\cup\{\varsigma\}
, rule (3) gives
t_{k}\in R
.
Now
a_{k}\in L_{r}
and recall that
[u_{1},X_{1},u_{2}]\overset{}{\underset{}{\leadsto}}t_{k}\beta
is a production of
G
.
By rule (2c) we have either:

(i)

u_{2}=q_{k}
,
X_{1}=Z_{k}\neq\bot
using rule (2c.i).
Then,
[u_{1},X_{1},u_{2}]=[p_{k},Z_{k},q_{k}]\in LN
.
Since
t_{k}=(p_{k},a_{k},Z_{k},q_{k})
and
a_{k}\in L_{r}
, rule (4.i) says that
t_{k}\in R
.

(ii)

u_{2}=-
,
X_{1}=Z_{k}=\bot
using rule (2c.ii).
Now
[u_{1},X_{1},u_{2}]=[p_{k},Z_{k},-]\in LN
.
Again,
t_{k}=(p_{k},a_{k},Z_{k},q_{k})
and
a_{k}\in L_{r}
give
t_{k}\in R
using rule (4.ii).

Now, we have
t_{k}\in R
for all
1\leq k\leq n
,
s_{0}\in S_{in}
and
Q_{in}=S_{in}
.
Then, as in Eq. (
28
) we can now write

(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{Q}}{\rightarrow}}(f,W_{1}\cdots W_{m}\bot)
.
Thus
\sigma\in tr(\mathscr{Q})
, and we now have
tr(\mathscr{S})\subseteq tr(\mathscr{Q})
.

Now, if
tr(\mathscr{S})=tr(\mathscr{Q})
then
otr(\mathscr{S})=otr(\mathscr{Q})
follows easily from
Definitions
3.5
and
3.6
.

Finally, assume that
\mathscr{S}
is deterministic.
Since
\mathscr{Q}
is constructed by removing transitions from
\mathscr{S}
, it is clear from Definition
3.8
that
\mathscr{Q}
is also deterministic.
This completes the proof.
∎

A.9
Proof of Lemma
5.9

Proof.

We will construct a fault model
\mathscr{T}
.
According to Definition
5.8
, in order for
\mathscr{T}
to be

ioco-like
complete for
\mathscr{B}
we need that, for all implementations
\mathscr{I}
, it holds that
\mathscr{I}
passes
\mathscr{T}
if and only if
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{B}
.
From Corollary
5.4
we know that
\mathscr{I}\text{\,\,\bf ioco-like}\,\,\mathscr{B}
if and only if

otr(\mathscr{I})\cap T=\emptyset
, where
T=\overline{otr}(\mathscr{B})\cap[otr(\mathscr{B})L_{U}]
.
That is, we need
\mathscr{T}
such that, for all implementations
\mathscr{I}
we have
\mathscr{I}
passes
\mathscr{T}
if and only if
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap T=\emptyset
.

Let
\mathscr{B}=\langle S_{\mathscr{B}},S_{in},L_{I},L_{U},\Delta_{\mathscr{B}},T_{\mathscr{B}}\rangle
be the given deterministic specification,
L=L_{I}\cup L_{U}
and
n=|S_{\mathscr{B}}|
.
Using Proposition
3.9
we know that
\mathscr{B}
is a deterministic VPTS with no
\varsigma
-moves
3
3

3

To keep the notation unclutered, we will refer to an IOVPTS and to its associated VPTS by the same symbol.
.
The desired fault model
\mathscr{T}=\langle S_{\mathscr{T}},T_{in},L_{U},L_{I},\Delta_{\mathscr{T}},T_{\mathscr{T}}\rangle
is constructed as follows.
Let
T_{in}=S_{in}
,
\Delta_{\mathscr{T}}=\Delta_{\mathscr{B}}
, and extend the state set
S_{\mathscr{B}}
and the transition set
T_{\mathscr{B}}
as follows.
Define
S_{\mathscr{T}}=S_{\mathscr{B}}\cup\{\text{fail}\}
where
\text{fail}\not\in S_{\mathscr{B}}
.
Fix some symbol
Z\in\Delta_{\mathscr{B}}
, and let

\displaystyle T_{\mathscr{T}}=

\displaystyle T_{\mathscr{B}}

\displaystyle\cup\big\{(s,\ell,Z,\text{fail})\,|\,\ell\in L_{U}\cap L_{c}\text{ and }(s,\ell,W,p)\not\in T_{\mathscr{B}}\text{ for any $p\in S_{\mathscr{B}}$, any $W\in\Delta_{\mathscr{B}}$}\big\}

(30)

\displaystyle\cup\big\{(s,\ell,W,\text{fail})\,|\,\ell\in L_{U}\cap L_{r}\,\,\text{and $(s,\ell,W,p)\not\in T_{\mathscr{B}}$ for any $p\in S_{\mathscr{B}}$}\big\}

(31)

\displaystyle\cup\big\{(s,\ell,\sharp,\text{fail})\,|\,\ell\in L_{U}\cap L_{i}\text{ and }(s,\ell,\sharp,p)\not\in T_{\mathscr{B}}\text{ for any }p\in S_{\mathscr{B}}\big\}

(32)

It is clear that
\mathscr{T}
has a single
fail
state, and has
n+1
states.

Claim 1.

\mathscr{T}
is deterministic.

Proof.

By construction
\mathscr{T}_{in}=S_{in}
.
Since
\mathscr{B}
is deterministic we get
|S_{in}|\leq 1
.
Thus,
\mathscr{T}
has at most one initial state.

For the sake of contradiction, assume that
\mathscr{T}
does not satisfy Definition
3.8
.
Then, we must have
\sigma\in L^{\star}
,
p,q\in S_{\mathscr{T}}
and
\alpha,\beta\in\Delta_{\mathscr{T}}
such that

\displaystyle(t_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\rightarrow}}(p,\alpha\bot)\quad\text{and}\quad(t_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\rightarrow}}(q,\beta\bot)

(33)

with
p\neq q
or
\alpha\neq\beta
, and
t_{0}
initial in
\mathscr{T}
.
If all transitions used in the runs in Eq.
33
are in
T_{\mathscr{B}}
we get an immediate contradiction to the determinism of
\mathscr{B}
.

Assume now that not all transitions used in Eq.
33
are in
T_{\mathscr{B}}
.
Since
fail
is a sink state, we must have either
p=\text{fail}
or
q=\text{fail}
.
Assume first
p=\text{fail}
and
q\neq\text{fail}
.
Then,
\sigma=\sigma_{1}x
, with
x\in L
and write the runs in Eq.
33
as

\displaystyle(t_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma_{1}}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\rightarrow}}(p_{1},\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{x}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\rightarrow}}(\text{fail},\alpha\bot)\quad\text{and}\quad(t_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma_{1}}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\rightarrow}}(q_{1},\beta_{1}\bot)\overset{x}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\rightarrow}}(q,\beta\bot)

(34)

where
p_{1}\neq\text{fail}
,
q_{1}\neq\text{fail}
.
Thus, all transitions over
\sigma_{1}
are in
\mathscr{B}
, and since
\mathscr{B}
is deterministic, we get
p_{1}=q_{1}
and
\alpha_{1}=\beta_{1}
.
Hence, we have transitions
(p_{1},x,X,\text{fail})
,
(p_{1},x,Y,q)
in
T_{\mathscr{T}}
.
Now, we follow the construction of
\mathscr{T}
and distinguish three cases.
When
x\in L_{U}\cap L_{c}
, condition (
30
) gives
X=Z
.
Since
q\neq\text{fail}
we have
(p_{1},x,Y,q)
in
T_{\mathscr{B}}
, contradicting
condition (
30
) of the construction.
When
x\in L_{U}\cap L_{i}
the reasoning is the same, now using
condition (
32
).
When
x\in L_{U}\cap L_{r}
, the first run in (
34
) gives
\alpha_{1}=X\alpha
, and the second run gives
\beta_{1}=Y\beta
.
Since
\alpha_{1}=\beta_{1}
we get
X=Y
.
Now we have
(p_{1},x,X,\text{fail})
,
(p_{1},x,X,q)
in
T_{\mathscr{T}}
.
Again,
q\neq\text{fail}
implies
(p_{1},x,X,q)
in
T_{\mathscr{B}}
, contradicting
condition (
31
).
The case
p\neq\text{fail}
,
q=\text{fail}
is similar and also leads to a contradiction.
This shows that we must have
p=\text{fail}=q
in (
33
).
So, we can write

\displaystyle(t_{0},\bot)

\displaystyle\overset{\sigma_{1}}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\rightarrow}}(p_{1},\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{x}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\rightarrow}}(\text{fail},\alpha\bot)

(35)

\displaystyle(t_{0},\bot)

\displaystyle\overset{\sigma_{1}}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\rightarrow}}(q_{1},\beta_{1}\bot)\overset{x}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\rightarrow}}(\text{fail},\beta\bot)

(36)

where
p_{1}\neq\text{fail}
,
q_{1}\neq\text{fail}
.
We recall that we need either
p\neq q
or
\alpha\neq\beta
in (
33
).
We are then left with
\alpha\neq\beta
in (
35
) and (
36
).

The determinism of
\mathscr{B}
forces
p_{1}=q_{1}
and
\alpha_{1}=\beta_{1}
because both runs on
\sigma_{1}
in (
35
) and (
36
) use only transitions in
T_{\mathscr{B}}
.
We have transitions
(p_{1},x,X,\text{fail})
in (
35
) and

(p_{1},x,Y,\text{fail})
in (
36
).
We now argue that we always reach
\alpha=\beta
, a contradiction.
If
x\in L_{U}\cap L_{c}
, condition (
30
) says that
X=Z=Y
, and then
\alpha=Z\alpha_{1}
and
\beta=Z\beta_{1}
, and we get
\alpha=\beta
.
If
x\in L_{U}\cap L_{i}
, condition (
32
) immediately gives
\alpha=\alpha_{1}=\beta_{1}=\beta
.
When
x\in L_{U}\cap L_{r}
we need
\alpha_{1}=X\alpha
and
\beta_{1}=Y\beta
, so that
\alpha=\beta
again.
We conclude that
\mathscr{T}
is, indeed,
deterministic.

The next claim shows that any
\sigma\in\overline{otr}(\mathscr{B})\cap\big[otr(\mathscr{B})L_{U}\big]
leads
\mathscr{T}
to the
fail
state.

Claim 2.

Let
\sigma\in\overline{otr}(\mathscr{B})\cap\big[otr(\mathscr{B})L_{U}\big]
.
Then,
(s_{0},\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\Rightarrow}}(\text{fail},\alpha\bot)
for some
s_{0}\in T_{in}
,
\alpha\in(\Delta_{\mathscr{T}})^{\star}
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma=\mu\ell
,
\sigma\not\in otr(\mathscr{B})
,
\ell\in L_{U}
and
\mu\in otr(\mathscr{B})
.
Because
otr(\mathscr{B})=tr(\mathscr{B})
, we get
(q_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\rightarrow}}(p,\alpha\bot)
, where
q_{0}\in S_{in}=T_{in}
and
\alpha\in(\Delta_{\mathscr{B}})^{\star}
.
By construction, all transitions in
\mathscr{B}
are also transitions of
\mathscr{T}
, so that
(q_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\rightarrow}}(p,\alpha\bot)
.
Now we argue that
(p,\alpha\bot)\overset{\ell}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\rightarrow}}(\text{fail},\beta\bot)
for some
\beta\in(\Delta_{\mathscr{T}})^{\star}
, so that composing we get
(q_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu\ell}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\rightarrow}}(\text{fail},\beta\bot)
.
We note that we cannot have
(p,\alpha\bot)\overset{\ell}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\rightarrow}}(z,\gamma\bot)
for any
z\in S_{\mathscr{B}}
,
\gamma\in(\Delta_{\mathscr{B}})^{\star}
, because then we would get

(q_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu\ell}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\rightarrow}}(z,\gamma\bot)
, and then
\sigma=\mu\ell\in tr(\mathscr{B})=otr(\mathscr{B})
, a contradiction.
There are three simple cases.
If
\ell\in L_{U}\cap L_{c}
, then
(p,\ell,W,z)\not\in T_{\mathscr{B}}
for any
z\in S_{B}
and any
W\in\Delta_{\mathscr{B}}
.
Then, Eq. (
30
) gives
(p,\ell,Z,\text{fail})\in T_{\mathscr{T}}
, as needed.
If
\ell\in L_{U}\cap L_{i}
, the reasoning is the same, using Eq. (
32
).
Now let
\ell\in L_{U}\cap L_{r}
.
Since
(p,\alpha\bot)\overset{\ell}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\rightarrow}}(z,\gamma\bot)
is not allowed,
we cannot have
(p,\ell,W,z)
in
T_{\mathscr{B}}
for any
z\in S_{\mathscr{B}}
, where
W\in\Delta_{\mathscr{B}}
is the first symbol in
\alpha\bot
.
Now, Eq. (
31
) gives
(p,\ell,W,\text{fail})\in T_{\mathscr{T}}
.
Since
(q_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu\ell}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\rightarrow}}(\text{fail},\beta\bot)
then, from Proposition
3.9
we also have
(q_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu\ell}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\Rightarrow}}(\text{fail},\beta\bot)
.

The next claim deals with the converse.

Claim 3.

Let
\mu\in L^{\star}
,
\ell\in L
and
(t_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\Rightarrow}}(p,\alpha\bot)\overset{\ell}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\Rightarrow}}(\text{fail},\beta\bot)
where
t_{0}\in T_{in}
,
p\in S_{\mathscr{T}}
,
\alpha
,
\beta\in(\Delta_{\mathscr{T}})^{\star}
.
Then we must have
\mu\ell\in\overline{otr}(\mathscr{B})\cap\big[otr(\mathscr{B})L_{U}\big]
.

Proof.

By construction
\mathscr{T}
has no
\varsigma
-moves, since
\mathscr{B}
has no
\varsigma
-moves.
Thus, we must have
(t_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\rightarrow}}(p,\alpha\bot)\overset{\ell}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\rightarrow}}(\text{fail},\beta\bot)
.

Since
fail
is a sink state we get
p\neq\text{fail}
, and we know that all transitions in
(t_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\rightarrow}}(p,\alpha\bot)
are in
\mathscr{B}
, so that

(t_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\rightarrow}}(p,\alpha\bot)
.
We must also have a transition
(p,\ell,X,\text{fail})
in
\mathscr{T}
.
By the construction, it can only be inserted in
T_{\mathscr{T}}
by force of Eqs. (
30
),(
31
), or (
31
).
In any case, we get
\ell\in L_{U}
.
Hence
\mu\ell\in otr(\mathscr{B})L_{U}
.
We now argue that
\mu\ell\not\in otr(\mathscr{B})
.
This will give
\mu\ell\in\overline{otr}(\mathscr{B})\cap otr(\mathscr{B})L_{U}
, completing the proof.

For the sake of contradiction, assume
\mu\ell\in otr(\mathscr{B})
, so that

(t_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\rightarrow}}({p^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\bot)}\overset{\ell}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\rightarrow}}({r,\gamma\bot)}
, with
t_{0}\in S_{in}
,
p^{\prime},r\in S_{\mathscr{B}}
and
\gamma\in(\Delta_{\mathscr{B}})^{\star}
.
Recall that
\mathscr{B}
is deterministic and has no
\varsigma
-move.
Hence we can write
(t_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\Rightarrow}}(p,\alpha\bot)
and
(t_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\Rightarrow}}({p^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\bot)}
.
Now from Definition
3.8
we get
p=p^{\prime}
and
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}
.
Also recall that
(p,\ell,X,\text{fail})
is a transition of
\mathscr{T}
.
Together with
(p,\alpha\bot)\overset{\ell}{\underset{\mathscr{B}}{\rightarrow}}(r,\gamma\bot)
,
there are three cases.

If
\ell\in L_{c}
then
(p,\ell,W,r)
is a transition of
\mathscr{B}
for some
W\in\Delta_{\mathscr{B}}
.
In this case we must have used Eq. (
30
) to insert
(p,\ell,X,\text{fail})
in
T_{\mathscr{T}}
.
But then we need
X=Z
and
(p,\ell,Y,q)\not\in T_{\mathscr{B}}
for any
q\in S_{\mathscr{B}}
,
Y\in\Delta_{\mathscr{B}}
, and we get a contradiction.
If
\ell\in L_{i}
then
W=\sharp
,
(p,\ell,\sharp,r)
is a transition of
\mathscr{B}
and we must have used Eq. (
32
) to insert
(p,\ell,\sharp,\text{fail})
in
T_{\mathscr{T}}
.
But this requires
(p,\ell,\sharp,q)\not\in T_{\mathscr{B}}
for any
q\in S_{\mathscr{B}}
, and we reach a contradiction again.
If
\ell\in L_{r}
then
(p,\ell,W,r)
is a transition of
\mathscr{B}
where
W
is the first symbol in
\alpha\bot
.
But, according to used Eq. (
31
) we now need

(p,\ell,W,q)\not\in T_{\mathscr{B}}
for any
q\in S_{\mathscr{B}}
.
This final contradiction completes the proof.

Let
\mathscr{I}=\langle S_{\mathscr{I}},I_{in},L_{I},L_{U},\Delta_{\mathscr{I}},T_{\mathscr{I}}\rangle
be an arbitrary IUT.
As argued above, we need
\mathscr{I}
does not pass
\mathscr{T}
if and only if
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap T\neq\emptyset
, where
T=\overline{otr}(\mathscr{B})\cap\big[otr(\mathscr{B})L_{U}\big]
.
According to Definition
5.8
,
\mathscr{I}
does not pass
\mathscr{T}
if and only if for some
\sigma\in L^{\star}
we get
((t_{0},q_{0}),\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{\mathscr{T}\times\mathscr{I}}{\Rightarrow}}((\text{fail},q),\alpha\bot)

where
((t_{0},q_{0}),\bot)
is an initial configuration of
\mathscr{T}\times\mathscr{I}
,

q\in S_{\mathscr{I}}
and
\alpha=(X_{1},Y_{1})\cdots(X_{n},Y_{n})\in(\Delta_{\mathscr{T}}\times\Delta_{\mathscr{I}})^{\star}
,
n\geq 0
.
From the construction, it is clear that
fail
is a sink state of
\mathscr{T}
, and transitions into
fail
in
\mathscr{T}

are over symbols in
L_{U}
.
Hence, we can say that
\mathscr{I}
does not pass
\mathscr{T}
if and only if

((t_{0},q_{0}),\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{\mathscr{T}\times\mathscr{I}}{\Rightarrow}}((p,r),\beta\bot)\overset{\ell}{\underset{\mathscr{T}\times\mathscr{I}}{\Rightarrow}}((\text{fail},q),\alpha\bot),

for some
\mu\in L^{\star}
,
\ell\in L_{U}
,
p\in S_{\mathscr{T}}
,
p\neq\text{fail}
,
r\in S_{\mathscr{I}}
,
\beta=(W_{1},Z_{1})\cdots(W_{m},Z_{m})\in(\Delta_{\mathscr{T}}\times\Delta_{\mathscr{I}})^{\star}
,
m\geq 0
.

From Definition
5.7
, we know that
\mathscr{T}\times\mathscr{I}
is the IOVPTS associated to
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{T}}\times\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{I}}
, where
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{T}}
and
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{I}}
are the VPAs associated with
\mathscr{T}
and
\mathscr{I}
, respectively.
Using Proposition
3.13
we can say that

\mathscr{I}
does not pass
\mathscr{T}
if and only if

((t_{0},q_{0}),\mu\ell,\bot)\overset{i}{\underset{\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{T}}\times\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{I}}}{\mapsto}}((p,r),\ell,\beta\bot)\overset{k}{\underset{\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{T}}\times\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{I}}}{\mapsto}}((\text{fail},q),\varepsilon,\alpha\bot),

where
i\geq|\mu|
,
k\geq 1
.
Using Proposition
2.11
we get
\mathscr{I}
does not pass
\mathscr{T}
if and only if

\displaystyle(t_{0},\mu\ell\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{T}}}{\mapsto}}(p,\ell,W_{1}\cdots W_{m}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{T}}}{\mapsto}}(\text{fail},\varepsilon,X_{1}\cdots X_{n}\bot)

(37)

\displaystyle(q_{0},\mu\ell\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{I}}}{\mapsto}}(r,\ell,Z_{1}\cdots Z_{m}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{I}}}{\mapsto}}(q,\varepsilon,Y_{1}\cdots Y_{n}\bot).

(38)

Assume that
\mathscr{I}
does not pass
\mathscr{T}
. Then, from Eq. (
38
)
and using Proposition
3.14
we obtain
\mu\ell\in L(\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{I}})=otr(\mathscr{I})
.
From Eq. (
37
) and Proposition
3.13

we get

(t_{0},\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\Rightarrow}}(p,W_{1}\cdots W_{m}\bot)\overset{\ell}{\underset{\mathscr{T}}{\Rightarrow}}(\text{fail},X_{1}\cdots X_{n}\bot).

Using Claim 3 and recalling that
p\neq\text{fail}
, we get
\mu\ell\in T=\overline{otr}(\mathscr{B})\cap\big[otr(\mathscr{B})L_{U}\big]
.
Thus
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap T\neq\emptyset
.

Now assume
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap T\neq\emptyset
, so that we have
\mu\ell\in otr(\mathscr{I})
,

\mu\ell\in otr(\mathscr{B})L_{U}
and
\mu\ell\not\in otr(\mathscr{B})
, where
\mu\in L^{\star}
and
\ell\in L_{U}
.
From Proposition
3.13
we get
(q_{0},\mu,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{I}}}{\mapsto}}(r,\ell,Z_{1}\cdots Z_{k}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{I}}}{\mapsto}}(q,\varepsilon,Y_{1}\cdots Y_{n}\bot)
, where
q_{0}\in I_{in}
,
r,q\in S_{\mathscr{I}}
,
Z_{1}\cdots Z_{k},Y_{1}\cdots Y_{n}\in(\Delta_{\mathscr{I}})^{\star}
, and
k,n\geq 0
.
Using Claim 2 and Proposition
3.13
we also get a run over
\mu\ell
in
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{T}}
as
(t_{0},\mu,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{T}}}{\mapsto}}(p,\ell,W_{1}\cdots W_{j}\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{T}}}{\mapsto}}(\text{fail},\varepsilon,X_{1}\cdots X_{m}\bot)
, with
t_{0}\in T_{in}
,
p\in S_{\mathscr{T}}
,
W_{1}\cdots W_{j},X_{1}\cdots X_{m}\in(\Delta_{\mathscr{T}})^{\star}
,
j,m\geq 0
.
Use Proposition
2.12
to conclude that
k=j
and
n=m
.
We now have

(q_{0},\mu\ell,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{I}}}{\mapsto}}(q,\varepsilon,Y_{1}\cdots Y_{n}\bot),\quad(t_{0},\mu\ell,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{T}}}{\mapsto}}(\text{fail},\varepsilon,X_{1}\cdots X_{n}\bot).

From Proposition
2.11
it follows that

((t_{0},q_{0}),\mu\ell,\bot)\overset{\star}{\underset{\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{T}}\times\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{I}}}{\mapsto}}((\text{fail},q),\varepsilon,\alpha\bot)
, and using Proposition
3.13
we now have

((t_{0},q_{0}),\bot)\overset{\mu\ell}{\underset{\mathscr{T}\times\mathscr{I}}{\Rightarrow}}((\text{fail},q),\alpha\bot)
.
This shows that
\mathscr{I}
does not pass
\mathscr{T}

Now we have that
\mathscr{I}
does not pass
\mathscr{T}
if and only if
otr(\mathscr{I})\cap T\neq\emptyset
, as needed.
∎

A.10
Proof of Theorem
5.11

Proof.

A VTPS
\mathscr{P}=\langle Q,Q_{in},L,\Gamma,\rho\rangle
with no transitions of the form
(p,a,\bot,q)
in
\rho
, and two states
s_{i}
,
s_{e}
with
s_{i}\neq s_{e}
, are input to Algorithm
1
.

At lines 1 and 2 we start with
V=null
and
R[p,q]=0
for all pairs
(p,q)
.
Inspecting lines 6, 10, 14, 16 and 19, we see that a pair
(p,q)
is added to
V
only when we currently have
R[p,q]=0
and, upon entering
V
, we immediately set
R[p,q]\neq 0
.
Further, at no other point in the code we reset
R[p,q]
to zero again.
Hence, a pair
(p,q)
can enter
V
at most once and, therefore, the main loop at line 11 must terminate.
So, Algorithm
1
always stops.

Next we claim that, at any point during the execution of the algorithm, if
R[p,q]\neq 0
then it codes for a string that induces a balanced run from
p
to
q
.
This is immediate from the initialization lines 6 and 10.
Proceeding inductively, assume that this property holds after a number of executions of the main loop.
At line 14, we have removed
(p,q)
from
V
, and so we now have
R[p,q]\neq 0
because
(p,q)
entered
V
in a previous iteration.
At that moment we made
R[p,q]\neq 0
and then, inductively, it codes for a string
\sigma
such that
(p,\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,\bot)
.
Now, at line 14 we require
R[s,p]\neq 0
so that, inductively, it also codes for a string
\mu
such that
(s,\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(p,\bot)
.
Composing, we get
(s,\bot)\overset{\mu\sigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,\bot)
, and so, we extended the induction in this case when we now make
R[s,q]\neq 0
coding for the string
\mu\sigma
.
The reasoning at line 16, is very similar.
We now look at line 19.
At that point we have a push transition
(s,a,Z,p)
, a pop transition

(q,b,Z,t)
, and
(p,\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,\bot)
for some
\sigma\in L_{\varsigma}^{\star}
.
Recall that the algorithm assumes that the given VPTS
\mathscr{P}
has no pop transitions on the empty stack.
With this hypothesis, we claim the following general property of
\mathscr{P}
:

If
(p,\alpha_{1}\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,\alpha_{2}\bot)
for some
p
and
q\in Q
and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\Gamma^{\star}
, then for all
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\Gamma^{\star}
we also have
(p,\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,\alpha_{2}\beta_{2}\bot)
.

A simple proof can be obtained by induction on
|\sigma|\geq 0
.

With
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=\varepsilon
,
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}=Z
, from
(p,\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,\bot)
we get
(p,Z\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,Z\bot)
.
Now we have

(s,\bot)\overset{a}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(p,Z\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,Z\bot)\overset{b}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(t,\bot)
, so that making
R[s,t]
code for the string
a\sigma b
also extends the induction after line 19 is passed.
Since we have completed one more iteration of the main loop, we see that upon termination of the main loop, if we have
R[s_{i},s_{e}]\neq 0
, then we do have a string that induces a balanced run from
s_{i}
to
s_{e}
.
Moreover, it easy to see that the simple recursive call
getstring(
s_{i},s_{e}
)
at line 21 does correctly extract one such string.

Next, we argue in the other direction.
Suppose that the main loop terminates with
V=null
. Then we claim that for all pairs
(p,q)
, with
p\neq q
, if
R[p,q]=0
then there is no string capable of inducing a balanced run from
p
to
q
.
For the sake of contradiction, assume that the main loop terminates with
V=null
, and we have
p\neq q
,
R[p,q]=0
, and a string
\sigma
such that
(p,\bot,)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,\bot)
.
Among all such pairs, choose one for which
|\sigma|
is minimum.
Since
p\neq q
, we need
|\sigma|\geq 1
.
If
|\sigma|=1
, then we need a transition
(p,\sigma,\sharp,q)
in
\rho
.
But then, at line 6, we make
R[p,q]=[p,\sigma,q]
and it is never rest to
0
again.
This is a contradiction, and we can assume
|\sigma|\geq 2
.

Now there are two cases, depending on the internal part of the run
(p,\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,\bot)
.

A configuration
(r,\bot)
occurs in the run
(p,\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,\bot)
.

Write

(p,\bot)\overset{\sigma_{1}}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(r,\bot)\overset{\sigma_{2}}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,\bot)
, with
\sigma=\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}
.
We know that
\sigma_{1}\neq\varepsilon\neq\sigma_{2}
because
\mathscr{P}
has no transitions on the empty stack.
If
p=r
we get
(p,\bot)\overset{\sigma_{2}}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,\bot)
.
Since
|\sigma_{2}|<|\sigma|
, the minimality of
|\sigma|
forces
R[p,q]\neq 0
, a contradiction.
Similarly,
q=r
also leads to a contradiction.

Now, assume
p\neq r\neq q
.
Since
|\sigma_{1}|<|\sigma|
and
|\sigma_{2}|<|\sigma|
, when the main loop terminates with
V=null
we must have
R[p,r]\neq 0
and
R[r,q]\neq 0
.
Moreover, for this to happen, both
(p,r)
and
(r,q)
were added to
V
.
Suppose that
(p,r)
is removed from
V
before
(r,q)
.
Then, at the iteration of the main loop when
(r,q)
is removed from
V
we have
R[p,r]\neq 0
and
p\neq q
.
Hence, at line 14, since
R[p,q]=0
we make
R[p,q]=[p,r,q]
and, since it is never reset to
0
again, we have a contradiction.
If
(r,q)
is removed first from
V
, the reasoning is the same using line 16.
So, this case can not happen.

A configuration
(r,\bot)
does not occur in the run
(p,\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,\bot)
.

Then, we must have a push transition
(p,x,Z,s)
with
\sigma=x\sigma_{1}
, and
we are left with
(p,\bot)\overset{x}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(s,Z\bot)\overset{\sigma_{1}}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,\bot)
.
If
|\sigma_{1}|=1
, we need a pop transition
(s,y,Z,q)
and now line 10 makes
R[p,q]=[x,q,q,y]
, a contradiction.
Hence
|\sigma_{1}|\geq 2
.
Since no configuration of the form
(u,\bot)
occurs in the run over
\sigma
, we must have a pop transition
(t,y,Z,q)
and

(p,\bot)\overset{x}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(s,Z\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(t,Z\bot)\overset{y}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,\bot)
,
with
\sigma=x\mu y
.

Next we claim that in any VPTS
\mathscr{S}=\langle S,S_{in},L,\Gamma,T\rangle
if a run does not shorten initial stack, then that string can be substituted for any other.
More precisely,

Let
p
,
q\in S
,
\sigma\in L_{\varsigma}^{\star}
and
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\star}
with
(p,\alpha\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,\alpha\bot)
.
Assume that a configuration
(u,\gamma)
, with
|\gamma|<|\alpha|
, does not occur in that run over
\sigma
.
Then, for any
\beta\in\Gamma^{\star}
we also have
(p,\beta\bot)\overset{\sigma}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(q,\beta\bot)
.

An easy induction over
|\sigma|\geq 0
gives the result.

Recall that we already have
(s,Z\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(t,Z\bot)
and a configuration
(u,\bot)
dos not occur on the run over
\mu
since it can not occur on a run over
\sigma
.
Using the claim we get
(s,\bot)\overset{\mu}{\underset{}{\rightarrow}}(t,\bot)
.
Now, since
|\mu|<|\sigma|
, the minimality of
|\sigma|
says that when the main loop terminates with
V=null
, we must have
R[s,t]\neq 0
.
But then, at some moment
(s,t)
was added to
V
.
Since the main loop terminates with
V=null
, at some iteration we have removed
(s,t)
from
V
.
Note that we have a push transition
(p,x,Z,s)
and a pop transition

(t,y,Z,q)
.
Hence, line 19 says that we will set
R[p,q]=[x,s,t,y]
and, since
R[p,q]
is never reset, we see that the main loop terminates with
R[p,q]\neq 0
, which is a contradiction.

Since both cases lead to contradictions, we conclude that when the main loop terminates with
V=null
and
R[s_{i},s_{e}]=0
, then there is no string capable of inducing a balanced run from
s_{i}
to
s_{e}
.
Hence, line 20 correctly reports the inexistence of any such strings.

Thus, in any case, lines 20–21 always report as expected, and Algorithm
1
is correct.
∎
